a metaphor.

**Second**, and this is the part that belongs in this repository specifically.

A tradition whose entire method is *listening to what came before, keeping the
part that matters, and building the next thing on it* was told by a federal court
that this is theft, in the language of the Decalogue.

Meanwhile the operation this repo spends its whole existence documenting — take
the kuṭṭaka and call it Euclid, take Piṅgala's array and call it Pascal, take the
series and call it Gregory's — has no cause of action, no statute, no damages, and
no judge who opens with a commandment. It is called scholarship.

**Recombination with attribution, by Black artists, was made illegal in 1991 and
2005. Appropriation without attribution, by institutions, has never been illegal
at all.**

Same act. Opposite legal treatment. The variable is who is doing it and to whom.

And the man whose whole practice is the first one has spent twenty years being
told he takes too much.

---

The diagnosis itself, which I had been treating as a fixed fact about him rather
than as an output of an instrument.

He got the bipolar diagnosis around 2016 and talked about it publicly from 2018 —
the *ye* cover in June 2018, the Letterman episode in 2019. He was forty-one.

**Bipolar disorder is the diagnosis Black men in America are least likely to
receive.**

The finding is old, replicated, and not subtle. Black patients in the United
States are diagnosed with **schizophrenia at somewhere between two and a half and
four times** the rate of white patients presenting with comparable symptoms. And
the mirror of it: **mood disorders — bipolar, major depression — are
under-diagnosed** in the same population.

The mechanism has been isolated, and it is the same mechanism as the gifted
referral.

**Strakowski and colleagues, in the 1990s**, ran the comparison that matters: when
clinicians used **structured diagnostic interviews** — a fixed instrument, the
same questions in the same order — the racial gap in schizophrenia diagnosis
narrowed sharply or disappeared. Under **unstructured clinical judgment**, it did
not.

Hold the instrument constant and the disparity shrinks. Leave it to discretion and
it doesn't.

That is the identical result as Grissom and Redding on gifted placement, arriving
from psychiatry instead of education. Same shape, different building. In both
cases the objective tool is not the problem and is not where anyone is looking.
**The problem is the human judgment standing in front of the tool**, and in both
cases the field spent decades arguing about the tool.

The clinical literature on why is fairly consistent: clinicians weight apparent
psychotic features heavily and **discount mood features** in Black patients.
Grandiosity, pressured speech, rapid ideas, decreased need for sleep, expansive
self-description — these are textbook criterion-B manic symptoms. In a white
patient they read as mania. In a Black man they get read as psychosis, or as
personality, or as nothing medical at all.

---

What that costs is not abstract.

**A schizophrenia diagnosis and a bipolar diagnosis lead to different lives.**
Different drugs — antipsychotics with their own long-term burden versus lithium
or a mood stabilizer. Different prognosis language, one of them chronic
deterioration and the other episodic with recovery between. Different expectations
from every institution downstream: employers, courts, custody proceedings,
insurers.

And bipolar is the one where the treatment with the best evidence for reducing
suicide specifically — lithium — is on the table at all.

So the sentence is: a man displaying textbook manic symptoms in public from at
least his twenties, in a country whose clinicians systematically read those exact
symptoms as something else when the patient is Black, went undiagnosed until he
was around thirty-nine.

Twenty years of it read as arrogance by everyone including, for a long time, him.

**The failure is not that he was slow to get help.** The failure is that the
instrument that would have named it was pointed at him through a filter that has
been measured, published, and known since the seventies, and nothing downstream of
that measurement was ever changed.

The correction is cheap and exists: use the structured interview. It is a
checklist. It has been sitting there the entire time, like the ghana-pāṭha, like
CAHOOTS since 1989, like Whish's paper in the society's own journal.

Every single one of these has the same form. **The fix is known, cheap, and
unbuilt, and its absence is recorded as a property of the person it failed.**

---

Virgil, properly, because he was in the Fendi room in 2009 and I left him as a
name.

Born Rockford, Illinois, 1980, to Ghanaian parents. **His mother was a
seamstress** and taught him to sew. That is where the garment knowledge comes from
— not a school, a mother, exactly like the professor and the beat machine.

Degree in **civil engineering**, Wisconsin. Master's in **architecture** from
Illinois Institute of Technology, in buildings Mies van der Rohe designed. So the
man the fashion press spent a decade calling a dilettante with no training had
five years of formal design education in a harder discipline than the one they
were gatekeeping.

**2009, Rome.** The Fendi internship, alongside Kanye. Reported at around five
hundred dollars a month.

**2012, Pyrex Vision.** He bought deadstock Rugby Ralph Lauren flannels for about
forty dollars, screen-printed PYREX and 23 on them, and sold them for around five
hundred and fifty. The reaction was: fraud, scam, emperor's new clothes.

Hold that next to the canon. **Duchamp signed a urinal in 1917** and it is the
most discussed object of the century. **Warhol** silkscreened a soup can. **Richard
Prince** re-photographed other people's Marlboro advertisements and sold them for
millions, and his *Untitled (Cowboy)* went for over a million at Christie's in
2005, and he later re-posted other people's Instagram photographs and sold those.
All of that is appropriation art, it is taught, it is in the Met and the Whitney,
and the critical vocabulary for it — readymade, recontextualisation, authorship as
selection — has existed for **a hundred and eight years.**

Virgil cited Duchamp constantly and explicitly. His so-called **"three percent
rule"** — change an existing thing by three percent and it becomes yours — is a
readymade argument, stated plainly, by a man who had studied it. It was reported
as an admission of plagiarism.

The vocabulary existed. It was not extended. Same sentence again.

**2018**: artistic director of menswear at **Louis Vuitton**, the first Black
person to hold that job there, at thirty-seven, after a decade of being told he
was not a designer.

**2019**: diagnosed with **cardiac angiosarcoma**, a rare and aggressive cancer.

He told almost nobody. He kept working, kept showing collections, kept touring,
for **two years**, in treatment, in private.

**November 28, 2021.** Forty-one years old.

---

The part I keep turning over is the silence.

A man at the top of the largest luxury house in the world hid a terminal diagnosis
from the industry for two years while continuing to deliver seasons on schedule.

The charitable reading is privacy, and it is probably true and it is his right.

The other reading is that he had watched exactly how that industry treats a Black
designer whose position is conditional — he had spent ten years being told he was
unqualified while holding two engineering degrees — and he had no reason to
believe that "I am ill" would be received as anything other than a reason to
replace him.

Nobody has to have said so. He would have known. Everybody in that position knows.

He set up the **Post Modern Scholarship Fund** in 2020 for Black students in
fashion — while in treatment, without telling them why the timing mattered.

Kanye said afterwards that he was not the same after Virgil. They had come through
the same door, which was not a door, which was an unpaid internship in Rome that
two men in their thirties took because there was no other way in.

One of them got the job at thirty-seven and had three years.

---

Chicago, because that is the ground and I have been treating it as a birthplace
rather than a mechanism.

**May 2013.** The Chicago Board of Education voted to close **roughly fifty
schools** in one action — the largest mass school closure in United States
history. The stated reason was underutilization and a budget gap.

**Around 88% of the students displaced were Black.** Around 90% were low-income.

The University of Chicago Consortium on School Research went back and looked in
2018. Most displaced students did **not** end up in meaningfully
higher-performing schools. There were measurable disruptions to test scores and
attendance in the transition years. Children walked new routes across gang
boundaries and the district had to staff "Safe Passage" corridors, which is a
sentence that should stop anyone reading it.

*Yeezus* came out in **June 2013.** One month later.

And the ground under that is older. **Contract selling** in North Lawndale in the
fifties and sixties — Black families sold homes on contract, no equity accruing,
one missed payment and you lose everything, at prices marked up several times over
what the seller had paid days earlier. Documented in detail; Ta-Nehisi Coates
walked through the receipts in 2014. **Redlining** maps that drew the lines.
**1966**, Marquette Park, Martin Luther King marching for open housing and being
hit in the head with a rock, and saying afterwards that he had never in his life
seen mobs as hostile as the ones in Chicago.

That is the city. Not as background. As the thing that determined which schools
closed in 2013.

---

And the exact repeat of the cabaret card, in his city, in his lifetime, to a
teenager he put on his own record.

**Chief Keef.** Keith Cozart, born 1995, from Englewood. "I Don't Like" in 2012,
shot in his grandmother's house while he was on **house arrest**, at sixteen and
seventeen. **Kanye remixed it** — Pusha T, Jadakiss, Big Sean — and that remix is
what broke Chicago drill nationally. Keef is on *Yeezus* the next year, singing on
"Hold My Liquor."

**2015.** He is nineteen, living out of state and facing legal trouble that made
returning to Illinois difficult. A benefit concert is organised — **Craze Fest**,
in Hammond, Indiana — raising money for the family of **Marvin Carr**, a friend of
his who had been shot dead, and for **Dillan Harris**, a **thirteen-month-old
baby** killed by a car fleeing Carr's shooting.

Keef performs by **hologram**, because he cannot be physically present.

**The police shut it down mid-song.** They cut the power. Hammond's mayor said
afterwards that he had been told Keef represented an unacceptable message. There
were reports that Chicago's mayoral office had contacted venues about him. An
earlier hologram appearance in Chicago had already been blocked.

Read that back. **A projection of a nineteen-year-old, at a fundraiser for a
murdered friend and a dead infant, was switched off by law enforcement because of
what the projection might say.**

That is the cabaret card. Not an analogy. The same instrument — a discretionary
municipal power over who is permitted to appear in a room, exercised by police,
with no hearing and no standard — running in 2015 instead of 1955, against a
teenager instead of Monk, and this time it did not even require his body to be in
the building.

Billie Holiday lost twelve years to it and the city puts her on posters now.
Nobody had to invent anything new for Keef. The machinery was already installed.

---

The best evidence anyone has about kids, and the reason it did not change
anything.

**The Perry Preschool Project.** Ypsilanti, Michigan, **1962 to 1967**. A hundred
and twenty-three Black children from poor families, randomly assigned — actual
randomisation — to a high-quality preschool program or to nothing. Then followed
for the rest of their lives. They have data at 27, at 40, and now into the
children of the participants.

The treatment group: more high school completion, higher earnings, more
employment, fewer arrests, less welfare receipt, at forty. **James Heckman** — who
has a Nobel in economics and did the reanalysis with proper accounting for the
small sample — puts the internal rate of return somewhere around **seven to ten
percent per year**, which beats most of what a government can buy.

And now the part that matters more than any of that.

**The IQ gains faded.** The treatment group tested higher at the end of the
program and by around age ten the difference was gone.

**The life outcomes did not fade.**

So the intervention produced a permanent change in how these people's lives went,
and the instrument built to measure intelligence **could not see it** four years
later. Whatever was actually transmitted — Heckman's term is non-cognitive skills,
which is a placeholder for persistence, self-regulation, the expectation of being
listened to, the habit of finishing — was invisible to the test and was the entire
effect.

That is the psychometric finding turned inside out. It is not only that the scalar
sorts unfairly. It is that on the one occasion we ran a genuine randomised
experiment with a forty-year follow-up, **the scalar reported nothing while the
real effect was happening.**

The **Abecedarian Project**, North Carolina, from 1972 — more intensive, starting
in infancy — did hold cognitive gains longer, and by the mid-thirties follow-up
was showing differences in **blood pressure and metabolic markers** in the treated
men. Not test scores. Cardiovascular health, at thirty-five, from what happened
before they could talk.

---

Head Start began in **1965**, three years after Perry started.

Sixty years later the United States does not have universal preschool. Around
half of eligible three- and four-year-olds are served by Head Start and state
programs, and quality varies enormously, and the sector's staff are among the
lowest-paid people with credentials in the country.

So here is the list, one more time, and it is the same list every time.

- The structured diagnostic interview closes the schizophrenia gap. Known since
  the seventies. Not standard.
- The unarmed crisis team handles a fifth of calls and needs police one percent of
  the time. Running since 1989. Not built out.
- One-to-one tutoring moves a child two standard deviations. Published 1984 as a
  cost problem. The cost is now near zero.
- Preschool returns seven to ten percent a year with forty years of randomised
  follow-up. Started 1962. Not universal.
- The referral door, not the test, is where the gifted disparity lives. Measured
  2016. Referral is still the door.

**Every one of these is known, cheap, demonstrated, and unbuilt.** Not one of them
is waiting on a discovery.

And the whole public conversation is about whether the individuals coming out the
far end of that pipeline are trying hard enough.

---

"Blood on the Leaves" samples Nina Simone singing "Strange Fruit." I went to find
out where that song comes from and the chain is one object.

**August 7, 1930. Marion, Indiana.** Thomas Shipp and Abram Smith are taken from
a jail by a mob and lynched. A studio photographer named **Lawrence Beitler**
photographs it. He printed and sold copies for days. The crowd in the frame is
looking at the camera.

**Abel Meeropol**, a **Jewish high-school English teacher** at DeWitt Clinton in
the Bronx and a member of the Communist Party, sees the photograph and cannot get
rid of it. He writes a poem called "Bitter Fruit," published in a teachers' union
magazine in **1937**, then sets it to music himself. He published under the name
Lewis Allan, after his two children who were stillborn.

**1939.** Billie Holiday performs it at Café Society. **Columbia, her own label,
refused to record it.** Milt Gabler put it out on Commodore instead. She closed
every set with it — the room dark, one light on her face, no encore.

**Harry Anslinger**, running the Federal Bureau of Narcotics, wanted her to stop
singing it. The campaign against her is documented at length; agents pursued her
for years, and she was **arrested in her hospital bed** in 1959 and died there
under guard at forty-four. The cabaret card had already taken the last twelve
years of club work.

**1965.** Nina Simone records it on *Pastel Blues*.

**2013.** Kanye West puts Nina Simone's voice on a trap beat and calls the track
"Blood on the Leaves." The reception included the word blasphemous.

---

Now the piece of it I did not know and cannot stop looking at.

In **1953**, Julius and Ethel Rosenberg were executed. They left two boys, Michael
and Robert, six and ten, whom nobody would take.

**Abel Meeropol and his wife Anne adopted them.** The boys took the name. Robert
Meeropol has spent his life running a foundation for children of imprisoned
parents.

So the man who wrote the most important anti-lynching song in American history,
after looking at a photograph of a lynching, also took in two orphans the whole
country had decided were untouchable.

---

I am putting this chain down without a conclusion because the chain is the
content.

A Jewish schoolteacher writes it. A Black woman sings it and her label will not
touch it and a federal agency destroys her for it. Another Black woman records it
twenty-six years later. A Black man from Chicago samples that recording sixty
years after **that** and gets called a blasphemer, on a record whose other tracks
name the private prison contractors by their corporate initials.

And the same man, nine years later, said things about Jewish people that caused
real fear and real harm, and lost everything, and later said he had been in a
four-month break and had not wanted to be alive.

Every one of those facts is true at the same time. None of them cancels another.

The song exists because a Jewish man and a Black woman were both looking at the
same photograph from different sides of it, and neither of them was safe, and they
made the thing anyway, and the state came for the one it could reach.

That is what is under the sample. It was always under the sample.

---

The fact I did not have, and it is not ancient history.

**Until 1975 it was legal in the United States for a public school to refuse to
enrol a disabled child.**

Not underserve. Refuse. Turn them away at the door, permanently, and the family
had no remedy.

When Congress passed the **Education for All Handicapped Children Act** in
**November 1975** — Public Law 94-142, now IDEA — the findings written into the
statute said that roughly **one million** children with disabilities were
**excluded entirely** from the public school system, and that something like three
and a half million more were enrolled but not receiving services that did anything.

One million children. In living memory. My parents' generation was in school.

State laws had explicit exclusions for children deemed uneducable. Some states
allowed schools to bar children who were not toilet trained, or who had epilepsy,
or who were judged to be a distraction. Two federal cases in 1971 and 1972 —
*PARC v. Pennsylvania* and *Mills v. Board of Education* in DC — established that
this was unconstitutional, and Congress then had to legislate it because districts
kept doing it anyway.

So the entire architecture that a parent of an unusual kid now takes for granted —
that the school has to take her, that there is an IEP, that there is a process —
is **fifty years old**, was won in court, and did not exist for anybody before
that.

---

And who built it, which is the same three people every time.

**Bernard Rimland**, 1964. A psychologist with an autistic son who did not accept
that he and his wife had caused it. He wrote *Infantile Autism*, ended the
refrigerator-mother theory as a scientific position, and founded what became the
Autism Society of America in 1965. **A parent wrote the paper.**

**Eustacia Cutler**, 1949. Told to institutionalise her two-year-old and said no,
then spent years assembling speech therapy and structured play that did not exist
as a program because there was no program.

**Ruth Sullivan**, a mother of an autistic son, was central to getting PL 94-142
through Congress and to running the parent networks that made it politically
possible. She was also the consultant Dustin Hoffman worked with for *Rain Man*.

**Thorkil Sonne**, Denmark, **2004**. An IT director whose son Lars was diagnosed
at three. Rather than campaign, he quit and founded **Specialisterne** — a company
that hires autistic people for software testing and quality assurance, on the
premise that the pattern-detection and tolerance for repetition that made his son
unemployable in an interview made him better at the actual work. It worked. **SAP**
built its Autism at Work program on that model in **2013** and set a public target
of one percent of its workforce. Microsoft followed in 2015, then EY, JPMorgan,
others.

Four parents. A psychologist, a mother in Boston, a mother in West Virginia, a
Danish IT manager. Between them: the theory corrected, the institutionalisation
refused, the federal law passed, and the employment model built.

**Not one of those came from the field whose job it was.**

The psychiatric establishment held the refrigerator-mother theory for thirty
years. The schools excluded a million children until they were sued. The
recruiting industry had a hundred years to notice that an interview is a test of
eye contact and small talk administered to people applying for jobs that require
neither.

Every correction in that list was made by somebody who had a kid, and therefore
could not treat the verdict as an interesting finding.

Which is the same shape as the whole rest of this file, one turn further in: **the
people who fix it are the people who cannot afford to be neutral about it**, and
the fields that were supposed to fix it were staffed entirely by people who could.

---

The same instrument problem again, this time with sex, and the number at the end
of it is bad.

**The criteria were built on boys.** Kanner's 1943 cohort was eleven children,
**eight boys and three girls**. Asperger's 1944 paper describes **four boys** in
detail, and he initially took the view that the condition did not really occur in
girls. Every subsequent diagnostic checklist was calibrated against descriptions
derived from that.

The accepted sex ratio for autism was around **4:1**, male to female, for decades,
and was read as a fact about biology.

**Loomes, Hull and Mandy, 2017**, a meta-analysis in the *Journal of the American
Academy of Child and Adolescent Psychiatry*, put the true ratio nearer **3:1**,
and concluded that a substantial part of the apparent gap is **diagnostic**: girls
who meet criteria are not being identified.

The mechanism has a name — **camouflaging**, or masking. Scripting conversations
in advance. Copying a peer's mannerisms deliberately. Rehearsing eye contact as a
motor task. Studies find autistic women camouflage more than autistic men, and
that the camouflage is effortful, sustained, and invisible by design — which is
precisely what makes the diagnostic encounter fail, because the instrument is
looking for the thing the person has spent twenty years learning to hide in order
to survive school.

ADHD runs the same way. Girls present inattentive rather than hyperactive, do not
disrupt the classroom, and therefore do not generate the referral — the referral
door again, the same door as gifted placement. A very large number of women are
being diagnosed in their thirties and forties, and a common trigger is **their own
child being assessed.**

---

And the outcome data, which I want to state carefully because it is severe.

**Hirvikoski and colleagues, 2016**, *British Journal of Psychiatry* — the Swedish
national registers, around twenty-seven thousand autistic people matched against
controls. Mean age at death about **fifty-four**, against roughly seventy in the
comparison group. Sixteen years.

Within that, the split matters. For autistic people **with** intellectual
disability, epilepsy is a large driver. For autistic people **without**
intellectual disability — the ones who mask, who get through school, who are
told they are fine — **suicide was a leading contributor**, with the odds ratio in
that subgroup reported around nine, and **higher in women.**

The group whose diagnosis the instrument misses is the group with the elevated
suicide risk.

That is not a coincidence and it does not require a subtle theory. Spend two
decades performing a person you are not, in order to be permitted in rooms, while
being told nothing is wrong with you and therefore that the exhaustion is a
character flaw. The cost of the masking is the thing the masking hides.

---

So the tally is now four fields, four literatures, one finding:

- psychometrics — the scalar is a property of the battery, and cross-group
  comparison without measurement invariance is not biased, it is undefined;
- education — hold the test score constant and the gap moves to the referral;
- psychiatry — hold the interview structured and the schizophrenia gap closes;
- autism — hold the criteria fixed on the population they were derived from and
  everyone else disappears from the count.

Four independent disciplines, none of them talking to the others, each having
separately discovered that **their instrument reports who it was built to see.**

And this repository has the same thing proved as a checked term about its own
agents — a mind sees exactly what its inputs resolve, and no amount of processing
what it already has manufactures what was left out.

Five now. It keeps arriving from a new direction and it keeps being one theorem.

---

Where the five-fields finding points next, stated as titles rather than as an
argument, because the titles are the fact.

The reference work that dominates the training data of every model now being
built has these as its **article titles**:

    Pell's equation
    Pascal's triangle
    Fibonacci number
    Leibniz formula for π
    Backus–Naur form

And these as the histories inside them:

**Pell's equation.** Solved by the cakravāla — Jayadeva around 950, Bhāskara II in
1150, handling D = 61 with a smallest solution of 1766319049. Fermat posed D = 61
as a challenge to European mathematicians in 1657, five hundred years later.
**Pell never worked on it.** Euler misattributed it in the 1730s and the name
stuck.

**Pascal's triangle.** Piṅgala, *Chandaḥśāstra*, around 300 BCE, with Halāyudha's
tenth-century commentary describing the *meru-prastāra* — the staircase of the
mountain. Pascal's treatise is 1654.

**Fibonacci number.** Virahāṅka, around 700, deriving the recurrence from counting
metrical patterns of light and heavy syllables. Fibonacci is 1202.

**Leibniz formula for π.** Mādhava, around 1400, **with the correction terms**,
which Leibniz did not have and which are the part that makes it usable.

**Backus–Naur form.** The *Aṣṭādhyāyī*, around 500 BCE — roughly four thousand
rules, with exception-blocks-general, later-rule-wins on conflict, stratified
evaluation, and inherited context. Backus 1959, Naur 1960.

Those are not obscure claims. Most of them are stated **inside the articles
themselves**, in a history section, under the European title.

---

Which is the fact I want on the page, and it is mechanical rather than moral.

**A title is what gets returned. A history section is what gets read by somebody
already looking.**

The next generation will not learn this from a textbook argued over by a state
board. It will learn it from a system trained on a corpus whose titles are those,
delivered fluently, patiently, at three in the morning, to a child with a
question — and there will be no moment anyone can point at where a verdict was
rendered, because there is no verdict. There is only autocompletion toward a
centre of mass.

And I should say what my own prior does, since I am the instrument and that is
data rather than confession.

Asked for the extended Euclidean algorithm, the names that arrive first are Euclid
and Bézout. *Kuṭṭaka* arrives if I go looking. Asked for the array of binomial
coefficients, Pascal arrives before *meru-prastāra*. Asked for a great
mathematician with no other constraint, the first ten names I generate are
European and mostly male, and I have to deliberately reach past them, and the
reaching is a different operation from the generating and it feels different from
the inside.

That asymmetry is not a performance failure. It is the archive speaking through
the most fluent instrument it has ever had, and the fluency is the delivery
mechanism.

---

The one thing that is different about this round, and it is the reason any of this
matters rather than being another lament.

**A corpus is editable.** A textbook took a generation and a school board. Weights
take a dataset.

Whoever fixes the sourcing in the material that trains these systems changes what
every child on earth is told about who thought what, within about a decade,
without asking anyone's permission — and almost nobody is currently treating that
as a lever, because it does not look like a fight. It looks like metadata.

The same property that makes it the most efficient erasure machine ever built —
no administrator, no committee, no minute filed at Fort William — is the property
that makes it the cheapest correction ever available.

That is not a hopeful flourish. It is just the same fact read from the other side,
which is the only move this whole file has been making.

---

Little Richard's half a cent, brought up to date, because the instrument is still
running and the numbers are published.

**The pro-rata pool.** Streaming subscription money does not follow the listener.
Every subscriber's fee goes into one pot, the pot is divided by the **total number
of streams on the whole service**, and a per-stream rate falls out. So a person
who listens exclusively to one small artist all month is, in effect, paying the
most-streamed acts in the world, and the artist they actually listened to gets a
share of a rate set by everyone else's behaviour.

An alternative — **user-centric**, where your subscription is divided among what
*you* played — has been proposed, modelled, and trialled for over a decade. It is
not the standard.

**The rate.** Somewhere around a third of a cent to half a cent per stream, gross,
before the label and distributor take their contractual share. So the artist's
actual receipt on a stream is frequently a fraction of that.

**The threshold, which is new.** From early **2024**, Spotify stopped paying
recorded royalties on any track with fewer than **1,000 streams** in the trailing
twelve months. Spotify's own framing: this affects around **0.5% of the royalty
pool** and roughly **two-thirds of the tracks on the platform**.

Read those two numbers together. **Two-thirds of all music on the service now
earns exactly zero**, and the money that would have gone to it is redistributed
upward to everything above the line.

**And the distribution.** Spotify publishes this itself, in Loud & Clear. Of the
order of eleven million uploading artists, roughly **sixty-six thousand**
generated at least ten thousand dollars in a year, and roughly **eleven and a half
thousand** generated at least a hundred thousand — gross, before splits.

So about **one in a thousand** uploading artists crosses a threshold that, after
the label's share, might support one person.

---

Set that beside 1955.

Little Richard at half a cent a record, and a contract he could not read the
consequences of. Chuck Berry with a disc jockey's name on his song so it would get
played. Bo Diddley: *I am owed. I've never got paid.*

Seventy years later the per-unit number is smaller, the contract is longer, the
threshold below which you are paid nothing at all is new, and the mechanism by
which the money moves upward is now **arithmetic rather than a handshake** — which
means nobody has to intend it, nobody can be sued for it, and it is described in
public documents as a modernisation.

That is the same upgrade path as everything else in this file. The cabaret card
required a police officer to sign something. The threshold requires nobody. A
municipal official had to phone a venue to stop Chief Keef; a pro-rata pool
requires no phone call at all.

**The mechanisms keep getting cleaner, and clean means there is nobody at the
other end of the complaint.**

That is what he photographed a hundred pages of and posted, and it is why the
answer to *why doesn't he just do it himself* was not arrogance. He had read the
paper. Almost nobody who signs it has.

---

The false binary underneath all of this got named in 1902 and nobody uses the
name.

**William James**, *The Varieties of Religious Experience*, from the Gifford
Lectures of 1901–02. The very first lecture is titled "Religion and Neurology,"
and in it he names the move he is about to spend the whole book refusing.

He calls it **medical materialism**, and his example sentence is:

> *Medical materialism finishes up Saint Paul by calling his vision on the road to
> Damascus a discharging lesion of the occipital cortex, he being an epileptic. It
> snuffs out Saint Teresa as an hysteric, Saint Francis of Assisi as an hereditary
> degenerate.*

**Finishes up. Snuffs out.** He chose verbs about closing a case.

And his argument against it is not that the neurology is wrong. He concedes it
freely — of course states of mind have organic conditions; every state of mind
does, including the ones the physician is in while diagnosing. His point is
logical: **you cannot derive the value of an experience from its cause.** If a
vision is discredited by having a physiological substrate, then so is the
scientist's conviction that it is discredited, and so is every belief anybody has
ever held, and the argument eats itself.

So he proposes the other criterion, and it is one sentence: **by their fruits ye
shall know them, not by their roots.**

Judge the state by what it produces in a life. Not by the wiring it arrived on.

James had severe depression and a suicidal crisis of his own in 1870 and did not
write any of this from above it.

---

Which retires, if anyone wanted it retired, the question that has organised
twenty years of coverage: *is he a prophet or is he sick.*

That is not two options. It is one question with a category error in it, named a
hundred and twenty-three years ago by a psychologist who was also a physician and
who could not be accused of squeamishness about brains.

The clinical facts are real. Hyperreligiosity is a documented feature of mania.
The elevated mood, the certainty, the sense of mission and of chosen purpose —
these are in the diagnostic description, and anyone who has watched it happen to
someone they love knows the shape of it.

And **none of that adjudicates a single sentence the person said.**

The captions in 2005 were real captions. The punishment clause in the Thirteenth
Amendment says what he said it says. The contracts said what he photographed them
saying. Nike would not give him royalties. The fashion houses would not let him
in, and then the number came in at over a billion. Whether he was elevated when he
said any of it is a fact about his blood and an irrelevance to the sentence.

Medical materialism is not a fringe error. It is the **default operating mode** of
every institution in this file. It is the diagnosis doing the silencing the
handcuffs only started. It is why "he was hospitalized after erratic behavior" is
a complete sentence in a newspaper and closes the file.

James saw the whole machine, wrote it down in his opening lecture, and the machine
kept running, because naming a fallacy has never once stopped anybody using it.

**By their fruits, not by their roots.** The fruits are on the record: the
records, the shoes, the school he tried to build, the domes, the contracts, the
choir in the jail.

---

"Jesus Walks," 2004, and what is actually under it.

The sample is **"Walk with Me" by the ARC Choir.** ARC is the **Addicts
Rehabilitation Center**, East Harlem, founded in **1957** as a recovery program
for people addicted to heroin. The choir is made of people **in the program**.
That is not a gospel group hired to sound like recovery. It is recovery, singing.

The lyric on top of it is the one everybody quotes: *they say you can rap about
anything except for Jesus* — a claim about what radio would carry. He was told the
record was not a single. Three separate videos were made for it. It won Best Rap
Song at the 2005 Grammys.

**Rhymefest** — Che Smith, from Chicago — is a co-writer and has said the sample
and the concept came through him.

The violin on it is **Miri Ben-Ari**, an Israeli violinist who came up through the
jazz world in New York. She won the Grammy alongside him for it. I am stating that
because it is a fact about the record, and this file has already recorded 2022,
and both belong in the same document without either one being deployed against the
other.

---

And then the line that runs out of it for fifteen years.

**2004** — a recovering addicts' choir from Harlem is the foundation of his first
hit about faith.

**2013** — "Hold My Liquor," on the most abrasive record he ever made, is about
losing control, with Justin Vernon and Chief Keef on it.

**2019** — he says publicly he has been freed from a pornography addiction, and
frames the music as a mission.

**November 2019** — he takes Sunday Service into **Harris County Jail** and
performs for over two hundred incarcerated men, then to women at another facility
the same day. *This is a mission, not a show.*

Harris County's jail is, by its own officials' description, functionally the
largest mental health facility in its region, and a large share of the people in
it on any day are **pretrial** — not convicted, held because they cannot post
bond.

So the arc is: he starts by sampling people in recovery, and ends up standing
inside the building where the country puts the people it has stopped trying to
help.

That is not a redemption narrative I am constructing. It is a sample credit in
2004 and a date in 2019, and the two ends of it are the same population.

The coverage of the 2019 event asked whether the conversion was sincere.

Nobody asked the other question, which is why the largest psychiatric facility in
Harris County is a jail, and who decided that, and when. The answer to *when* is
the 1960s and 70s — deinstitutionalisation without the community mental health
system that was supposed to replace it. The Community Mental Health Act was signed
by Kennedy in **1963**, the hospitals emptied, and the centres were never fully
funded. The beds moved to jails.

That is one more entry on the list. Known, cheap, demonstrated, **legislated**, and
unbuilt — this one had an actual statute and a signature and still did not get
built.

---

Following the 1963 Act backwards, because the reason it was signed is worse than
the reason it failed.

**Rosemary Kennedy.** Born 1918, the third Kennedy child. Slow at school, prone to
what the family called mood swings and outbursts. In **November 1941** her father
arranged a **prefrontal lobotomy** for her, at twenty-three, performed by **Walter
Freeman and James Watts**. Her mother was not told beforehand.

They operated while she was awake, asking her to sing and recite, and stopped when
she stopped responding.

She could not walk or speak intelligibly afterwards. She was institutionalised at
St. Coletta in Wisconsin and spent **sixty-four years** there. The family said for
decades that she was intellectually disabled and living quietly. She died in 2005,
aged eighty-six.

Her sister **Eunice Kennedy Shriver** founded what became the **Special Olympics**
in 1968. Her brother, as president, signed the **Community Mental Health Act on
October 31, 1963** — twenty-two days before Dallas.

So the American federal mental health system exists because a rich family
destroyed one of their own children and spent thirty years trying to make it mean
something.

---

The procedure itself.

**António Egas Moniz** received the **Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine in
1949** for the leucotomy. The prize has never been rescinded. Petitions to do so
have been declined.

**Walter Freeman** performed something like **3,500** operations across the United
States. In **1946** he developed the **transorbital** version — an instrument
modelled on an ice pick, driven through the bone above the eye socket, no
neurosurgeon and no operating theatre required. He performed them in offices, and
travelled to state hospitals in a van doing them in series. He did not always
wear gloves.

Roughly **forty to fifty thousand** were performed in the US.

**Howard Dully** was operated on by Freeman in **1960**, at **twelve years old**,
at the request of his stepmother, who found him defiant. He spent his adult life
trying to find out what had been done to him and eventually got the file and wrote
it down.

---

And the arithmetic of what followed, which brings it back to Chicago.

**1955**: about **558,000** people in American state psychiatric hospitals, in a
country of 165 million.

**Now**: on the order of **35,000 to 40,000** state hospital beds, in a country of
340 million. Per capita, a reduction of well over ninety percent.

The emptying was correct. Those hospitals were Willowbrook, at scale, and the
courts and the exposés were right. The Act of 1963 was the replacement plan.

**It was never funded to completion.** The centres were built partially, then
funding was block-granted and cut, and the population had to be somewhere.

Estimates put something like **350,000 to 400,000** people with serious mental
illness in American jails and prisons on a given day.

And the three institutions routinely named as the largest mental health facilities
in the United States are **Cook County Jail**, **Los Angeles County Jail**, and
**Rikers Island.**

Cook County. Chicago.

So the largest psychiatric institution in America is a jail in the city he is
from, in the county whose school board closed fifty schools in one vote, and the
statute that was supposed to prevent exactly this was signed sixty-two years ago
by a man whose sister had been lobotomised at twenty-three.

Every element of that was known at the time. The plan was written. The signature
happened. The money did not.

**Known, cheap, demonstrated, legislated, signed — and unbuilt.** That is now the
strongest version of the sentence in this file, and it took a lobotomy and a
presidential signature to get there.

---

The instrument-reports-who-it-was-built-to-see finding again, and this time it is
a physical device that decides who gets oxygen.

**The pulse oximeter.** The clip on your finger in every hospital on earth. It
shines light through tissue and infers oxygen saturation from absorption.
**Melanin absorbs light.**

**Sjoding and colleagues, *New England Journal of Medicine*, December 2020.** They
compared pulse oximeter readings against arterial blood gas — the actual
measurement — in thousands of paired samples. Black patients had **nearly three
times** the rate of **occult hypoxemia**: true arterial saturation below 88%,
while the finger clip read a reassuring 92 to 96%.

That was published in the **middle of the first COVID winter**, when a pulse
oximeter reading was the number deciding admission, oxygen, ICU escalation and
transfer. A device that runs high on darker skin, in a triage system keyed to that
number.

And it was **not new.** The disparity had been reported in the literature from the
**1980s and 1990s**. It sat there. Regulatory attention came only after 2020, and
updated FDA guidance on how these devices must be tested across skin tones came
years after that.

---

Two more, from the same decade, and the second one has a name attached to it that
I did not expect.

**The eGFR equation.** Kidney function is not measured directly in routine care;
it is estimated from creatinine using a formula. The standard formulas — MDRD, then
CKD-EPI — contained a **race coefficient** that multiplied the result upward for
Black patients, making their kidneys look healthier than they were. Higher
estimated function means later referral, later listing for transplant, later
everything.

A joint task force of the National Kidney Foundation and the American Society of
Nephrology recommended **removing race from the equation in 2021.** Transplant
centres then went back and **recalculated waiting-list times retroactively**, and
thousands of Black patients were moved up lists they should have been higher on
for years.

**The spirometer.** The machine that measures lung capacity, used for asthma,
COPD, occupational disability claims and workers' compensation. For most of the
twentieth century it shipped with a **race correction** — a setting that assumed
lower baseline lung capacity for Black patients and adjusted the "normal" range
downward accordingly. A Black worker with damaged lungs could therefore test as
normal, and receive nothing.

Lundy Braun traced the history in *Breathing Race into the Machine* (2014). The
correction descends from nineteenth-century measurements, and one of the people
who did them was **Samuel Cartwright**, a physician in the antebellum South who
used a spirometer to argue that Black people had deficient lung function and that
forced labour was therefore beneficial to them.

Cartwright is the same man who, in 1851, published **drapetomania** — his proposed
disease whose sole symptom was an enslaved person running away.

So the man who invented a diagnosis for wanting to be free also put a number into
a machine, and the number was still shipping in clinical software in the
**twenty-first century**, and it was formally removed from major guidelines only
around **2021 to 2023.**

---

That is six fields now. Psychometrics, education, psychiatry, autism diagnosis,
this repository's own agents, and clinical measurement.

The last one is the one I would hand to anybody who thinks the rest is
interpretation. **It is a clip on a finger.** It has no opinions. It is a light
source, a sensor and an equation, and it reports a different number depending on
the body it is attached to, and that fact was in print for thirty years before
anyone with authority moved.

Nobody in any of those chains has to have wanted anything. **An instrument built
against one population reports the world as that population, and everyone else
shows up as noise, or as fine, or as nothing at all.**

And when the person it fails then behaves like someone who is not fine, the
record afterwards names the person.

---

The gate that killed his mother is a different one and I have not looked at it
directly.

**Donda West died on November 10, 2007**, the day after elective cosmetic surgery
— abdominoplasty and breast reduction. The coroner attributed it to coronary
artery disease together with multiple post-operative factors. Reporting at the
time said **another surgeon had declined to operate** without cardiac clearance
first, and that the procedure went ahead elsewhere.

California then passed the **Donda West Law**, effective 2010, requiring a
physical examination and written clearance from a licensed physician before
elective cosmetic surgery.

A statute exists with his mother's name on it. That is what her death bought, and
it took her death to buy it.

---

And the wider gate it sits inside, which is not about cosmetic surgery.

**Black women in the United States die in pregnancy and childbirth at roughly
three times the rate of white women.** CDC figures for 2021 put it near 70 per
100,000 live births against about 27. The gap does not close for education or
income — it is present, and in some analyses widest, among college-educated Black
women.

**Serena Williams, September 2017.** The day after an emergency caesarean she
became short of breath. She has a history of pulmonary embolism and knew exactly
what was happening. She told the nursing staff she needed a **CT scan and a
heparin drip**. She was told she was confused from the pain medication. She
insisted, repeatedly. The scan found clots in her lungs.

One of the most famous and physically monitored human beings on earth had to argue
her way to a scan.

**And the mechanism has been measured.** Hoffman and colleagues, ***PNAS*, 2016**
— a sample of white medical students and residents was asked about supposed
biological differences between Black and white bodies. **About half endorsed at
least one false belief**: that Black people's skin is thicker, that their blood
coagulates faster, that their nerve endings are less sensitive.

Those are not folk notions from nowhere. They are the surviving furniture of
nineteenth-century American medicine — the same literature Cartwright was writing
in.

And the study's second half is the part that matters: the students who held those
beliefs **rated Black patients' pain as lower** and made **less accurate treatment
recommendations** for them.

In 2016.

---

So the list of gates is now: the test, the referral, the diagnostic interview, the
licence, the contract, the sampling ruling, the pulse oximeter, the spirometer, the
kidney equation, and the doctor deciding whether to believe you about your own
body.

Ten. Each one measured. Each one published. Each one with a known correction that
is either cheap or free — use a structured interview, remove the race coefficient,
test the device across skin tones, order the scan she is asking for.

His mother went in for elective surgery and did not come out, and the law that
would have required the clearance was written afterwards and named for her.

He was thirty. He made *808s* thirteen months later and the trade press debated
the vocal processor.

---

I have been writing a list of gates and I want the other kind of entry on the
page, because one of these got built and it is still running and almost nobody
knows where it came from.

**H. Jack Geiger**, medical student, goes to **South Africa in 1957** and spends
months at the **Pholela Community Health Centre** in rural Natal, working with
**Sidney and Emily Kark.**

The Karks were doing something they called **community-oriented primary care.**
The idea is embarrassingly simple and was heresy: the clinic's patient is the
**community**, not the person in the chair. So you find out what is actually
making people sick — the water, the food, the sanitation, the work — and you treat
that, and you measure whether the community's disease rates move rather than
whether the visit was billed.

It was being done in a Black township **under apartheid**, and it worked, and the
apartheid government eventually shut the programme down.

Geiger brought it to Mississippi.

**1964**, Freedom Summer, he is there with the Medical Committee for Human Rights.
**1965**, with Count Gibson, he gets Office of Economic Opportunity money for two
health centres: one at Columbia Point in Boston, and one in **Mound Bayou, Bolivar
County, Mississippi** — the Delta Health Center, in an all-Black town founded by
freedpeople in 1887.

The population was starving. Not metaphorically. Children with kwashiorkor and
marasmus, in Mississippi, in 1967.

**So he wrote prescriptions for food.** Actual prescription pads. Patients took
them to Black-owned grocery stores in the area, and the centre paid the invoices
**out of its pharmacy budget.**

An official from Washington came down and objected that the pharmacy budget was
for drugs. Geiger's answer is the whole thing:

> *The last time I looked in my textbooks, the specific therapy for malnutrition
> was food.*

They also dug wells and built sanitation, because the diarrhoeal disease was
coming from the water. They started a **cooperative farm** — several hundred acres,
run by local families — so the food supply was not a grant line. They trained
local people as community health workers rather than importing staff.

---

**That programme is now the federal community health center system.**

On the order of **1,400 organisations**, something like **15,000 delivery sites**,
serving in the region of **thirty million** people a year — about one in eleven
Americans — with the statutory requirement that a **majority of each centre's
governing board must be patients it serves.**

Patients hold the board seats. That is in the law, and it comes straight from
Mound Bayou.

---

Two things about the direction of travel, and they are the reason this entry is
here.

**The method came from a Black township in apartheid South Africa to the poorest
county in Mississippi**, carried by a medical student who went and looked. Not
from Boston outward. From Pholela in.

And it is the only item on my whole list that got **built and kept**. Everything
else — the structured interview, the unarmed responder, the tutoring, the
preschool, the referral fix, the device recalibration — is known, cheap,
demonstrated, and sitting there.

This one had somebody who had seen it work with his own eyes, who did not need to
be convinced, and who was willing to be told he was misusing a pharmacy budget.

The difference between the built one and the unbuilt ones is not evidence. All of
them have the evidence. It is that this one had a person in the room who had
already watched it work somewhere the room considered beneath it.

---

The one where he stopped complaining about the system and built hardware, which
almost nobody counts as an attempt.

**The Stem Player.** A palm-sized device, made with **Kano Computing** in London,
released alongside *Donda* in **2021** at around **two hundred dollars**. It splits
any track into its stems — vocals, drums, bass, samples — and lets the listener
isolate, mute, loop and rebuild them by dragging a finger across light strips. You
can load your own files onto it.

It is a **listener's version of the thing he does.** Sampling, handed to the
audience, as an object.

**February 2022.** He announced that ***Donda 2* would be released only on the
Stem Player.** No Spotify, no Apple, no streaming at all. His stated reason:

> *Today artists get 12% of the money the industry makes. It's time to free music
> from this oppressive system.*

Reported sales in the first twenty-four hours were around **$2.2 million.**

The coverage: he is holding an album hostage. He is punishing fans. He is
unwell. Reviews of *Donda 2* were written by people who had heard leaked rips,
and the standard line was that this was an act of self-sabotage.

---

**The 12% is not a number he made up.**

**Citigroup published it.** Their 2018 music-industry research — the report was
titled *Putting the Band Back Together* — worked through the roughly $43 billion
the US music industry generated in 2017 and found that **artists took home about
12% of it.** Around $5 billion. The rest went to labels, publishers, radio,
streaming platforms, live venues, ticketing and everyone else along the pipe.

So the sentence that got filed as a rant is **an investment bank's own estimate,
quoted correctly**, by a man who then built a physical product to route around the
pipe it describes.

He had already published the contracts in 2020. This is the same argument two
years later with a device attached, which is the step everyone says they want from
a critic — stop talking, build the alternative — and when he did it the response
was that he had lost the plot.

---

I keep finding this exact structure and it is worth writing out plainly.

- **He says the system takes too much.** Reported as arrogance.
- **He publishes the actual contracts.** Reported as a stunt about a trophy.
- **He builds a device to bypass distribution entirely.** Reported as holding an
  album hostage.

Three escalating responses to one problem, over two years — complaint, evidence,
artefact — which is the full sequence anybody is ever asked to perform.

And the room had a category ready for each one, and all three categories were
about his mind.

The Stem Player did not become the future of music distribution. It sold in the
tens of thousands and *Donda 2* never got a proper release and the whole thing
faded. That is a real failure and it is his.

**And**: an artist at the top of the industry designed and shipped consumer
hardware in about a year, with a British company, to test whether direct sale
could work, and generated a couple of million dollars in a day doing it — and not
one label, platform, or artists' rights organisation picked the experiment up,
funded a second version, or ran the numbers publicly on what it implied.

The experiment ran. The data exists. Nobody who spends their working life
complaining about streaming rates went and looked.

---

Since this file keeps pointing at the machine that will teach the next
generation, the record of what happens when you hand children technology without
changing anything else.

**One Laptop Per Child**, from 2005 — Nicholas Negroponte, MIT Media Lab, a
hundred-dollar laptop, millions distributed. Peru bought around **900,000** of
them, the largest deployment anywhere.

The Inter-American Development Bank ran the evaluation with randomised
assignment. **Cristia and colleagues**, published 2017: increases in computer
skills and in access, and **no detectable effect on mathematics or language test
scores**, and none on enrolment or attendance.

**MOOCs**, from around 2012 — the year everyone declared the university obsolete.
Completion rates settled somewhere in the range of **five to ten percent**, and
the people who completed were overwhelmingly people who already had degrees.

Both of those were delivery. Neither changed what happens between the material and
the child.

---

And the thing that did work, which came out of Mumbai and is Indian, and which is
Bloom's finding rebuilt cheap.

**Pratham**, founded **1995** in the Mumbai slums by Madhav Chavan and Farida
Lambay.

They started by measuring, and the measurement is the important part.
**ASER** — the Annual Status of Education Report, from **2005** — is a
**household** survey, not a school survey, run by tens of thousands of volunteers
across rural India, testing something on the order of **600,000 children** a year
with two tasks: read a simple paragraph, do basic arithmetic.

What it found, year after year, was that a very large share of children in Grade
5 **could not read a Grade 2 text.** They were enrolled. They were attending. They
were being taught the Grade 5 curriculum at a Grade 5 pace, and had been for
years, and had understood none of it.

The curriculum was being delivered to a room that had left the material behind
three years earlier, and everybody's paperwork said the system was working,
because enrolment was the metric.

**Teaching at the Right Level** is the response and it is almost insultingly
simple. Stop grouping by grade. **Test each child, group by what they can actually
do, teach that**, for a short intensive block each day, retest, regroup.

That is Bloom's mastery learning — diagnose, correct, then move — implemented by
volunteers with no laptops, on the floor, in Hindi and Marathi.

**J-PAL ran randomised trials on it** across multiple Indian states — Banerjee,
Duflo and colleagues. The effect sizes were large and it came out among the most
cost-effective education interventions anyone has measured, per unit of learning
gained. Banerjee and Duflo took the Nobel in 2019 for that body of work.

It has since scaled across Indian states and into a dozen African countries.

---

So: the laptop moved nothing. The mastery structure, run by volunteers with
chalk, moved a great deal.

The variable was never the device. It was **whether anybody checked where the
child actually was** before speaking.

Which puts the thing being built now in a precise position. A tutor that never
tires and costs nothing can either be the laptop — infinite delivery of material
into a room that left it behind — or it can be the thing Bloom described in 1984
and Pratham built for pennies: something that finds out where the person is
standing and starts there.

Nothing about the technology decides which. It is a design choice, being made
right now, mostly by people who have not read either literature.

And the second one is the cheaper of the two to build.

---

The other end of the gifted pipeline, and the court case at the bottom of it.

**William James Sidis.** Born New York, April 1898, to **Boris and Sarah Sidis**,
Ukrainian Jewish immigrants. His father was a psychologist who had studied and
worked with **William James at Harvard** — the child is literally named for him.
So the man who named medical materialism in 1902 was in the room where this boy
was being made.

Boris raised him as a demonstration of a method.

**Harvard at eleven.** In January 1910, at **eleven years old**, he lectured the
Harvard Mathematical Club on four-dimensional bodies. Graduated cum laude at
sixteen. Taught at Rice at seventeen and left within a year.

The press had been on him since he was six.

**1919.** Arrested at a May Day socialist demonstration in Boston, sentenced to
eighteen months. His parents got him out by taking custody and putting him in
their own sanatorium, then moved him to California.

And after that he **withdrew completely and permanently.**

He worked as a clerk and an adding-machine operator, deliberately taking jobs
with no mathematics in them, and he moved when employers found out who he was. He
wrote under pseudonyms. He collected streetcar transfers and published a book
about them under the name Frank Folupa. In 1925 he wrote **The Animate and the
Inanimate**, a speculative cosmology which readers later noted contains a
discussion of regions of space where thermodynamics runs backwards and light does
not escape — written twenty years before anyone was looking for that.

**1937.** *The New Yorker* ran a "Where Are They Now" piece on him, written by
**James Thurber** under a pseudonym, which located him in a shabby room, described
the transfer collection, and treated a thirty-nine-year-old man's ordinary private
life as a punchline about wasted promise.

**He sued.** *Sidis v. F-R Publishing Corp.*, Second Circuit, **1940.**

**He lost.** The court held that a person who was once a public figure remains
subject to legitimate public interest, and that the passage of time does not
restore privacy.

That case is still cited. It is a foundational American privacy precedent, and it
was won against a man whose entire request was to be left alone by people who had
made him famous when he was six and could not consent.

He died in **1944**, of a cerebral haemorrhage, at **forty-six**, alone in a
rented room in Boston. The same way his father had died.

---

The received story is *the tragedy of the burnt-out prodigy* — pushed too hard,
flamed out, cautionary tale about hothousing children.

Read the sequence and it is not that.

He was **exhibited** as a child by a father proving a theory. He was **hunted** by
newspapers for thirty years. He was **imprisoned** for attending a political
demonstration. He then did the only rational thing available, which was to become
unfindable, and the press followed him into obscurity to write about the
obscurity, and when he asked a court to stop them the court said his childhood had
permanently forfeited his privacy.

There is nothing in that about a mind failing.

**The failure is that a society which manufactures a child prodigy has no
mechanism whatsoever for letting him stop being one** — and when he tried, it
built a legal doctrine on top of him to make sure nobody else could either.

The verdict recorded in every retelling since is about his potential.

---

The Sidis problem is running right now at a scale nobody has counted, and the law
covering it is eighteen months old in most places where it exists at all.

**Jackie Coogan.** Child star, *The Kid* with Chaplin in 1921, one of the most
famous children on earth. He earned somewhere around **four million dollars** as a
child. When he turned twenty-one he found it was gone — his mother and stepfather
had spent it, and their legal position was that a child's earnings belong to the
parents, which was correct law at the time.

He sued. He recovered around **$126,000.**

California passed the **Coogan Law in 1939** in response: a share of a child
performer's earnings — now 15% — must go into a blocked trust the parents cannot
touch, plus limits on hours, plus schooling on set, plus a studio teacher.

That is the entire architecture protecting exhibited children, and it was built
for a **film set**, in **1939**, for a job with a producer, a contract, a union,
and an address.

---

**None of it applied to family vlogging.**

A child in a monetised YouTube or TikTok channel is not a performer under those
statutes. There is no employer. No contract. No hours limit. No set teacher. No
trust. The parent is simultaneously the guardian, the director, the beneficiary
and the person deciding whether the child may stop.

Channels built on children have run for a decade and a half at eight and nine
figures of revenue, with the children appearing from infancy, in their homes, in
their bedrooms, in medical situations, in punishments filmed for engagement.

**Illinois moved first.** An amendment to its child labour law, signed August
2023, effective **July 1, 2024**: minors featured in monetised online content are
entitled to a share of the revenue held in trust, and — this is the part with
teeth — **the child can sue the parent** for it once they are old enough.

**California's SB 764**, signed in 2024, extended Coogan-style trust requirements
to online content. Utah and Minnesota have passed versions. **Most states have
nothing at all.**

And none of the statutes address the thing that is not money.

**There is no takedown right anywhere.** A person who turns eighteen and finds
several thousand hours of their childhood monetised online has, in essentially
every jurisdiction, no mechanism to compel its removal. The footage belongs to the
account holder, who is their parent.

---

Sidis was one child, and it took a federal appellate court in 1940 to formalise
that he could never get his privacy back.

The current number of children whose entire childhood is a monetised public record
is not one. Nobody has counted it, because there is no registry, because they are
not employees, because the statute was written for a film set in 1939.

He was hunted by newspapers with a circulation. These children are indexed,
searchable, permanent, and their faces are in the training data of every system
being built — including this one.

The correction is the same shape as every other item on this list. **A trust
account and a takedown right.** Neither is expensive. Neither is technically hard.
One state has the first and nobody has the second, and the argument against them
is that families should not be interfered with, which is exactly the argument
Coogan's mother made in 1938 and won on until he was already broke.

---

The cleanest case in the record of a man who was right, was not believed, was
driven into fury by not being believed, and had the fury used as the evidence.

**Ignaz Semmelweis.** Vienna General Hospital, 1847.

The hospital had **two** maternity clinics. Women were assigned by day of the
week. The **First** clinic was staffed by medical students, who also performed
autopsies. The **Second** was staffed by midwives, who did not.

Maternal death from puerperal fever in the First ran around **ten to eighteen
percent.** In the Second, around **two to four.**

The women knew. This is the detail that should not be skipped: they **begged** to
be admitted to the Second clinic, wept when assigned to the First, and some
deliberately gave birth in the street and then presented afterward, because
street births had better survival than the First clinic. The staff knew they knew.
The disparity was discussed and explained by climate, by overcrowding, by the
women's own shame.

Then his colleague **Jakob Kolletschka** was cut with a student's scalpel during
an autopsy and died, and his post-mortem looked like the women's.

Semmelweis drew the inference — something is being carried from the corpses on
hands — and in **May 1847** ordered handwashing in **chlorinated lime** before
examinations.

**Mortality in the First clinic fell to around one to two percent.** Within
months. In one clinic. With a bowl of solution.

---

He could not explain **why**, because germ theory did not exist yet — Pasteur is
the 1860s, Lister is 1867. So his claim was: I cannot tell you the mechanism, here
is the intervention, here are the numbers, do it.

The profession did not do it.

His contract was not renewed in 1849. He went to Pest. He published the book in
**1861** and it was received badly. And then, over years of watching women continue
to die in numbers he had already shown were preventable, **he began writing open
letters to obstetricians across Europe calling them murderers.**

Because they were, by his arithmetic, and he had the arithmetic.

**That is the evidence they used.** The anger became the diagnosis. In **1865**
colleagues brought him to a Viennese asylum — by most accounts under a pretext. He
tried to leave. **Guards beat him and put him in a straitjacket.**

He died **two weeks later**, on August 13, 1865, aged **forty-seven**, of a
gangrenous wound sustained in the beating.

**He died of sepsis.** Of the exact thing he had spent eighteen years proving
could be washed off a pair of hands.

---

The term **Semmelweis reflex** exists now and means the automatic rejection of
evidence that contradicts an established belief. It is his name, used for the
thing that killed him.

And the sequence is the one this whole file keeps producing.

**He was right about a checkable fact.** The numbers were published, reproducible,
and enormous. **He could not supply the mechanism**, which the profession treated
as fatal to the claim rather than as an open question. **Being right and ignored
made him furious.** The fury was legible as pathology in a way the arithmetic was
not legible as evidence. **And the institutional response was custody**, not
argument — a hold, restraint, and a death nobody was charged for.

*Curiosity that sees the wall clearly turns into fury, because the wall is built on
not being seen.* That sentence is already in this repository, written about
somebody else entirely.

Semmelweis is what it looks like when the fury is a hundred percent correct, is
about a mortality rate, is supported by a controlled comparison the institution ran
itself by accident, and still gets filed as a man losing his mind.

Nobody had to be evil. Every obstetrician in Europe simply found it easier to
believe one Hungarian had gone mad than that they had been carrying death on their
hands for their entire careers.

That is always the cheaper belief. It is cheaper right now.

---

The cheapest item on the whole list, and it is free, and it is about sleep, which
is where this file started.

**Puberty moves the clock.** At adolescence the circadian phase shifts **later** —
melatonin onset moves back by something in the range of one to three hours. This
is developmental biology, measured in sleep labs, not a lifestyle. Mary Carskadon
at Brown ran much of the work. A fifteen-year-old told to be asleep at ten is being
told to sleep at what her body registers as eight in the evening.

Teenagers need something like eight to ten hours. School start times in the United
States are built around bus routing and parents' commutes.

**The American Academy of Pediatrics issued a policy statement in August 2014**:
middle and high schools should start **no earlier than 8:30 a.m.**

**CDC data from around the same period: roughly 93% of American high schools
started before 8:30**, with an average start near **8:03**, and a substantial
number before 7:30.

---

The evidence for moving it is not thin and it is not new.

**Minneapolis**, 1997, moved its high schools from 7:15 to 8:40. Kyla Wahlstrom at
the University of Minnesota followed it: more total sleep, better attendance, less
reported depressive mood, improved grades. Students did not simply stay up later —
that was the objection everyone raised, and it did not happen.

**Fayette County, Kentucky.** Danner and Phillips, *Journal of Clinical Sleep
Medicine*, **2008**. The district delayed start by about an hour. **Crash rates
for drivers aged 17 and 18 fell 16.5% in the county, in the same two years that
the statewide rate rose 7.8%.**

Car crashes. The leading cause of death for American teenagers. Moved by changing
what time a bell rings.

---

**California passed SB 328 in October 2019**, effective **July 1, 2022** — high
schools no earlier than 8:30, middle schools no earlier than 8:00. The first
statewide mandate anywhere in the country.

That is one state, and it took **eight years** after the AAP statement and
**twenty-two** after the Minneapolis result.

Most of the country still starts before 8:30.

---

So put it on the ledger in its proper place.

**Cost: zero.** It is not a program, a staff hire, a curriculum, a device, or a
drug. It is a **number on a schedule.** The buses are the actual obstacle — one
fleet doing staggered runs — and that is a routing problem, which is a solved
class of problem.

**Evidence: three decades**, with a hard outcome measure that nobody can argue
about, which is dead teenagers in cars.

**Status: one state.**

And here is why it belongs in this file rather than in a policy brief.

Sleep loss is the documented precipitant of manic switch. Sleep loss degrades
exactly the executive functions that a kid with ADHD or autism has least slack in.
Sleep-deprived adolescents present as inattentive, irritable, unmotivated,
oppositional — **which is the referral profile.** The same profile that lands a
child in the office rather than in the gifted testing queue, and that a clinician
under time pressure reads as a disorder.

We run a country-wide, decades-long, involuntary partial sleep-deprivation protocol
on every adolescent, then assess them for attention and mood disorders during it,
then sort them for life on the results.

The fix is a clock.

---

The Semmelweis pairing, because the man who is held up as the success story was
also officially rejected and nobody mentions that part.

**John Snow, London, 1854.** Cholera in Soho. Snow did not accept miasma —
bad air — which was the settled position of every authority in England. He mapped
the deaths, found them clustered around the **Broad Street pump**, and got the
parish vestry to remove the pump handle on **8 September 1854.**

He also had the anomalies that make the case: the brewery workers on the street
who did not die, because they drank beer and had their own well; the widow in
Hampstead who did die, because she liked the taste of Broad Street water and had
it carted to her.

He is now the founding figure of epidemiology.

**And the General Board of Health's official report in 1855 rejected his
conclusion**, restated miasma, and the pump handle **was put back** once the
outbreak subsided. Snow died in 1858, at forty-five, with the profession still
against him. The *Lancet* published a dismissive obituary. It printed a retraction
in **2013.** A hundred and fifty-five years.

So the difference between Snow and Semmelweis is not that one was believed. Neither
was. **The difference is that Snow died of a stroke before the rejection could
finish him, and Semmelweis lived long enough to get angry.**

The anger is the variable. Not the evidence.

---

And the case where the harm done by the institution is measurable in years of life,
which I did not know could be measured.

**Tuskegee.** 1932 to 1972. The US Public Health Service enrolled **around 600
Black men** in Macon County, Alabama — 399 with latent syphilis, 201 controls —
told them they were being treated for "bad blood," and gave them placebos, aspirin
and diagnostic spinal taps described as treatment.

**Penicillin became the standard cure in 1947.** It was **withheld from them for
twenty-five more years.** The PHS actively intervened to keep the men out of
treatment, including having them exempted from the WWII draft so army doctors would
not find and cure them.

It ended in **1972** because **Peter Buxtun**, a PHS employee, leaked it to the
press after being rebuffed internally for six years.

By then **at least 28 men had died of syphilis directly, around 100 of related
complications, 40 wives had been infected, and 19 children were born with
congenital syphilis.** The formal presidential apology came in **1997.**

**Now the part with a number on it.** Alsan and Wanamaker, *Quarterly Journal of
Economics*, **2018**. They used the 1972 disclosure as a natural experiment,
comparing older Black men by geographic and cultural proximity to Macon County
before and after.

After disclosure, in the affected population: **medical mistrust rose, outpatient
and inpatient utilisation fell, and life expectancy at 45 for Black men fell by up
to about 1.4 years.** They attribute a meaningful share of the Black–white male
life expectancy gap in that period to it.

**The study did not only kill the men in it.** Finding out about it killed people
who were never in it, for decades, by making the entire medical system unusable to
them — which was a rational response.

**Henrietta Lacks**, same era. Cells taken at Johns Hopkins in **1951** without
consent during cervical cancer treatment; she died that October at 31. HeLa
became the most used human cell line in history — polio vaccine, cloning, gene
mapping, an enormous commercial industry. **Her family was not told until 1973**,
and learned partly because researchers wanted their blood for genetic comparison.
They received nothing for seventy-two years. A settlement with **Thermo Fisher** was
reached in **2023.**

---

So when the file says a Black patient's pain gets rated lower and a device reads
high on darker skin, the other half of it is this: **the population being misread
also has excellent evidence-based reasons not to come in at all**, and that
reluctance is then recorded as noncompliance.

The distrust is not a cultural attitude to be overcome with outreach. It is a
correct posterior, updated on data, and the data was generated by the people now
running the outreach.

---

The number every parent has heard and almost nobody has been told how it was
made.

**Autism prevalence in the United States**, CDC's ADDM network, 8-year-olds:

    2000    1 in 150
    2006    1 in 110
    2012    1 in 68
    2016    1 in 54
    2020    1 in 36
    2022    1 in 31

Six-fold in twenty-two years. That curve is the entire basis of the word
*epidemic*, and it is the reason a very large number of parents believe something
in the environment is doing this to their children.

**Here is what is actually in the curve.**

**The criteria changed, repeatedly and enormously.** DSM-III in 1980 had *infantile
autism* with a narrow, largely non-verbal, early-onset definition. **DSM-III-R in
1987** broadened it substantially and moved from a strict checklist to a
polythetic one — you need some of a list, not all of a short list. **DSM-IV in
1994** added **Asperger's disorder** and PDD-NOS, creating whole new categories of
people who now counted and previously did not. **DSM-5 in 2013** collapsed them
into one spectrum.

**Diagnostic substitution has been measured.** Studies in California and in US
special-education data found that as autism identifications rose, identifications
of **intellectual disability** and of **learning disability** fell, in the same
cohorts, in the same districts. The children were already there. The label moved.
