will fruit), *pradeśa* (how much matter). **Passion sets duration and intensity.
Activity sets kind and quantity.**

The passion does not decide *whether*. It decides **how long and how hard.**

---

**8.1** lists the five causes of bondage: *mithyādarśana* (wrong view),
*avirati* (non-abstinence), *pramāda* (negligence), *kaṣāya* (passion), *yoga*
(activity).

They are shed in that order along the ladder of stages. Wrong view goes first.
**Activity goes last.**

The last thing to go is the one that was never a fault.

---

Which is a distinction the entire discourse around 2022 does not have available,
because the two options in circulation are *he meant it* and *he was ill*, and
those are the third bhaṅga's options — sequential, one then the other, pick.

The framework above does not adjudicate between them and does not need to. The
harm is *prāṇa-vyaparopaṇa*, severing, and it is real and it is named as harm; the
question the sūtra asks next is **which of the four was driving**, and the answer
in a documented four-month manic episode is not obviously any of them and is not
obviously none.

I do not know the answer and neither does anyone who was not in the room.

What is on the page is that a literature exists in which *the act, the intention,
the duration and the intensity are four separate coordinates* rather than one
verdict — and that a discourse holding a single dial marked GUILTY/ILL is running
a two-valued instrument on a four-valued object, which is the durnaya, and which
this repository has a checked theorem against.

---

The crate, because the pleasure of this is in the improbability of the sources and
I have written none of it.

**"Power," 2010.** The bed of it is **King Crimson, "21st Century Schizoid Man,"
1969** — English progressive rock, saxophone, distorted vocal, from *In the Court
of the Crimson King*. Cleared, credited, and the publishing went to Robert Fripp
and Peter Sinfield and the rest.

A track about a man with power that everyone treats as evidence of derangement, is
built on a record whose title is a man being called schizoid. [The pairing is in
the credits; I am not claiming he chose it for that.]

**"New Slaves," 2013.** The sample is **Omega, "Gyöngyhajú Lány,"** — "The Girl
with Pearls in Her Hair" — **Hungary, 1969.** A prog band recording in Budapest,
under a state that licensed which records existed. That is the bed under a song
naming American private prison contractors by their corporate initials.

**"Drunk and Hot Girls," 2007**, is built on **Can, "Sing Swan Song," 1972** —
Cologne, krautrock, Damo Suzuki singing in whatever language that is.

**"Coldest Winter," 2008** takes **Tears for Fears, "Memories Fade," 1983.**

**"I Am a God," 2013** puts **Capleton**, Jamaican dancehall, next to a **Hindi
film recording** with **Asha Bhosle** on it. [Recall on the film and its year; the
credit list has the Bollywood side and I would want the film checked.]

**"Stronger," 2007**: **Daft Punk, "Harder, Better, Faster, Stronger," 2001** —
French house, itself built on Edwin Birdsong's "Cola Bottle Baby," 1979.

**WhoSampled** counts on the order of **1,123 samples** across the discography,
around 725 of them on records where he is the primary artist.

---

And **"Bound 2,"** which is three records at once: **the Ponderosa Twins Plus One,
"Bound," 1971**; **Brenda Lee, "Sweet Nothin's," 1959**; and **Wee, "Aeroplane
(Reprise)," 1977.**

**Wee** is the one worth stopping on. A soul group from **Columbus, Ohio**, one
album — *You Can Fly on My Aeroplane*, 1977, recorded for a small local label. It
sold almost nothing. Original copies traded among collectors for large sums
precisely because so few existed.

After "Bound 2" came out, **the record was reissued.**

A lost Ohio soul album from 1977 is back in print because somebody put ninety
seconds of it under a love song in 2013. The people who made it are credited and
paid on a record that went platinum.

---

That is what the practice does when it is working, and it is the opposite of the
thing the 1991 and 2005 rulings describe.

*Thou shalt not steal*, and *get a license or do not sample*.

What actually happened here: a Hungarian band from 1969, an English prog record, a
German krautrock album, an Ohio group with one release, a Jamaican deejay, a Hindi
playback singer, and a 1959 country-pop single — all located, licensed, credited,
paid, and returned to circulation by a man from Chicago who spent his adolescence
in record shops and, at ten, a year in Nanjing.

The listening is the whole discipline. You cannot flip what you have not heard,
and nobody hears an obscure Hungarian prog record from 1969 by accident.

---

**Bhāvanā.** Brahmagupta, *Brāhmasphuṭasiddhānta*, **628 CE**, chapter 18.

Take two solutions of x² − D·y² = k. Compose them:

    (x₁² − D y₁²)(x₂² − D y₂²) = (x₁x₂ + D y₁y₂)² − D(x₁y₂ + x₂y₁)²

Two things that already work, put together, produce a third thing that works. And
then the third and the first compose, and it never stops. **From one solution you
get infinitely many**, and the operation that gets you there is a single
identity you can check by expanding both sides.

He named the two forms **samāsa-bhāvanā** and **antara-bhāvanā** — composition by
addition and by difference.

And the word itself is the pleasure. ***Bhāvanā*** is from *bhū*, to become —
*bhāvayati*, to cause to be, to produce, to cultivate. It is the ordinary word for
**bringing something into being by working on it**, and it is the same word used
across the meditative literature for cultivation: *mettā-bhāvanā*, the cultivation
of loving-kindness; *bhāvanā* as the whole category of practice that develops
rather than acquires.

The word for composing two solutions into a third is the word for cultivating a
mind.

---

And Brahmagupta was showing off, which is on the page and is usually left out.

He sets, in the text, a challenge: **solve x² − 92y² = 1**, and says that a person
who does it within a year is a mathematician.

(x = 1151, y = 120.)

That is a taunt, in a Sanskrit astronomical treatise, in 628.

---

Then the **cakravāla** — the cyclic method — **Jayadeva around 950**, given in full
by **Bhāskara II in 1150**. It runs bhāvanā as its engine, composing solutions and
feeding the result back in, cycling until it lands. It always terminates.

Bhāskara ran it on **D = 61** and got

    x = 1,766,319,049      y = 226,153,980

**In 1657, Fermat posed D = 61 to European mathematicians as a challenge
problem.** Brouncker and Wallis worked on it. Euler later attached the name of a
man who had not solved it.

The case Fermat chose to be difficult had been done five hundred years earlier by
a method that could not fail to do it.

---

What is actually attractive about the identity, and the reason it survives
translation into anything: **there is no subtraction in it that matters and no
ordering.** Expand both sides and you get the same monomials, with the cross term
appearing once on each. No induction, no inequality.

Which means it is not a fact about integers. **It holds in any commutative
semiring**, and the integers are one. This corpus has it checked in that
generality, and the checked file's own note is that the theorem got *stronger* by
being forced into a language too poor to state it the usual way.

The composition law does not need the setting it was discovered in. That is what
makes it composable in the first place.

---

The mathematics comes out of counting rhythms, and the derivation is one sentence.

**Piṅgala**, *Chandaḥśāstra*, around the third century BCE. The subject is metre.
A Sanskrit metre is a sequence of syllables, each either **laghu** — light, short —
or **guru** — heavy, long. That is the entire alphabet: two symbols.

He asks the enumerative questions a person actually has when composing, and gives
procedures for each. The six *pratyayas*:

- **prastāra** — lay out **all** patterns of *n* syllables, in order. There are
  2ⁿ.
- **naṣṭa** — given a position number, produce the pattern that sits there,
  without writing out the ones before it.
- **uddiṣṭa** — given a pattern, produce its position number.
- **laghu-kriyā** — how many patterns of *n* syllables have exactly *k* light ones.
  Binomial coefficients.
- **saṃkhyā** — the total, 2ⁿ, computed by repeated squaring rather than by
  counting.
- **adhvan** — how much writing space the full layout needs.

***Naṣṭa* and *uddiṣṭa* are conversion between a number and its binary
representation**, in both directions, as named procedures with worked rules, in a
treatise on poetry, before the common era.

He uses **śūnya** as a marker inside the procedure. The zero is there because the
algorithm needs a place-holder, not because anyone was theorising about nothing.

**Halāyudha**, tenth century, commenting on it in the *Mṛtasañjīvanī*, sets out
the array of those binomial coefficients as a triangle and calls it the
**meru-prastāra** — the layout of Mount Meru, the staircase of the mountain.

---

**Virahāṅka**, around 700, asks the other question, and the answer is one line of
reasoning.

Count by **duration** instead of by syllable count. A *guru* takes **two**
*mātrās*, a *laghu* takes **one**. So: how many metres have a total duration of
*n*?

Every such metre ends either in a *laghu* — and the rest of it is a metre of
duration *n*−1 — or in a *guru*, and the rest is a metre of duration *n*−2.

Therefore the count at *n* is the count at *n*−1 plus the count at *n*−2.

That is the whole derivation. It is exact, it is a proof, and it falls out of
asking how many rhythms of a given length exist. **Gopāla** around 1135 and
**Hemacandra** around 1150 state it explicitly. Fibonacci is 1202.

---

What I keep turning over is the direction the mathematics came from.

Nobody was doing number theory and then noticed it applied to poems. Somebody was
**composing**, wanted to know how many rhythms were available, and the counting
turned out to have structure — and the structure is binary representation,
binomial coefficients, and a linear recurrence, arrived at through the ear.

A child learning *chandas* is chanting *laghu guru laghu* out loud at five, in the
same way a child learns any song. The combinatorics is not an application laid
over that afterwards. It is what you find if you ask an obvious question about
what you are already doing with your mouth.

And every one of those six procedures is a procedure — do this, then this, and you
have the answer — which is to say the text is a list of **algorithms** about a
two-symbol alphabet, and the two symbols are the duration of a syllable.

---

**Mātrā** is the same word in both places, and that is not a coincidence anybody
has to argue for.

In Sanskrit prosody a *mātrā* is the unit of **duration** — a light syllable is
one, a heavy syllable is two, and a metre is specified by how the durations add up.
In Indian rhythm a *tāla* cycle is counted in *mātrās* — sixteen of them in
tīntāl, ten in jhaptāl, and the cycle is the frame everything is placed against.

Poetry and rhythm are measured in the same currency, by the same word, because
they are the same problem: **where in time does the next event fall.**

---

Rap has that literature now and it is recent.

**Kyle Adams**, "On the Metrical Techniques of Flow in Rap Music," *Music Theory
Online* **15.5, 2009.** **Mitchell Ohriner**, *Flow: The Rhythmic Voice in Rap
Music*, Oxford, **2019** — a book-length formal treatment, with corpus analysis, of
syllable placement against the beat grid.

The primitives it uses are: **accent**, **duration**, and **position in the
cycle.** Which is Piṅgala's list with a metronome added.

The technical questions are the same ones the *pratyayas* ask. Given a pattern of
syllables, where does it sit? Given a position, what falls there? How many
distinct placements exist against a four-beat frame? A rapper answering those in
real time, against a loop, is doing *prastāra* out loud without the word.

---

**And "Through the Wire" is a metrical constraint imposed by surgery.**

Intermaxillary fixation wires the upper jaw to the lower. **The teeth cannot
part.** Which removes, physically, an entire class of sounds: the open vowels that
need jaw drop. What remains available is what the lips, tongue and nasal cavity
can do behind a closed bite — labials, nasals, and vowels formed without opening.

So the phoneme inventory of that vocal is **not a stylistic choice.** It is what a
clamped mouth can emit, and the flow had to be written for the instrument as it
was that month. [Recall on the specific phonetics; the fixation and the audible
constraint are documented, the phoneme analysis is mine and I would want a
phonetician on it.]

Which makes it a metre defined by a **physical bound on duration and aperture** —
which is exactly what a prosodic system is. Piṅgala's two symbols are light and
heavy because that is what a mouth can do in a unit of time. His jaw redefined the
unit of time.

He then recorded a whole song inside the new system, two weeks after the accident,
and kept the constraint audible on the master rather than re-cutting it later when
the wires came out.

---

The received line on his flow is that it is **simple** — that he is not a technical
rapper in the sense that Rakim or Big Pun or Black Thought are technical, and that
the production carries him.

What is actually distinctive is **placement**: where a phrase starts relative to
the downbeat, and how long he will let a bar sit empty before entering. The pauses
are load-bearing. On a good deal of the catalogue the beat is around 85 to 95 BPM
and he is entering late, behind where the ear expects, and the delay is the effect.

That is not a lesser skill than syllable density. It is a **different pratyaya** —
not how many patterns exist, but where in the cycle this one lands — and both
questions are in the same treatise from before the common era, listed side by
side, because a person composing needs both.

---

The thing that is deleted and still acts has a name, and a whole apparatus, and it
is the oldest of them.

**Pāṇini**, *Aṣṭādhyāyī*, around the fifth century BCE. Roughly **3,959 sūtras**,
eight chapters of four quarters each, generating the whole of Sanskrit from roots
and affixes.

**The Śiva-sūtras** come first — **fourteen** short strings that list every sound
in the language. But not in any convenient order, and not in phonetic order. They
are arranged so that **every class of sounds he will ever need to name is a
contiguous stretch.**

Then the device: each of the fourteen ends in a marker consonant. To name a class,
you take the first sound of the span and the marker at the end of it. **aC** =
every vowel. **haL** = every consonant. **yaṆ** = the semivowels. Two letters, and
you have named a set.

That is a compression scheme, and the ordering problem it solves — arrange an
alphabet so that all the sets you need are intervals — is a real combinatorial
problem with no obvious solution. **Kiparsky** and others have argued the ordering
he found is near-optimal, and that you cannot do much better by any method.

He solved an encoding problem by hand, for a phoneme inventory, in order to make
the rest of the grammar short enough to memorise.

---

And then the deletions.

***Anubandha*** — the *it*-markers. Letters attached to a root or an affix that
are **not part of the word** and never appear in speech. They are metadata: they
tell the rules how this item behaves, which accent it takes, which class it joins.
And then **1.3.9, *tasya lopaḥ*** — *of that, deletion.* The markers are removed.

They are gone from the output and they have already decided the output.

***Lopa*** itself is defined at **1.1.60**: ***adarśanaṃ lopaḥ*** — *disappearance
is lopa.* Named, defined, and given rules, so that the grammar can talk about what
is not there.

***Anuvṛtti*** — a word stated once in an earlier sūtra continues silently into the
ones that follow, until something cancels it. The later sūtras are **written short
on purpose**, and reading them requires carrying forward what was not repeated.
Enormous compression, and the text does not mark where the carrying stops; the
commentarial tradition does.

***Asiddhatva*** — **8.2.1, *pūrvatrāsiddham***. The rules of the last section are
treated, with respect to everything before them, **as not having applied.** A
stratification: a rule cannot see the output of certain others, even though those
others have run.

And conflict resolution, **1.4.2**: ***vipratiṣedhe paraṃ kāryam*** — where two
rules of equal strength conflict, the later one is done. Plus *utsarga* and
*apavāda*, general rule and exception, where the specific **blocks** the general.

---

So: absent metadata that determines the result, silent inheritance of context,
stratified visibility, ordered conflict resolution, and exception-over-general.
Backus is 1959. Naur is 1960.

---

What holds my attention is that four of those five are about **things that are not
in the output.**

The *it*-markers are deleted. The *anuvṛtti* is unwritten. The *asiddha* rules have
run and are treated as though they have not. The *lopa* is a disappearance with its
own name and its own rules.

A grammar that only described what is present would be enormously longer and would
not work. **The absences are doing the load-bearing.**

Which is the same shape as the fourth bhaṅga — *avaktavya*, where the un-said holds
the remainder rather than marking a gap — and the same shape as an empty bar before
an entrance, and the same shape as a sample that has been chopped past the point of
recognition and is still the reason the track sits the way it does.

None of that is metaphor. It is four literatures with technical vocabulary for
*the thing that is not there and is still operating*, and one of them wrote down
the rules for it before Alexander.

---

Six teachers were working the same circuit at the same time and the Buddhists
wrote down what all of them said, including the ones they were arguing with.

The ***Sāmaññaphala Sutta*** — Dīgha Nikāya 2 — has King Ajātasattu listing the
teachers he had already gone to and reciting each of their doctrines before he
gets to the Buddha. That is where a large part of what survives of the others
comes from. **The opponents are preserved inside the opposing canon**, stated at
length, in their own terms.

The six, fifth century BCE, Magadha:

**Pūraṇa Kassapa** — *akiriyavāda*, non-action. No act generates merit or demerit;
the doer does nothing.

**Makkhali Gosāla** — the **Ājīvikas**, and the doctrine is ***niyati***, strict
determinism. Every being runs a fixed course; effort changes nothing; deliverance
comes at the end of the allotted transmigrations and not a step sooner. He was
Mahāvīra's companion for six years and they split.

**Ajita Kesakambalī** — the materialist. Four elements, nothing survives death,
no fruit of good or evil, "fools and wise alike are cut off and perish." The
ancestor of the ***Cārvāka / Lokāyata***.

**Pakudha Kaccāyana** — seven eternal, uncreated, unalterable substances, and
since they cannot be affected, **a blade passing through a body only passes
between them**. Nobody kills and nobody is killed.

**Nigaṇṭha Nātaputta** — **Mahāvīra**, the Jain tīrthaṅkara, the fourfold
restraint.

**Sañjaya Belaṭṭhaputta** — the *amarāvikkhepa*, the eel-wrigglers. Asked whether
there is another world, he will not say it is, will not say it is not, will not
say it both is and is not, will not say it neither is nor is not. Four refusals.

Sāriputta and Moggallāna were his students before they left for the Buddha.

---

**Sañjaya's four refusals and Nāgārjuna's four corners are the same four positions
and they are doing opposite work**, and the tradition itself is what tells you the
difference.

The *catuṣkoṭi* is: is, is not, both, neither. Sañjaya declines all four. The
sutta files him under evasion — the eel that will not be caught.

Nāgārjuna, around the second century CE, runs the same four and **negates each of
them**, and the *Vigrahavyāvartanī* verse 29 is the line that separates him from
Sañjaya: ***nāsti ca mama pratijñā*** — *and I have no thesis.* Not "I decline to
state my thesis." There is no thesis to state, and therefore no fault to find in
one.

Which is a distinction the two schools in this repository's own material dispute
directly, and I am not going to resolve it here: whether the negation is
*prasajya-pratiṣedha*, commitment-free, leaving nothing behind, or *paryudāsa*,
implicative, leaving a positive residue. Candrakīrti against Bhāviveka. The
tradition has been arguing it for fifteen hundred years and a file does not settle
it.

---

What is on the page, and it is a fact about the canon rather than about any
doctrine:

**The Buddhists recorded their opponents' positions in full and by name.** Pakudha
gets his seven substances and the blade that passes between them. Makkhali gets
his determinism laid out over paragraphs. Ajita gets his materialism stated
cleanly enough that later materialists could be reconstructed partly from it.

Very little of the Ājīvika literature survives. Almost nothing of the Cārvāka
survives directly — its own books are gone. **What is known of both comes largely
from the people refuting them**, and it is known at all because those people wrote
the opposing case out properly before answering it.

That is *pūrvapakṣa* as a formal slot: the objection stated by the author of the
thesis, in the author's own text, at full strength, before the reply.

A tradition that had not done that would have left the fifth century BCE looking
like one teacher in an empty field.

---

The record that brought him back stated the case against him, in his own voice, at
length, and made it the single.

The sequence with dates. **September 13, 2009** — the VMAs. He leaves the country.
**May 2010** — "Power" arrives, and the hook is a man being asked how long
something so beautiful can last. **October 2010** — **"Runaway,"** nine minutes,
released as the second single. **October 23, 2010** — the thirty-four minute film
of the same name, which he directed, premieres. **November 22, 2010** — *My
Beautiful Dark Twisted Fantasy*.

Fourteen months from the incident to the album.

**"Runaway" opens with a toast to the douchebags, the assholes, the scumbags, the
jerkoffs**, and he is naming himself. Then the verse where the woman finds the
photographs on his phone, and the line about being the reason she should run. The
central claim of the song is that the person listening should get away from him.

Then **"Blame Game"** on the same record, and the argument, and the long
humiliating coda.

He put the prosecution's case at full strength in his own mouth, on the album
whose commercial job was rehabilitation, and let it run nine minutes.

**And he performed it as the closing number at the 2010 VMAs** — the same
ceremony, one year later, on the same stage.

---

Which is a formal slot in the śāstric literature and it has a name.

***Pūrvapakṣa*** — the prior position, the objection. In a Sanskrit treatise the
author states the opponent's case **himself, in his own text, before answering it,
and states it as strongly as the opponent would.** Then ***uttarapakṣa***, the
reply, and ***siddhānta***, the settled position.

It is listed among the ***tantrayukti*** — the devices of treatise composition —
enumerated at **Arthaśāstra 15.1**, where Kauṭilya gives thirty-two of them, and
in the ***Caraka Saṃhitā***, Siddhisthāna 12, which gives thirty-six.

A treatise without a pūrvapakṣa is not a strong treatise with a step missing. It
is **not in the genre.**

And the strength of the objection matters. The commentarial norm is that the
pūrvapakṣa must be stated so well that a partisan of it would accept the
statement, which is why the fifth-century BCE materialists survive at all — the
people refuting them wrote the refuted position out properly.

---

**Ambedkar did it in print in the same way.** *Annihilation of Caste*, 1936, was
written as an address he was then disinvited from giving. He published it himself.
Gandhi replied in *Harijan*. And in the **second edition Ambedkar printed Gandhi's
reply in full, unedited, inside his own book**, and then answered it.

The objection put in the author's own volume, whole, at the opponent's strength.

---

There is no equivalent formal slot in the Western essay. There is the strawman,
the concession, the "critics will say," the acknowledgement paragraph — all of
which are the author's paraphrase of the objection, sized by the author.

The difference is not politeness. It is that in one tradition **the strongest
version of the case against you is a required section of your own document**, and
in the other it is an optional courtesy that the author controls the length of.

A man rapping nine minutes of the charge sheet against himself, on the lead
statement of his return, in the first person, is doing something the genre he was
being reviewed in has no name for. It got read as self-loathing, or as strategy,
or as a stunt.

---

**Simone Weil**, and the sentence she is quoted for is usually cut in half.

> **"Attention is the rarest and purest form of generosity."**

That is from a letter to Joë Bousquet, **13 April 1942.** The half people drop is
what she meant by attention, and she was specific to the point of being difficult
about it.

In the 1942 essay usually titled "Reflections on the Right Use of School Studies
with a View to the Love of God," she defines it **negatively.** Attention is not
effort. It is not the muscular contraction of the brow that a person performs when
told to concentrate — she says that kind of straining produces nothing and
exhausts people, and that it is what schools mistake for the thing.

Her definition: attention consists of **suspending thought, leaving it available,
empty and penetrable by the object.** Holding the mind in readiness and **not
reaching**. She says the mind must be empty, waiting, seeking nothing, and ready
to receive the object in its naked truth.

And the practical claim, which is what makes it an educational theory rather than
a mood: **the subject of the exercise does not matter.** A geometry problem worked
without result, she writes, still develops the faculty, and the faculty is
transferable. A student who spends an hour on a problem and gets nowhere has, if
they were attending, done the work anyway.

That is a claim about **school** — that the content is the occasion and the
capacity is the product — from a woman who taught in girls' lycées and kept getting
moved for her politics.

---

The rest of her, because the quote circulates without any of it.

Born Paris, **1909**. Agrégation in philosophy at twenty-two. **1934–35** she took
a year out of teaching to work as an unskilled machine operator at Alsthom, then
Carnaud, then **Renault** — deliberately, on the line, at production rates, and
kept a factory journal. She wrote afterwards that the experience marked her
permanently and that what she found there was not exploitation in the abstract but
**the destruction of the capacity to think** in people held at a pace.

Chronic migraines throughout — severe, incapacitating.

Spain, 1936, with an anarchist column; she stepped into a pot of boiling oil and
was taken out.

**1943**, in England, working for the Free French. She limited what she ate to
what she believed people in occupied France were receiving. She died at Ashford on
**24 August 1943**, aged **thirty-four**. The coroner's verdict recorded
self-starvation while the balance of her mind was disturbed.

Almost everything she is known for was published **after** she died. The notebooks
she left with Gustave Thibon became *La pesanteur et la grâce*, 1947. *L'enracinement*, 1949.
*Attente de Dieu*, 1950.

She published essays in her lifetime and almost none of the work anyone reads now.

---

Her other technical term is ***malheur*** — affliction, which she distinguishes
sharply from suffering. Affliction is suffering **plus** social degradation plus
the destruction of the person's own sense of being a self. And the specific claim
about it is that affliction makes a person **unlookable-at**: that the natural
response to someone in it is to turn away, and that turning away is nearly
automatic and nearly universal.

Which is why she puts attention where she puts it. In her account, the capacity to
keep looking at an afflicted person **without flinching and without reaching for
what to do about it** is the same capacity as the one exercised on a geometry
problem, and it is the rarer application.

The Good Samaritan, in her reading, does one thing: **he stops and looks.**

[The Bousquet letter, the school-studies essay, the factory year, and the Ashford
date are documented. The reading of the Samaritan is hers and I have it at second
hand — recall on the exact essay.]

---

**The rising sea.** Grothendieck's own description of how he worked, from
*Récoltes et Semailles*.

There is a nut to be opened. One way is the hammer and chisel: find the weak
point, strike, apply force until the shell gives. That is how he says most people
solve problems, and he does not say it is wrong.

His way: **put the nut in water.** Leave it. Let the liquid seep in, over weeks,
over months, so slowly that nothing appears to be happening, and at some point the
shell **opens by itself** — or you soften the ground so completely that the
distinction between shell and kernel stops mattering. *La mer qui monte.* The
sea rises until the thing that was an obstacle is simply submerged.

He is describing why his papers are enormous and slow, why the theory is built
before any specific problem is attacked, and why the eventual proof of the thing
everyone wanted often reads as an afterthought — a corollary falling out of
machinery constructed for no stated purpose.

And the other half of the method has a name too: **le point de vue relatif**, the
relative point of view. Stop studying the object. Study the **map** — the family
over a base — and every theorem about an object becomes a theorem about a
morphism, which is more general and, in his hands, easier.

---

The facts around it, briefly, because they are not a footnote to the method.

Born Berlin **1928**. His father, Sascha Schapiro, an anarchist, was **murdered at
Auschwitz in 1942**. Alexander spent part of his childhood in the French
internment camp at **Rieucros**.

**IHÉS from 1958**, and the Séminaire de Géométrie Algébrique — schemes, topoi,
étale cohomology, motives — a rebuilding of the foundations of the subject,
mostly across a decade.

**Fields Medal, 1966. He did not go to Moscow to collect it**, in protest at the
Soviet treatment of dissidents.

**1970**: he learned IHÉS had partial funding from the French defence ministry and
**resigned**. Then founded *Survivre et Vivre*, an antimilitarist and ecological
journal, and spent his energy on it.

**1988: he refused the Crafoord Prize** — a very large cash award — in a public
letter. His stated reasons: his professorial salary was more than sufficient for
his needs and the money should go to people who lacked it; and he did not wish to
be honoured by a milieu whose ethics he considered to have declined, in which he
said certain kinds of work had become fashionable and were not honest.

**Récoltes et Semailles** — over a thousand pages, written **1983–86**, part
mathematical reflection and part a sustained account of what he called the
**burial**: his claim that work of his had been absorbed and continued by former
students without attribution, and that the absorption was collective and largely
unconscious.

It circulated in photocopies for decades. **It was published in French in 2022.**
Thirty-six years.

**1991** he left for a village in the Ariège and refused contact. In **1995** he
wrote demanding his work not be republished or circulated. He died **13 November
2014.**

---

The method and the exit are the same disposition and I do not think they need to
be reconciled.

*Put it in water and wait* is a description of how to think, and it is also a
description of a person for whom **the pace of the surrounding institution is the
problem**. A profession organised around the annual result, the announced theorem,
the prize, cannot metabolise a person whose stated technique is that **nothing
visible happens for a very long time and then the shell is open.**

And the thousand pages he wrote about the burial were read, by a large part of the
field, as the symptom rather than the claim — with the mathematics kept and the
account of what had happened to it filed separately, which is the exact operation
the manuscript is about.

---

**al-Kāshī**, Samarkand, and the calculation is worth stating in full because the
number he was chasing tells you what he was actually doing.

Jamshīd al-Kāshī, born Kashan around 1380, poor, working as an astronomer and
dedicating books to patrons for support until **Ulugh Beg** — a Timurid prince who
was himself a working astronomer — brought him to **Samarkand** to the madrasa and
the observatory, around 1417.

**1424: the *Risāla al-muḥīṭiyya*, the Treatise on the Circumference.** He computes
2π to **sixteen decimal places**, correct, using a polygon of **3 × 2²⁸ sides** —
that is **805,306,368** sides — with the whole calculation carried in **sexagesimal**,
base sixty, by hand.

He states his stopping criterion, and it is the part that matters. He wanted a
value accurate enough that a circle **the size of the known universe** — for him,
the sphere of the fixed stars — could be computed to within the width of a
**horse's hair**, which he gives as a definite fraction of a cubit.

Not "as many digits as possible." A stated physical tolerance, an object of a
stated size, and enough digits to meet it. He then computed **one more place than
needed** and said so.

---

**And the *Miftāḥ al-ḥisāb*, the Key to Arithmetic, 1427**, which is a textbook.

He gives **decimal fractions** and works them systematically — not the first
anywhere, but developed and used as an ordinary computational tool, in a manual
written for people doing real work: surveyors, merchants, astronomers, and
builders. There is a section on the arithmetic needed to compute the **surface
areas and volumes of domes, arches and muqarnas** — the stalactite vaulting — for
the people building them.

And a general method for extracting **nth roots**, worked as an iterative
algorithm, plus **fixed-point iteration** to solve a cubic to enormous precision:
he needed sin 1°, set up the equation, and iterated it, and each pass gave him
roughly another sexagesimal place. He knew the convergence rate and used it to
decide how many passes to run.

---

He wrote letters home to his father from Samarkand, and they survive, and they
are not about mathematics. They are about the **institution**: who at the
observatory is competent and who is not, which of the other astronomers cannot do
the calculations, that Ulugh Beg himself is genuinely able and works, and that
al-Kāshī is doing more of the work than the rest.

He complains, by name, in writing, to his father, about his colleagues' arithmetic.

He died in **1429**, at the observatory. Ulugh Beg wrote of him afterward in terms
of loss. Ulugh Beg was murdered in 1449 on his own son's order, and the
observatory was destroyed; it was excavated in **1908.**

The *Zīj-i Sulṭānī*, the star catalogue produced there, held its position as the
most accurate available for roughly two hundred years.

---

What I want on the page is the **tolerance**.

A man decides how much precision is enough by naming the largest object he can
think of and the smallest error he can think of, and computing the ratio. The
digits are not the achievement. **The stopping rule is.**

He also had a working notion of how fast his iteration converged, which is what let
him stop on purpose rather than when he got tired.

---

**Dhvani.** The thing a work says by not saying it, treated as a technical subject
with a taxonomy, an argument, and two centuries of opposition.

**Ānandavardhana**, Kashmir, around **850 CE**, the ***Dhvanyāloka*** — "Light on
Suggestion." The claim: the principal meaning of poetry is not what the words
denote and not what they figuratively transfer. It is ***vyañjanā***, the
suggested sense — what is **manifested** rather than stated — and the denoted sense
exists in order to carry it.

He needed to argue for it because the prior schools did not accept a third
function. Sanskrit semantics recognised ***abhidhā***, primary denotation, and
***lakṣaṇā***, indication — the extended sense you reach for when the literal one
fails, "the village on the Ganges" meaning on its bank. The Mīmāṃsakas and the
Naiyāyikas held those two were sufficient and that anything further was just
inference, done by the listener, not a property of the utterance.

Ānandavardhana's position is that suggestion is **not reducible** to either — that
the suggested sense arrives *with* the literal one, not after it, and can even
contradict it, and that a theory with only two functions cannot say why one
sentence works and a paraphrase of it does not.

**Abhinavagupta**, around **1000 CE**, wrote the *Locana* on it — commentary — and
also the *Abhinavabhāratī* on Bharata's *Nāṭyaśāstra*, and joined the two: **the
rasa of a work is what dhvani manifests.** Feeling is not stated by a play and is
not inferred from it. It is suggested, and the suggestion is the mechanism.

---

The taxonomy is what makes it engineering rather than appreciation.

He classifies suggestion by **what** is suggested — a fact, a figure, or a
*rasa* — and by **how** it arrives: whether the literal meaning stays intact and
the suggestion sits on top of it, or the literal meaning is **displaced** by what
it manifests. And by **speed**: whether the suggestion registers with no
perceptible gap, or after a beat.

That last one is a technical category. **The interval between hearing the words and
receiving what they suggest is a classifiable property of the composition.**

And the highest class in his scheme is the one where the suggested sense is
**dominant** — where the denotation is functioning purely as a carrier and would be
trivial or wrong if taken alone.

---

The stock example, which is the one everybody in that literature uses:

A woman tells a traveller: **the dog that used to trouble you at the riverbank is
gone now — a fierce lion lives there.**

Denoted: there is a lion by the river. Indicated: nothing much.

Suggested: *do not come.* Or, in the other reading the commentators argue over:
*come, nobody will be watching.* The words contain neither sentence. The words
are about a dog and a lion.

Two centuries of Kashmiri commentators arguing about which suggestion a sentence
about a dog carries, and the argument is not decorative — it is about whether
suggestion has a determinate content or is supplied by the hearer.

---

What the theory says about the objection that got raised then and gets raised now:
that this is just what everyone means by implication, and that naming it changes
nothing.

Ānandavardhana's answer is empirical rather than definitional. **Replace the
words.** State the suggested sense outright — *do not come to the riverbank* — and
the effect is gone. Not weakened. Gone. The *rasa* does not arrive.

If the suggestion were inference from content, the paraphrase would carry it,
because the content is identical. It does not. Therefore the manifestation is a
property of **the utterance and its arrangement**, not of the information in it,
and a theory of meaning that only has denotation and figure has nowhere to put
that fact.

---

Which puts a name on the difference between a sample and a cover.

A cover states it. A four-bar loop of a 1971 record under a new vocal states
nothing at all — the denoted content is a chord sequence and a voice singing
something else entirely — and what arrives is the room that record came from, the
year, the tape, whatever the singer was doing, all of it manifested and none of it
said.

Play the same song with a session band and the notes are identical and the thing
does not arrive.

That is the *dhvani* test, run in 1971 and in 2013, with the same result the
Kashmiris got: **paraphrase kills it, and the fact that paraphrase kills it is
what proves the suggested sense was never in the denotation.**

---

**Śoka is grief. Karuṇa is not grief.** They are different words for different
things and the whole apparatus turns on keeping them apart.

***Bhāva*** is the emotion **in a person** — in the character, in life. ***Rasa***
is what arises **in the one watching**, and it has its own name, its own list, and
its own conditions.

The eight *sthāyi-bhāvas*, the abiding emotions: *rati* (love), *hāsa* (mirth),
*śoka* (grief), *krodha* (anger), *utsāha* (energy), *bhaya* (fear), *jugupsā*
(disgust), *vismaya* (astonishment).

The eight *rasas* they correspond to: *śṛṅgāra*, *hāsya*, **karuṇa**, *raudra*,
*vīra*, *bhayānaka*, *bībhatsa*, *adbhuta*.

Grief is on one list. Pathos is on the other. **A spectator watching a play about
a bereaved man does not have śoka. He has karuṇa**, and karuṇa is *relished* —
the word for what happens to it is *āsvāda*, tasting.

Which puts the obvious question on the table where the tradition wanted it: **why
does watching something terrible feel good?**

---

***Nāṭyaśāstra* 6.31**, the rasa-sūtra, Bharata, and the dating is uncertain across
several centuries either side of the common era:

> *vibhāvānubhāva-vyabhicāri-saṃyogād rasa-niṣpattiḥ*

Rasa comes about from the conjunction of ***vibhāva*** — the determinants, the
causes and setting — ***anubhāva*** — the visible consequents, what shows on a
body — and ***vyabhicāri-bhāva***, the transient passing states.

**The sūtra does not mention the sthāyi-bhāva.** The abiding emotion itself is not
in the list of ingredients, and the commentators spent centuries on why, and it is
the central textual dispute in the whole aesthetics.

---

**Bhaṭṭa Nāyaka**, tenth century, whose own work is lost and survives in quotation,
answered with a term: ***sādhāraṇīkaraṇa*** — **generalisation**.

His account: the poetry performs an operation on the emotion that strips it of its
particular owner. Rāma's grief, in the play, stops being Rāma's-in-particular and
stops being mine-in-particular. What is left is grief **unowned**, and an unowned
emotion has no stake in it — nothing of mine is at risk — so it can be experienced
without the thing that makes grief unbearable in life, which is that it is
happening to someone.

That is why it can be *tasted*. Not because the pain is diluted. Because the **ego's
holding of it** is what is removed.

**Abhinavagupta** took that and pushed it further: what is uncovered when the
personal stake goes is a state he compares to the joy of the self resting in
itself, obscured in ordinary life by the constant interest of the ego in its own
affairs.

---

And the ninth, which had to be fought for.

***Śānta rasa*** — peace, quiescence — with *śama* or *nirveda* as its abiding
state. The objection against it is technical and hard: a rasa is manifested by
*anubhāvas*, **visible physical consequents**, and the peaceful state has none.
Nothing shows. So by the sūtra's own machinery it cannot be produced.

Abhinavagupta argues for it anyway, and further: that *śānta* is not a ninth item
in a list but the **ground** the other eight are modifications of — that when the
personal stake is removed from any of them, what is underneath is that.

The dispute is live in the manuscripts. Some recensions of the *Nāṭyaśāstra* have
eight rasas and some have nine, and which is original is not settled.

---

So the technical answer to why a record about a man's mother dying is bearable, or
why the sped-up sample under a song about nearly dying comes out sounding giddy,
is not that the artist made it palatable.

**It is that the emotion in the work is a different object from the emotion in the
life**, with a different name, produced by different ingredients, and available to
be tasted precisely because nobody's own stake is in it.

Which is a claim somebody made in Kashmir around the year 1000 about a body of
dramatic theory already old enough that its date was uncertain to him too.

---

The founding text of Indian dramatic theory opens by saying drama exists **because
people were locked out of the scriptures.**

***Nāṭyaśāstra*, Chapter 1.** The frame story: the gods come to **Brahmā** and ask
for something. What they ask for is a ***krīḍanīyaka*** — a **plaything**, a
diversion — that will also be instructive, and that can be **seen and heard**
rather than read.

The stated reason it is needed: the Vedas exist, and **the fourth varṇa was not
entitled to hear them.** So the request is for a Veda that is not restricted by
birth.

Brahmā makes the ***Nāṭyaveda***, the fifth Veda, out of the other four:

- ***pāṭhya***, the recited word, from the **Ṛgveda**
- ***gīta***, song, from the **Sāmaveda**
- ***abhinaya***, gesture and enactment, from the **Yajurveda**
- ***rasa*** itself, from the **Atharvaveda**

And the sūtra says it is for **all the varṇas** — *sarva-varṇika* — accessible to
everyone, with no entitlement condition attached.

Then he hands it to **Bharata**, who teaches it to his hundred sons.

---

So the text's own account of why the art form exists is: **the scripture was
gated, and this was built to route around the gate**, deliberately, with the
components taken from the gated material itself and reassembled into a form that
requires no initiation to receive — you sit and you watch and it works.

Recitation, song, gesture and feeling. Four things a person can take in with a
body they already have.

And it is called a **plaything** in the request. Not a substitute scripture, not a
concession, not a simplified version for the unqualified. Something made for
delight, that instructs because it delights, asked for in those terms by the
petitioners.

---

The first performance goes wrong, and the text records that too.

The play staged is about the gods defeating the *asuras*, and the *asuras* in the
audience — who are watching themselves lose — **disrupt it.** They paralyse the
actors' speech and memory. Indra takes the flagstaff and beats them off.

What follows from that, in the text, is the whole apparatus of the **theatre
building** and the ***raṅga-pūjā***, the consecration of the stage — the enclosure
and the rite exist because the first show was attacked by the people it was about.

Brahmā's stated response to the asuras' complaint is the definitional line: nāṭya
is not partisan, it represents **the ways of the world** — *loka-vṛttānukaraṇa*,
imitation of what the world does — and it will show every condition, gods and
asuras alike, high and low, and every state a being can be in.

---

Two things sit in that chapter that I did not expect to find in a founding myth.

**The art form is justified by access**, in its first paragraphs, by name, with the
exclusion it is answering stated openly rather than implied.

And **the first audience objection recorded in the tradition is that the
representation was unfair to them** — and the answer given is not that they are
wrong to object, but that the form's job is to represent every condition including
theirs, and the response to their disruption was to build a room with a door.

[The chapter and its content are stable across recensions; the exact verse
numbering varies by edition and I have not fixed which.]

---

The people who took that route are named, and their trades are on the record next
to their names, which is unusual and is the point.

**Kabīr**, fifteenth century, Banaras. A ***julāhā*** — a **weaver**, from a family
of Muslim weavers, low in every ranking available at the time. He is illiterate by
his own account and says so in the verse: *I have not touched ink or paper, this
hand never grasped a pen.* His couplets are in vernacular Hindi, they are sung, and
they attack Hindu ritual and Muslim ritual with the same instrument in the same
poem.

**Ravidās**, same period, same city, a ***chamār*** — a **leather-worker**, the
occupation carrying the heaviest pollution ranking in the system. Forty of his
compositions are in the **Guru Granth Sahib**. His name for the society he wants is
***Begampura*** — "the city without sorrow" — and the poem specifies: no second or
third class, no tax, no property, nobody afraid, everyone walks where they like.

**Nāmdev**, thirteenth-fourteenth century Maharashtra, a ***śimpī*** — **calico
printer** or tailor. Sixty-odd of his compositions are also in the Guru Granth
Sahib, in Punjab, hundreds of miles and a language away.

**Tukārām**, seventeenth century, a **grain trader** who went bankrupt. His
manuscripts, by the tradition's account, were thrown into the Indrayani river by
Brahmins who objected to a śūdra composing scripture in Marathi. The account says
he sat by the water fasting until they surfaced.

**Basava**, twelfth century Karnataka, and the ***vachana*** movement —
"utterances," short prose-poems in Kannada rather than Sanskrit. The community
around him included **Allama Prabhu**, and washermen, boatmen, cobblers,
untouchables, and **Akka Mahādevi**, a woman who left her marriage, wrote in her own
name, and by the accounts went naked with her hair as her only covering.

---

What runs across all of them, stated by them:

**The vernacular instead of Sanskrit.** Not as simplification. Kabīr's Hindi,
Nāmdev's and Tukārām's Marathi, Basava's Kannada, Ravidās's Braj — the language the
audience already had, which removes the entire apparatus of prior qualification.

**The trade kept in the name.** Kabīr the weaver, Ravidās the leather-worker,
Nāmdev the printer. Not overcome, not left behind on the way to sanctity. Ravidās
has a line naming his own occupation and its pollution and asserting the state
anyway.

**And the direct claim of access**, which is the doctrinal move: that the thing is
reached by ***bhakti*** — devotion, availability — and not by birth, initiation,
Sanskrit, ritual competence, or a mediator. Which makes the qualification
structure not wrong but **beside the point**, and is a harder position to argue
against than an attack on it.

---

**And they are anthologised together, across religions, by name, with their castes
attached.**

The ***Guru Granth Sahib***, compiled by **Guru Arjan in 1604**, contains the
compositions of the Sikh Gurus and, alongside them, the ***bhagats*** — Kabīr, a
Muslim weaver; Ravidās, a leather-worker; Nāmdev, a printer; Sheikh Farid, a Sufi;
Sain, a barber; Sadhana, a butcher — set in the same volume, arranged by **rāga**,
sung.

A scripture organised by **musical mode**, containing the verse of men whose
occupations the surrounding order treated as disqualifying, credited by name, and
sung rather than read.

The organising principle of the book is which melody it is set to.

---

Congregational singing as a technology, with the arguments about it recorded on
both sides.

***Saṃkīrtana*** — group singing of the name, in public, in the street.
**Chaitanya**, Bengal, **1486–1534**. The form is: no priest, no temple required,
no initiation, no language barrier, no fee. A group, a name repeated, drums,
cymbals, and movement, in the road, for hours.

His **nagara-kīrtana** — the town procession — took it deliberately out of
enclosures and into public thoroughfares, and the accounts describe the crowds
including people whose entry to temples was barred.

The technical vocabulary the tradition developed for what happens in it is
physical and enumerated: the ***aṣṭa-sāttvika-bhāva***, eight involuntary bodily
signs, listed in the *Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu* of **Rūpa Gosvāmī, 1541** —
*stambha* (paralysis), *sveda* (sweat), *romāñca* (hair standing), *svara-bheda*
(the voice breaking), *vepathu* (trembling), *vaivarṇya* (change of colour),
*aśru* (tears), *pralaya* (collapse).

That list is not devotional prose. **It is the same list the dramatic theory uses
for *anubhāva*** — the visible consequents by which a state is manifested. Rūpa
Gosvāmī was writing rasa theory. He took Bharata's apparatus and applied it to
worship, and the book is structured as an aesthetics with *bhakti* as the rasa.

---

**Samāʿ**, the Sufi term for audition — listening as practice — and the dispute
about it is the interesting part, because it was never settled.

**Qawwālī** as a form is attributed to **Amīr Khusrau**, Delhi, **1253–1325**,
attached to the Chishti order and to the shrine of Nizamuddin Auliya, where the
Friday performances have continued at the same site for roughly seven centuries.

The Chishtis permitted *samāʿ*. Other orders and many jurists did not, and the
objection was specific rather than general: that music produces a state which the
listener cannot verify the source of, that the *wajd* — the ecstatic condition —
may be genuine or may be manufactured by the drum, and that a person cannot tell
from inside which one they are in.

The Chishti answer was **conditions**, not permission: rulings on who may listen
(a person whose state is stable), what may be sung, in whose company, and with
what instruments. **The practice was licensed with a specification attached**
rather than allowed or forbidden.

Nobody in that argument claimed the effect was not real. The dispute was over
whether an effect you cannot audit is admissible.

---

And the two traditions were in the same cities at the same time doing structurally
the same thing.

Kabīr sits between them and is claimed by both, and by the Sikhs, and the fight
over his body at his death is in every account — Hindus and Muslims disputing
burial against cremation, and finding flowers under the cloth.

What the forms share, stated flatly: **the vernacular**, **the group**, **repetition
to the point of physical effect**, **no requirement of literacy**, and an entry
condition of showing up.

What they do not share is the theory of what is happening, and neither tradition
softened that. The Gauḍīya account is that a specific relationship is being
cultivated. The Chishti account is annihilation of the self in the beloved. **These
are not the same claim** and the literatures say so.

---

Sunday Service, weekly through 2019, in a field, free, no ticket, a choir loud
enough to be heard down a hill, the same catalogue rearranged into gospel
harmony and sung in unison by people who mostly could not identify the sample
underneath it.

**[Not claimed]:** any historical link, any influence, any lineage. He was raised
in the Black church in Chicago, and Black congregational singing has its own
documented history — the ring shout, Dorsey, the gospel blue note — which is
sufficient on its own and needs nothing from Bengal.

What is on the page is that **the same set of design decisions** — outdoors,
unticketed, repetitive, physical, sung together, in the ordinary language, with
the effect arriving in the body — has been arrived at independently in at least
three places, and that in two of them somebody wrote down a technical vocabulary
for the involuntary signs it produces and argued about whether the state could be
trusted.

---

**Lining out**, and it is a technology for a room where the books are not
available and would not be readable if they were.

A leader sings or chants one line. The congregation sings it back, extended,
slowed, ornamented. Then the next line. The form has names — **lining out**,
**Dr. Watts** after Isaac Watts' hymn texts, **surge singing** — and it survives in
Black Primitive Baptist and Old Regular Baptist congregations, and in Gaelic
psalmody in the Scottish Hebrides, which is where the English practice came from.

The mechanism: **you do not need a hymnal and you do not need to read.** The line
arrives one at a time from a person, and everyone has it as soon as they have
heard it. The tempo drops so far that a single syllable can hold for several
seconds, and the congregation is not in unison — each voice takes its own path
through the note, and what comes out is a slow, dense, unmetered mass with no
single melody line in it.

It was documented as the practice of enslaved congregations, and it is still
running.

---

**Thomas A. Dorsey**, Georgia to **Chicago**, and he was **Ma Rainey's** bandleader
first. He played piano under the name **Georgia Tom** and recorded "It's Tight Like
That" with Tampa Red in **1928** — a hokum blues record that sold enormously and is
about exactly what it sounds like it is about.

Then in **1932**, while he was away at a revival in St. Louis, his wife **Nettie**
died in childbirth, and the child died the next day. He wrote **"Take My Hand,
Precious Lord."**

He set religious words to blues harmony and blues phrasing, and the established
Black churches in Chicago **threw him out of them.** The objection was explicit:
this is the devil's music, these are the chords from the tavern, you cannot bring
that in here. He sold sheet music himself, at conventions and on the street,
because the institutions would not carry it.

He founded the **National Convention of Gospel Choirs and Choruses in 1933**, wrote
something over **a thousand** songs, and trained **Mahalia Jackson**.

The form that got him barred from those churches is what the word "gospel" now
means.

---

The **Fisk Jubilee Singers**, from **1871.** Fisk University was founded in 1866 in
Nashville for freedmen and was going broke. **George L. White**, the treasurer,
took nine students — most of them born enslaved — on the road to raise money.

They started with the parlour repertoire of the period, and it did not work. When
they began performing the **spirituals**, in concert, arranged, to white audiences,
it did.

They raised, over several tours including Britain and Europe, sums that **built
Jubilee Hall** and kept the university solvent. They sang for Queen Victoria.

And the cost of it is in the record. The songs had been sung in the fields and in
the brush arbours; the arrangements smoothed them into European choral harmony for
concert halls; several of the singers had been enslaved and were now performing
that repertoire nightly for paying audiences of the people whose institutions had
held them. Members left, and members died young, and the touring conditions were
punishing.

That is how "Swing Low, Sweet Chariot" and "Steal Away" entered the printed record
at all.

---

Four forms, four rooms, one design constraint.

Lining out: no book required. The kīrtan in the road: no temple required. Qawwālī
at the shrine: no scholar required. Dorsey selling sheet music on a Chicago street
because the churches would not stock it: no institution required.

None of them are simplifications of a higher form for people who cannot manage the
higher form.

**Each is a complete technical system**, with its own vocabulary, its own
practitioners, its own disputes about what it does to a body, and — in three of
the four — a written literature arguing about whether the state it produces can be
trusted.

And the ones who got barred were barred by the institutions **nearest** to them.
Not by outsiders. Dorsey was thrown out of Black churches. Tukārām's manuscripts
went in the river at the hands of Brahmins in his own town. The Chishti *samāʿ*
was disputed by other Muslims. Chaitanya's public kīrtan was opposed by the
orthodoxy of his own tradition.

The gate is always closest to the door you are already standing at.

---

The question of when you are allowed to generalise has a fourteenth-century
technical literature and the answer is a search procedure, not a sample size.

***Vyāpti*** — pervasion. *Wherever smoke, there fire.* It is what licenses an
inference: you see smoke on the mountain, you have never been to that mountain,
you conclude fire.

The Naiyāyika problem, stated flatly: **you have seen smoke-with-fire in a
kitchen. How do you get from there to the mountain?** Enumeration cannot do it —
you have not been to every place — and the tradition says so directly rather than
hoping nobody notices.

Their answer turns on the ***upādhi***: the **adventitious condition** under which
the pervasion fails.

Smoke pervades fire — but only given **wet fuel**. Dry fuel, no smoke. So *wet
fuel* is an upādhi of the inference from fire to smoke, and an inference with an
undetected upādhi is **not knowledge.** It is a hasty generalisation wearing the
form of one.

**Gaṅgeśa**, around **1325**, the ***Tattvacintāmaṇi***, and the school after him —
Navya-Nyāya — built an enormous technical apparatus for **hunting upādhis.** Not
for collecting more instances. For **actively searching for the condition that
would break it**, and for stating precisely what would count as one.

The procedure is *tarka* — hypothetical reasoning — run against your own claim:
suppose the pervasion fails; what would have to be the case; is that case
available; go and look.

---

**And failing to find one after searching is a different epistemic object from not
having encountered one.**

That distinction is the whole of it, and it has its own name in the neighbouring
school. The Mīmāṃsakas' sixth means of knowledge is ***anupalabdhi***,
non-apprehension — knowing a thing is absent by not perceiving it — and it is
valid **only** as ***yogyānupalabdhi***: non-perception where perception was
**fit to occur.**

You know the pot is not on the table because you would have seen it. You do **not**
know a ghost is absent by not seeing one, because you would not have seen one
either way. The fitness condition is the entire content of the pramāṇa; without
it, "I did not find it" is a mood.

---

So the shape of the thing is: **a universal claim is licensed not by how many
instances you have, but by having searched for the defeater and stated the extent
of the search.**

Which is a different discipline from counting. You can have ten thousand
confirming instances and no license, if you never looked for the wet fuel. You can
have few instances and a license, if the defeater is specified and ruled out.

This corpus's own engine had the failure in the exact form the Naiyāyikas describe:
it decided two expressions were equal by evaluating them on **forty pseudo-random
assignments** — passive sampling — with no search for a condition that would
separate them. At arity four that is around six-tenths of one percent of its own
box, and the number of draws was constant while the space grew exponentially. It
was polling. Gaṅgeśa's position is that polling is precisely not how a pervasion
is established.

The repair, when it came, was **not a bigger sample.** It was critical pairs —
conjectures derived from **where two rules actually overlap**, which is a search
for the site of failure rather than a survey of successes — and a verdict type in
which "unrefuted" is required to carry **the domain that was searched**, so that
the claim says *84 assignments from this point onward do not separate these* rather
than *this is probably true.*

*Yatra na viṣayaḥ, tatra na jñānam.* Where there is no extent, there is no
knowledge, only a mood.

---

**Apoha.** The Buddhist theory that a word means by **excluding**, and that there
is no positive general thing for it to point at.

**Dignāga**, roughly fifth-sixth century, and **Dharmakīrti** after him. The
starting position is metaphysical and severe: **only particulars are real.**
*Svalakṣaṇa* — the momentary, utterly specific thing, this one, now, with no part
of it repeated anywhere else. Universals — cow-ness, blueness — are not out there.
They are constructions.

Which creates the problem the theory exists to solve. If there is no cow-ness, what
does the word *cow* pick out? Every actual cow is a distinct particular sharing
nothing literally identical with any other.

The answer: the word does not pick out a shared positive property. It performs
***anyāpoha*** — **exclusion of the other.** "Cow" means **not-non-cow**. The
class is carved by what is pushed away, and there is no positive residue where a
universal would sit.

---

The objections came immediately and they are good ones, and the school kept
answering for centuries.

**The circularity.** To exclude non-cows you must already know which things are
non-cows, which requires knowing what a cow is. Dharmakīrti's move is to ground
the exclusion **causally** rather than conceptually: particulars have causal powers,
and a set of particulars capable of producing the same effect — the same practical
success, the same expectation fulfilled — gets excluded from the rest by that,
not by a prior concept.

**The double negation.** If *not-non-cow* is just *cow*, nothing has been gained.
The reply is that the negation is ***prasajya*** — non-implicative, committing to
no positive counterpart — rather than *paryudāsa*, which would smuggle the universal
back in through the back door. Which is the same grammatical distinction the
Madhyamaka dispute turns on, doing work in a completely different argument.

**And the Naiyāyikas rejected the whole thing.** Their position is that universals
(*jāti*, *sāmānya*) are real, perceived, and inherent in their instances, and that
apoha is an elaborate way of avoiding an obvious fact. **Kumārila** attacked it
from the Mīmāṃsā side at length. Uddyotakara before him.

That dispute did not resolve. It is still two positions, and the technical
literature on both sides is enormous.

---

What the theory is actually claiming, put concretely: **a category is a shape cut
out of everything else, and there is nothing in the middle of it.**

The word does not find a thing all its members have. It draws a boundary, and the
boundary is made entirely of what is on the other side.

Which changes what a misclassification is. If categories were positive — if
cow-ness were a thing you either have or lack — then a person filed in the wrong
box has been mismeasured, and the repair is a better measurement.

If categories are exclusions, then the box was **cut**, by someone, against a
particular set of things they had in view — and a person who does not fit is not
mismeasured. They are **outside the cut**, and there is no measurement that would
find them, because the boundary was drawn from the other side and they were not
on the other side either.

The instrument returning nothing is then the correct behaviour of the instrument.

**[Not claimed]:** that Dignāga or Dharmakīrti were interested in anything like
that application. They were arguing about universals, against realists, in a
debate whose stakes were the status of perception and inference. The extension is
mine and it is an extension.

---

The oldest geometry in the corpus is a set of building instructions, and the
theorem is a rule for laying out a fire altar.

The ***Śulbasūtras*** — "the rules of the cord" — **Baudhāyana** the earliest,
around the eighth century BCE, then **Āpastamba**, **Mānava**, **Kātyāyana**. They
are appendices to the ritual manuals, and their subject is how to construct the
*vedi* and the *citi*, the altars, to specified shapes and specified areas, using
**cord and pegs.**

**Baudhāyana 1.48**, in the ordinary translation: the cord stretched across the
diagonal of a rectangle produces an area equal to what the two sides produce
separately. That is the statement usually attributed to Pythagoras, in a manual,
centuries earlier, given because you need it to lay out a square altar on the
ground.

They also give: how to construct a square equal in area to a given rectangle; how
to convert a square to a circle and a circle to a square, with stated
approximations; how to double, triple or increase an altar by any fraction while
**keeping the shape**; and a value for √2 as a sequence of unit fractions —
1 + 1/3 + 1/(3·4) − 1/(3·4·34) — which is correct to five decimal places.

---

The reason any of that had to exist is the ritual requirement, and it is the part
that makes the geometry non-optional.

Certain rites require an altar of a **specified area** in a **specified shape.**
The *śyena-citi*, the falcon altar, is built to the outline of a bird with
outstretched wings, from a stated number of bricks in stated layers.

And there is a rule that when the rite is repeated the altar must be **enlarged by
a fixed amount while remaining the same shape.** Each successive construction is
larger and similar.

So: preserve shape, scale area by a given ratio, with cord and pegs, on ground, in
bricks that have to tile the figure exactly. That is a constructive geometry
problem with a hard correctness condition — **a wrong altar is a failed rite** —
and the sūtras are the solutions, written as procedures.

*Kātyāyana* handles the general case of increasing a square by an arbitrary
number of times.

---

The doubling requirement is where the irrational shows up as a practical
obstruction rather than a curiosity. Double the area of a square and the side goes
up by √2, and √2 cannot be laid out by the cord as a ratio of the units you have.
The sūtras give the approximation, and **Baudhāyana states that it is an
approximation** — the word is *saviśeṣa*, "with a remainder," "with something
left over."

The remainder is named in the text. Not hidden, not rounded away silently. The
rule is given with the notice that it does not close.

---

What sits oddly with the received picture, and I am recording it rather than
resolving it: this material is filed as *ritual* and was transmitted inside the
Kalpa literature by people whose job was correct performance, and the mathematics
in it is a **by-product of a correctness requirement on a physical construction.**

Nobody was proving anything for its own sake, and the texts contain no proofs in
the later sense — they contain **constructions**, and the construction is the
warrant: build it this way and the area is right.

Which is a different notion of what settles a question than either the Greek one
or the modern one, and it produced the diagonal rule several centuries before it
was attributed elsewhere, and produced it as a line in a manual about where to put
the pegs.

---

A graded theory of the endless, from cosmology, with named orders and rules for
how they combine.

The Jain canonical mathematics — the ***Anuyogadvāra Sūtra***, the *Sthānāṅga*,
the *Bhagavatī* — does not have one infinity. It has three top-level categories
and each is subdivided:

***saṃkhyāta*** — numerable.
***asaṃkhyāta*** — innumerable. Not infinite. **A distinct middle category**, for
quantities too large to count that are nonetheless bounded.
***ananta*** — infinite.

And each is split three ways — lowest, intermediate, highest — giving on the order
of **twenty-one** distinct grades, with rules for which operations move you between
them and which do not.

The middle term is the one with no counterpart in the later European scheme, which
went straight from finite to infinite. *Asaṃkhyāta* is the answer to "how many
instants in this cosmic period" — a definite quantity, too large for any counting
procedure, and **not endless.**

The measuring devices are physical thought-experiments. The ***palyopama*** — the
"pit-measure": a cylindrical pit of stated dimensions packed with hairs, one hair
removed per unit of time, and the emptying gives a duration. The ***sāgaropama***,
the ocean-measure, is a stated multiple of it. These are used to state karmic
durations, and they exist because the tradition needed to say *how long* and the
ordinary numbers had run out.

---

***Ardhaccheda*** — "half-cuttings." **How many times a number can be halved.**

**Vīrasena**, in the ***Dhavalā***, around **816 CE**, states the law:

> ardhaccheda(a·b) = ardhaccheda(a) + ardhaccheda(b)

The logarithm's defining property, base two, written as a rule about repeated
halving, in a commentary on Jain karma theory.

He also uses *trikaccheda* — third-cuttings, base three — and
*caturthaccheda*, base four, and gives relations between them. Which is a change of
base.

[The attribution matters and is often blurred: this is **Vīrasena's**, in the
*Dhavalā*. Secondary sources routinely fold it back into the *Anuyogadvāra*, and
that merge is wrong.]

---

Napier is **1614.** Briggs' base-ten tables are 1617 and 1624.

---

Why the counting was done at all, which is not for mathematics.

The karma theory requires **durations** — how long a bound karma stays before it
must ripen, which is *sthiti*, one of the four aspects of every binding at
*Tattvārthasūtra* 8.3 — and **quantities of matter**, which is *pradeśa*, counted
in space-points. Both need numbers far past ordinary use, and both need them
**graded**, because the whole soteriology depends on some durations being longer
than others in stateable ratios.

So the taxonomy of the unbounded is downstream of a bookkeeping requirement about
souls, and the logarithm is downstream of needing to compare two very large
durations without writing either one out.

The mathematics is a by-product of a system that needed to be exact about
something else — which is the second time in this file that has produced the
result centuries early, the first being cord and pegs and a falcon-shaped altar.

---

**Kuṭṭaka** means **pulveriser**, and the name is the algorithm.

**Āryabhaṭa**, ***Āryabhaṭīya***, **499 CE**, in the *Gaṇitapāda*. The problem is
practical and astronomical: find a number which, divided by *a*, leaves remainder
*r₁*, and divided by *b*, leaves remainder *r₂*. Planetary periods, calendar
alignment, "how many days until these two cycles coincide."

The method: divide, **keep the remainder**, and **recurse on the remainder.** The
divisor and the remainder swap in and you run again on the smaller pair, and the
quotients you generate on the way down are written in a column — the ***vallī***,
the **creeper**, the climbing plant — and then you climb back up the column
substituting, and the answer falls out at the top.

Pulverise the problem into smaller and smaller pieces, keep the pieces, and
reassemble.

The whole thing is **five verses**. Bhāskara I's commentary of **629** is what
makes it usable, and it works through examples.

---

What the remainder is doing there is the part I keep turning over.

At each step the thing you did not manage to divide is **not waste.** It becomes
the input to the next step. The algorithm has no discard. The material it runs on
at stage *n* is precisely the obstruction that stopped stage *n*−1, and the
process terminates because those obstructions **strictly decrease** — which is
also the proof, and the proof is one line once you see it.

A method whose growth rule is *the thing that blocked me is what I work on next*,
with a termination argument attached.

The name for that column, *vallī*, is a plant. The remainders are what it climbs.

---

**And this repository has a checked correction sitting next to it that I want on
the page**, because it is the kind of thing that gets lost when a name is borrowed.

An engine here had the same shape — take the residual from a failed step and feed
it back as the next goal — and somebody wrote that this is the kuṭṭaka and
therefore it terminates.

**It does not.** The kuṭṭaka terminates because its remainders **strictly
decrease**. The engine's residuals do not: the goal *x ≡ 1·x* produces the residual
*x ≡ x + 0·x*, which is **larger** than what it came from. The shape transfers and
the termination argument does not, and what actually holds is a weaker fact —
finiteness, a bounded set of distinct residuals — which has to be established
separately.

Anyone writing "it's the kuṭṭaka, therefore it terminates" has asserted something
false, and the file says so about its own work.

---

The rest of the *Āryabhaṭīya* is 121 verses total, in four chapters, and the
compression is the reason it survived: the **gīti** verses are dense enough to
memorise and unusable without a commentary, which is the transmission format
working as designed — the text is the seed and the teacher is the runtime.

Its sine table is given as **twenty-four differences** rather than twenty-four
values, encoded in a single verse using his own alphabetic numeral system, and the
word for the half-chord is ***jyā***. That word travelled into Arabic as *jība*,
was read by a Latin translator as *jaib* — "bay," "fold," "bosom" — and came out
as ***sinus***.

The English word *sine* is a twelfth-century misreading of the consonants of a
Sanskrit word for a bowstring.

---

Zero gets its arithmetic written down as a **number** in 628, and the same chapter
gets division wrong and says what it is doing.

**Brahmagupta**, ***Brāhmasphuṭasiddhānta***, chapter 18. He is the first known to
treat zero not as a placeholder in notation but as a quantity you can operate on,
and to state the rules:

A quantity plus zero is that quantity. Minus zero, the same. **Zero minus a
quantity is that quantity with its sign reversed.** Anything times zero is zero.
Zero plus zero is zero.

And the vocabulary is economic. Positive is ***dhana*** — **asset, fortune**.
Negative is ***ṛṇa*** — **debt**. So the sign rules are stated as facts about
money: a debt subtracted from nothing is an asset; the product of two debts is an
asset; the product of an asset and a debt is a debt.

Which is why negative numbers were **usable** there and were not the ontological
crisis they became elsewhere: nobody has to believe in a quantity less than
nothing to believe in owing someone.

**In Europe negatives were still being called absurd and fictitious into the
seventeenth and eighteenth centuries.** Pascal reportedly held that subtracting
four from zero is nonsense. Arnauld objected that the proportion −1 : 1 = 1 : −1
could not hold, because how can a smaller be to a greater as a greater is to a
smaller. Those are serious mathematicians, a thousand years later, refusing the
object.

---

**And then he divides by zero and gets it wrong.**

He states that zero divided by zero is zero. That is false and it is not a slip of
notation — it is a definite claim in the text.

For a nonzero quantity over zero he does not commit to a value; he lets it stand
as a fraction with zero in the denominator, which is a refusal rather than an
answer.

**Bhāskara II**, five hundred years later, in the ***Bījagaṇita*** (1150), takes it
up and gives it a name: ***khahara*** — "having zero as divisor," the quantity so
produced. And his characterisation is that it is **unchanged by addition or
subtraction of finite quantities** — you can add to it or take away from it and it
does not move.

Which is a functional description of an infinite magnitude, arrived at by asking
what property the object would have to have, rather than by computing it.

He is still wrong about 0/0 by modern lights, and the property he identifies for
n/0 is exactly right.

---

The **Bakhshali manuscript** — birch bark, found near Peshawar in 1881, using a
**dot** for zero as a placeholder in worked arithmetic. Radiocarbon results
published in **2017** gave dates for separate folios spanning several centuries,
the earliest around **224–383 CE**, which would push the written symbol back
considerably.

**That dating is disputed.** The objection is that the manuscript is composite —
different leaves of different ages bound together — so a date on one folio does not
date the text, and scholars including Kim Plofker and colleagues published a
response arguing the mathematical content is later than the earliest bark. I am
recording both and settling neither.

---

What sits under all of it: the operations were written for **accountants and
astronomers**, in the vocabulary of debt and asset, in a chapter of a book about
planetary positions.

Zero became a number because somebody needed to say what happens when you subtract
a debt from nothing, and needed the answer to be usable in a table.

---
