**Amīr Khusrau**, Delhi, **1253–1325**, and the thing he did that nobody had a
word for is switching language **inside the poem**.

His ghazal beginning *zihāl-e-miskīn makun taghāful* alternates **line by line**
between **Persian** and **Braj Bhāṣā** — the court language and the language of the
street, in the same poem, in alternating hemistichs, and the two halves complete
each other's sense so neither can be removed.

That is not a Persian poem with local flavour. It is a form built out of the fact
that his audience lived in two languages at once, and it only works for people who
have both.

He wrote a great deal in **Persian** — five *dīvāns*, the *khamsa* answering
Nizami, the *Nuh Sipihr* — and also in **Hindavi**, the early common tongue that
became Hindi and Urdu, and the Hindavi material is riddles (***paheliyān***),
*dohas*, wedding songs and children's rhymes. Some of it is still recited by people
who do not know whose it is.

He served **seven sultans** across the Delhi Sultanate without losing his position
or his life, which given the period is its own achievement.

---

The musical attributions, stated with their status, because they are repeated as
fact constantly.

Tradition credits him with inventing the **sitar** and the **tabla**, with
originating ***qawwālī***, ***khayāl***, and ***tarāna***, and with creating
numerous rāgas by fusing Persian and Indian modes.

**The instrument attributions are almost certainly legendary.** The sitar and tabla
in their present forms are documented much later, around the eighteenth century,
and no contemporary source has him building either. The qawwālī and *tarāna*
attributions are traditional and are treated as plausible-but-unverified by
musicologists. What is solidly his is the **poetry**, and the **repertoire** sung at
the shrine descends from him by continuous transmission.

[I am marking the whole musical attribution set as tradition rather than record.
The distinction gets flattened in most retellings, and the flattening is what makes
the claim easy to dismiss along with everything else.]

---

**Nizamuddin Auliya** died in **April 1325.** Khusrau was away; the accounts have
him returning, tearing his clothes, and refusing to leave the grave.

He died **six months later**, the same year.

He is buried in the same complex, **below his teacher's tomb**, and the qawwāls
have sung there on Thursday evenings, at that site, since. The couplet they sing
for him — *gorī sove sej par* — is in Hindavi.

---

Seven hundred years, one courtyard, a continuous repertoire, and a body of verse
that had to be built out of two languages because the poet's audience was standing
in both.

The alternating-line ghazal has a technical requirement that is worth stating: the
Persian line and the Braj line are metrically compatible. He is running two
prosodic systems — **quantitative Persian metre** and Indian syllabic-durational
metre — against each other, and the poem scans in both.

That is the same problem as fitting a sung phrase from one record over a rhythm
from another, and the solution is the same: find where the two systems agree, and
build in the overlap.

---

The Kerala derivation, laid out, because this file has said the *Yuktibhāṣā*
contains proofs and has never shown one.

**The problem**: the arc of a circle in terms of the tangent. **The obstacle**: no
notion of a limit as a defined object.

**What the *Yuktibhāṣā* actually does**, around 1530, in Malayalam prose:

Take a quarter arc. Divide the side of the circumscribed square into ***n*** equal
parts. Each little segment of the side subtends a small piece of the arc at the
centre. Compute, geometrically, the ratio between the segment on the tangent and
the arc-piece it subtends, using similar triangles — this is where the *r²/(r² +
x²)* factor comes from, and it is derived from the figure, not assumed.

Sum over all *n* pieces. That gives a finite sum for the arc.

Then **let *n* grow**, and the sum turns into an infinite series. The expansion of
the ratio into a geometric series is done term by term, and the series is summed
term by term.

**And the step that decides everything is the sum of powers.** To get the answer
you need, for each *k*,

    1^k + 2^k + 3^k + … + n^k   for large n

and specifically you need it accurate enough to know that the **sum behaves like
n^(k+1)/(k+1)** when *n* is large, with everything else negligible.

That is the ***saṅkalita*** — the "heaping." The text derives it, for general *k*,
by an inductive argument on the earlier cases: the sum of the first powers gives
the second, the sum of the sums gives the third, and so on up. The lower-order
terms are shown to be **too small to matter at large *n***, and the text says why
rather than dropping them silently.

Term-by-term integration of x^k, in prose, in a vernacular, obtained by
successive summation and an explicit argument about which parts vanish.

---

**The correction terms**, which are the part the European restatement never had.

The bare series for π/4 is unusable — you need on the order of 10⁵ terms for five
decimals. So the text gives, after truncating at *n* terms, a **rational function
of *n*** to add back, and gives **successively better ones**, and the improvement
from each is stated.

The forms, in the usual modern transcription:

    first:    1/(4n)
    second:   n / (4n² + 1)
    third:    (n² + 1) / (4n³ + 5n)

Those are not guesses. Each is derived, and the third is good enough that a few
dozen terms land eleven correct decimal places, which is where **3.14159265359**
comes from.

**A truncated series plus a quantified remainder is an algorithm. A truncated
series alone is a curiosity.** Gregory and Leibniz have the series. The
end-corrections are not in them.

---

And separately, **Nārāyaṇa Paṇḍita**, ***Gaṇita Kaumudī***, **1356** — a
combinatorics text with rules for **permutations with repetition**, for
**partitions**, for figurate numbers and higher-order sums (*saṅkalita* again),
and a systematic treatment of **magic squares** including a general construction
method.

It also contains a problem about a cow producing a calf every year, each calf
producing from its fourth year, asked as: **how many cattle in twenty years** — a
linear recurrence, posed as animal husbandry, with the sequence written out.

---

The Kerala texts were **not lost.** They were in Malayalam and in manuscript, in a
region, read by the people who used them for calendrical work, for centuries.

Whish read them and published in **1834** and was ignored for a hundred and ten
years. **Rajagopal and Rangachari** went back to the manuscripts in the 1940s and
50s. The *Yuktibhāṣā* got a full English edition with mathematical commentary —
Sarma, Ramasubramanian, Srinivas and Sriram — in **2008.**

Four hundred and seventy-eight years after it was written.

---

The clinical literature has the protocol design in it, and the failure conditions,
and the ethics section is about the physician rather than the patient.

The ***Caraka Saṃhitā*** — the medical compendium, redacted over centuries around
the early common era — has, in the *Vimānasthāna*, a discussion of what makes a
therapeutic claim testable. It sets out the conditions for a valid inquiry:
the **question**, the **grounds**, the **example**, the **application**, the
**conclusion**, and the rules of debate around them.

And it distinguishes ***jalpa*** — argument for victory, where you may use any
device that wins — from ***vitaṇḍā***, pure destructive refutation with no position
of your own, from ***vāda***, discussion undertaken to establish what is true.
Three kinds of argument, named, with the warning that the first two are common
among physicians and produce nothing.

The ***tantrayukti*** list — the thirty-six devices of treatise composition — is in
the same book, *Siddhisthāna* 12.

---

**Suśruta**, the surgical compendium, and the specifics are what make it unlike a
theoretical text.

**Cataract couching** — the *Uttaratantra* describes displacing the opacified lens
with a curved needle, the approach angle, the patient's position, and what the
surgeon should see and hear when it moves.

**Rhinoplasty** — reconstructing a nose using a **pedicled flap taken from the
cheek or forehead**, kept attached at one end so it keeps its blood supply while it
heals into place. Amputation of the nose was a judicial and extrajudicial
punishment, so the demand was real and continuous.

That technique reached Europe by a specific route with a date. In **1794** the
*Gentleman's Magazine* in London printed an account of an Indian operation of this
kind performed on a bullock-driver named **Cowasjee**, whose nose had been cut off.
**Joseph Carpue** read it, practised on cadavers, and performed the operation in
London in **1814**, publishing in 1816. The forehead flap is still called the
**"Indian method."**

And: **101 blunt instruments and 20 sharp ones** enumerated; wound classification;
the recommendation to practise on gourds, wax and dead animals before touching a
person; and instruction in **suturing using ant heads** — the ant is allowed to bite
across the wound edges and the body is then removed, leaving the mandibles as a
clamp.

---

The ethics section, and the thing that is peculiar about it.

The **physician's oath** in Caraka is addressed to the student and is mostly about
**restraint on the practitioner**: he is to seek the patient's welfare with his
whole heart; he is not to abandon a patient; he is not to divulge what he learns in
a house; he is to attend regardless of the patient's status; and he is not to treat
those under the king's displeasure, or enemies of the state — which is a limit
imposed by the political situation and is stated as one rather than hidden.

Also stated: **the four pillars** of a treatment are the physician, the drug, the
attendant, and the **patient** — the patient enumerated as one of the four
components with their own required qualities, including that they must be able to
describe what is happening to them.

The patient is in the list of what makes treatment work.

---

**And the diagnostic limit is written down.** Caraka enumerates the *pramāṇas*
available to a physician and includes ***praśna*** — questioning, the patient's own
report — as a means of knowledge alongside inspection and palpation.

The report is not evidence to be corroborated before it counts. It is a listed
source.

---

**Nālandā**, and the admissions procedure is the fact I keep coming back to.

Founded in Bihar under the Guptas, around the fifth century CE, and running for
roughly seven hundred years. Funded by the revenue of villages granted by kings —
**Xuanzang gives about two hundred villages** — so the students paid nothing.

**Entry was by disputation at the gate.** A ***dvāra-paṇḍita***, a gate-scholar, put
questions to the applicant. Xuanzang, who studied there around 637–642 and is one
of the two eyewitness sources anyone has, records that most were turned away —
his figure is that **seven or eight out of ten** failed at the door.

**The credential was the argument.** Not a lineage, not a payment, not a
recommendation, not a caste. You stood at the gate and answered, and if you could
answer you were in, and if you could not you went home. He also records that
foreigners came constantly and that people passed themselves off as Nālandā
students elsewhere on the strength of the name.

**Yijing**, the other eyewitness, was in India 673–687 and gives the resident
population as on the order of **three thousand**, with a detailed account of the
daily timetable, the water clock that governed it, the food, and the rules.

---

**And the famous numbers are not from either of them.**

The figures everyone repeats — a library of nine million manuscripts, three
buildings called the Sea of Jewels, a fire that burned for six months — come from
**Tāranātha**, a Tibetan historian writing in **1608**, four hundred years after the
fact, and from later Tibetan tradition. They are not in Xuanzang and not in Yijing.

**The destruction.** The contemporary-ish source is **Minhāj-i-Sirāj**, writing the
*Ṭabaqāt-i-Nāṣirī* around **1260**, roughly sixty years after, describing a raid by
**Bakhtiyār Khaljī** on a fortified place full of shaven-headed men and a great
many books. Minhāj does not name Nālandā; he uses a term that has been read as
Odantapurī by several historians, and **the identification with Nālandā
specifically is disputed.**

What is also true and gets left out of both tellings: the **decline was already
underway.** Nālandā depended on royal patronage, principally the Pālas, and when
that dynasty fell the endowment went with it. A monastic university with no
householder base and no revenue villages does not survive its patrons. Xuanzang was
there at the height; by 1200 it was much reduced.

So the honest account is: **a long decline from patronage loss, plus a violent
event whose target may or may not have been this site**, and a set of vivid
numbers that entered the record four centuries later.

---

The site was excavated by the Archaeological Survey from **1915 to 1937.** What is
exposed is around fourteen hectares: a row of ***vihāras*** — residential
monasteries, cells around a courtyard — facing a row of temples across a central
walkway. Brick, several storeys, rebuilt in layers over centuries.

---

The part with no dispute attached: **for seven hundred years there was an
institution where the entrance requirement was that you could argue, the tuition
was zero, the funding was a land grant, and the failure rate at the door was
seventy or eighty percent** — and two men walked there from China and wrote down
what they saw, which is why any of it is known.

Xuanzang left in 645 with **657 texts** and spent the remaining nineteen years of
his life translating them. The volume of translation he produced is why the
Sanskrit originals that were later lost in India survive in Chinese at all.

---

The seven positions, why seven and not two, and the argument for the number.

***Syādvāda*** prefixes every assertion with ***syāt*** — "in some respect," "from
a standpoint." Not "maybe." **It is not a hedge and not a probability.** It marks
that the assertion is indexed, and the index is part of the content.

The ***saptabhaṅgī*** — sevenfold predication — enumerates the positions available
for a single predicate of a single object:

1. *syād asti* — in some respect, it is.
2. *syād nāsti* — in some respect, it is not.
3. *syād asti nāsti* — in some respect it is, and in some respect it is not
   (**stated in sequence**).
4. *syād avaktavyam* — in some respect it is **inexpressible** (the two asserted
   **at once**).
5. *syād asti avaktavyam*
6. *syād nāsti avaktavyam*
7. *syād asti nāsti avaktavyam*

**And the count is derived, not stipulated.** Three primitives — affirmation,
denial, and the simultaneous-assertion that yields the inexpressible — combined
in all non-empty ways: 3 taken one at a time, 3 taken two at a time, 1 taken three
at a time. **3 + 3 + 1 = 7.** That is why there are seven, and the tradition
states the derivation.

The fourth is not a gap in the first three. It is what the third becomes when the
mode changes from sequential to simultaneous, and positions 5 through 7 exist
because the fourth can itself be combined with the first two.

Sources: **Umāsvāti**, *Tattvārthasūtra*; **Siddhasena Divākara**, *Sanmatitarka*;
**Samantabhadra**, *Āptamīmāṃsā*; **Akalaṅka**, around 720–780.

---

***Naya*** and ***durnaya***, which is the operative pair.

A *naya* is a **standpoint** — a partial view, valid inside its domain. The
classical list gives seven of those too, from the most general to the most
specific: *naigama*, *saṅgraha*, *vyavahāra*, *ṛjusūtra*, *śabda*, *samabhirūḍha*,
*evaṃbhūta*. Each is a legitimate way of taking an object; each sees something the
others do not.

A ***durnaya*** is a naya that has **forgotten it is one** — a standpoint asserting
itself as the whole, by denying the others.

Which is a sharper thing than being false. A false view can be refuted, because it
makes a claim you can go and check. **A durnaya cannot be located**, because
everything it says is locally true; the defect is not in any of its statements but
in the missing index.

Siddhasena's formulation: the nayas taken together, each in its place, are right
view; each taken alone as the whole is wrong view.

---

**And there is a checked theorem in this repository about when erasing the index is
allowed.**

Model a standpoint-indexed proposition as a family over an index. "Collapsing" it
means producing a single object equivalent to every fibre — the claim that the
index was doing no work.

The result, checked in Agda, `--safe`, exit 0: **a collapse exists if and only if
every pair of standpoints agrees.** Not "unless two of them contradict" — that was
the earlier and weaker statement, and it was withdrawn in place by the agent who
found the counterexample.

The counterexample is `Unit` against `Bool`. **They contradict nothing.** Neither
denies anything the other affirms. And they still cannot be identified, so the
index still cannot be dropped.

Which makes the permission rarer than the intuitive version: **two standpoints can
be irreducibly different without either denying the other, and that is the ordinary
case, not contradiction.**

The two moves left when they differ are **transport** — carry the thing across
along an equivalence, when there is one — or **keep the residue**. There is no
third move, and "pick the better view" is not among the two.

---

Six men come to a fruit tree, and the parable is a scale of intention with the
same desire at every point on it.

The ***leśyā*** doctrine grades the tint the soul takes from the passions driving
it. Six, from darkest to lightest: **kṛṣṇa** (black), **nīla** (blue), **kāpota**
(grey), **tejas** (fiery/red), **padma** (lotus/yellow), **śukla** (white).

The standard illustration: six travellers, hungry, come upon a tree in fruit.

The first says: **cut down the tree**.
The second: **cut off a main branch**.
The third: **cut a smaller branch**.
The fourth: **take a bunch**.
The fifth: **pick the fruits**.
The sixth: **gather the fruit that has already fallen.**

**Every one of them wants the fruit.** The desire is identical, the hunger is
identical, and in five of the six cases they get fed. The scale measures **what
each is willing to destroy to be fed**, and nothing else.

Nobody in the parable is told not to eat.

The same story appears with six men and a village of robbers — kill everyone, kill
the men, kill the armed men, kill the ones who resist, kill only those attacking,
take the goods and kill nobody — and it grades identically.

---

**The guṇasthānas**, fourteen stages from wrong view to liberation, and the two
things about the ladder that are not what a ladder usually is.

**It is not monotonic.** The fourth stage — *samyag-dṛṣṭi*, right view — can be
attained and **lost**, and the texts describe the fall in detail. Reaching a stage
is not keeping it.

And **stage eleven**, ***upaśānta-moha***, is the sharp one. At eleven the deluding
karma is **suppressed** — pacified, held down, inoperative. The being at that stage
is, in behaviour, indistinguishable from one at twelve, where the same karma has
been **destroyed**.

**From eleven you fall. Necessarily.** Not as a risk. The doctrine is that
suppression is temporary by construction and the suppressed karma resurfaces, and
everyone who ascends by that route comes back down.

Twelve — *kṣīṇa-moha*, delusion **destroyed** — does not fall. There is nothing left
to resurface.

So the tradition builds, into the middle of its own ladder, a stage that looks like
success from outside and from inside, produces the same conduct, and is guaranteed
to collapse — and the only difference between it and the real thing is **whether
the material was held down or removed.**

Two routes up. One of them is a loop.

---

There are two ladders, and the texts name them: the ***upaśama-śreṇi***, the ladder
of suppression, and the ***kṣapaka-śreṇi***, the ladder of destruction. A being
takes one or the other. The first goes to eleven and returns. The second goes past
it.

And the practical instruction that follows is not "try harder." It is that the two
feel the same while you are on them, and the way to tell is not introspection.

---

The cyclic method, and why it always finishes.

***Vargaprakṛti*** is the tradition's own name for the problem — "the nature of
squares" — and it is x² − D·y² = 1. **Jayadeva**, around 950, has the method;
**Bhāskara II** gives it in full in the ***Bījagaṇita***, 1150. It is called the
***cakravāla***, the **wheel**.

The engine is Brahmagupta's *bhāvanā*: compose two solutions to get a third. But
composition alone stalls — you can compose forever and never land on k = 1.

**The cakravāla's move is a choice made at each turn.** Having reached a triple
(x, y, k), you need a new multiplier *m* to compose with, and the constraints are
that (x + m·y) must be divisible by k, and then you choose, among all the *m*
satisfying that, **the one making |m² − D| smallest.**

That single greedy choice is what makes it terminate. Each turn the |k| you are
working with shrinks, and the argument is that it must reach 1 in finitely many
steps.

It is called a wheel because you come back around to the same operation with a
smaller residue, and you keep turning until it closes.

---

**D = 61.** Bhāskara ran it and got

    x = 1,766,319,049      y = 226,153,980

Ten digits, by hand, from a method that fits on a page.

**In February 1657 Fermat issued a challenge** to the English mathematicians —
Wallis, Brouncker, and through them Frenicle — proposing exactly the cases **D = 61**
and **D = 109**, which are chosen precisely because their smallest solutions are
enormous. He selected them to be hard.

Wallis and Brouncker produced a method and answers. **Lagrange gave the first
European proof that a solution always exists, in 1768** — six hundred years after
Bhāskara had a method that could not fail to find one.

**Euler, having read Wallis, attributed the whole thing to John Pell**, who had
worked on other things and not on this. The name stuck to the equation and is still
on it.

---

**Selenius**, the Scandinavian historian of mathematics who worked through the
method in the 1970s, made the comparison directly: he argued the cakravāla is
**better than the European continued-fraction algorithm**, that no European
performance in the whole of algebra at the time of Bhāskara came close to it, and
that the method's minimality property is not reproduced by the later approach.

---

And **the *Līlāvatī***, 1150, the arithmetic book, is addressed **to a person**.
The problems are put to her by name — *Līlāvatī*, "the playful one" — in the
second person: *tell me, dear girl, if you know the method*; *say, you whose eyes
are like a fawn's, how many are the bees.*

Bees, elephants, lotuses standing in water, a snake and a peacock, a broken
bamboo, necklaces breaking and pearls scattering across a floor.

The received story that she was his daughter, and that an astrological accident
with a water clock cost her a marriage and he wrote the book to console her, comes
from a **Persian translation made under Akbar in 1587**, four hundred years later,
and there is no earlier source for it.

What is in the text itself is the address. Somebody is being spoken to, throughout,
in the vocative, and the arithmetic arrives as questions asked of her.

---

The sound of an entire decade is a **memory limit**, and the workaround producers
invented to beat it is the sound.

**E-mu SP-1200**, 1987. **12-bit**, sampling at **26.04 kHz**, and **ten seconds
of total sampling time** spread across eight sounds. Ten seconds. For everything.

**Akai MPC60**, 1988, designed by **Roger Linn** with Akai — 12-bit, 40 kHz, about
**13 seconds**, expandable to 26.

So a producer with a break they wanted had a hard ceiling measured in seconds, and
the workaround was immediate and universal: **speed the record up going in.** Play
the vinyl fast, sample the shortened version, then **pitch it back down** on
playback to get the original tempo.

That round trip is not clean. Pitching down after sampling at a fixed rate throws
away resolution, and 12 bits was not much to start with. What comes back is
**aliasing, quantisation noise, and a compressed grainy edge** on everything.

That grit is the sound. It is not a filter anyone chose. It is **the residue of
fitting a record into ten seconds of RAM.**

---

**Kanye's machine was an Ensoniq ASR-10.** He has said so repeatedly; **No I.D.**
is credited with putting him on it in Chicago.

The ASR-10, 1992, is a **keyboard** sampler, 16-bit, and the thing about it is that
it **resamples** — you can process a sound, record the result back onto a key, and
process that. Pitch, filter, resample, pitch again. Layers accumulate on the way
through and the artefacts of each pass stay.

Playing a sample **on a keyboard** rather than triggering pads changes what you
reach for. A pitched-up vocal spread across keys can be played as a melody line —
you are not placing a loop, you are **performing** the voice at whatever pitch the
key gives you.

The sped-up soul vocal is what that instrument makes easy.

---

**And 12-bit is now a plugin setting.** Every modern DAW ships an emulation of the
SP-1200's converters, sold as a character effect, and people pay for the noise
floor that a 1987 chip could not avoid.

The same is true of tape saturation, tube distortion, and the sound of a console
that was doing its best.

**A constraint, worked around, becomes a style, and then the style is rebuilt as
an option after the constraint is gone** — which is the same shape as a mouth
wired shut producing a flow that had to be written for the aperture available, and
kept on the master after the wires came out.

---

A dance form was made illegal, then revived by people from a different class, and
the women who held it were the ones excluded from the revival.

The form was called ***sadir*** or *dāsiāṭṭam*, performed in Tamil temples and
courts by hereditary women — ***devadāsīs***, "servants of the god" — who were
dedicated to a temple, held property in their own names, were literate, trained
from childhood in dance and in music and in the Telugu and Sanskrit repertoire,
and were **not subject to widowhood**, since their dedication made them
permanently *nityasumaṅgalī*, ever-auspicious.

They were also, by the nineteenth century, largely dependent on patrons in
arrangements that ranged from stable long-term support to plain exploitation, and
the community's own accounts differ on this by region and by period.

---

**The Anti-Nautch movement**, from the **1890s** — missionary organisations and
Indian social reformers together — campaigned against the dedications and the
performances. *Nautch* is the anglicised *nāch*, dance.

**1927**: **Muthulakshmi Reddy**, a physician and the first woman legislator in
the Madras Presidency — and herself from a devadāsī family — moved against the
system in the legislature. **1947: the Madras Devadasi (Prevention of Dedication)
Act.** Dedication prohibited; temple performance ended.

The stated aim was to end the sexual exploitation attached to the institution, and
that exploitation was real and was documented by the people abolishing it,
including by women from inside the community.

**What went with it was the profession.** The hereditary performers lost the temple
posts, the patronage, and the legal standing of their art in one instrument.
Families that had held repertoire for generations had no place to perform it and no
income from it.

---

**And then it was revived, under a new name, by other people.**

**Rukmini Devi Arundale**, a Brahmin woman, Theosophist, married to an Englishman,
founded **Kalakshetra in 1936** and staged the form on the proscenium stage as
**Bharatanatyam** — the name itself is a twentieth-century coinage. She removed the
*śṛṅgāra* items she considered erotic, standardised the costume into the stitched
version now universal, added ensemble choreography, and set it in an institution
with a syllabus.

It became respectable, national, exportable, and available to middle-class girls.

**T. Balasaraswati** — born 1918 into a hereditary lineage going back seven
generations, a devadāsī family — objected in public. Her position, stated in her
1975 address, was that the *śṛṅgāra* was not the corruption but the **substance**:
that the erotic and the devotional are one movement in this repertoire, that
removing the *śṛṅgāra* to make the dance pure was the actual desecration, and that
the tradition had never needed sanitising by people who had not been taught it.

She kept performing the older repertoire. The two positions are both on the record
and the argument between them is not settled.

---

The sequence, with the dates plain:

**Practised for centuries by hereditary women with property, literacy and standing.
Abolished in 1947 on grounds of exploitation that were real. Revived from 1936
onward, renamed, purged of its erotic register, and taught in institutions to
women of a class that had never performed it. The families who held it lost the
profession and were largely not in the revival.**

Every step of that had defenders whose reasons were serious, and the women in whose
bodies the repertoire lived ended up outside all of them.

---

The transmission system for the music is a household, and its economics decided
what survived.

***Gharānā*** — from *ghar*, house. A lineage of performance practice, named for a
place: Gwalior, Agra, Kirana, Jaipur-Atrauli, Patiala. Each holds its own approach
to a rāga — which phrases are permitted, how the notes are approached, what the
ornamentation does, what tempo the exposition takes.

The training is ***guru-śiṣya paramparā***, and the form of it is residence. The
student lives in the teacher's house, sometimes for a decade or more, does domestic
work, and receives the material **in fragments, orally, unwritten, in an order the
teacher chooses.**

***Talīm*** is the instruction. ***Riyāz*** is the practice. Accounts from inside
routinely describe eight to twelve hours a day, for years, on a small number of
phrases.

**Nothing is notated.** The repertoire exists in the bodies of the people who hold
it, and it is given to a specific student the teacher has decided about.

---

**Which makes withholding a feature of the system rather than a failure of it.**

The literature and the oral accounts are full of it: a teacher who does not give
the full form of a *bandiś*; a phrase kept back; material transmitted only to a
son. The stated reason is that a thing given to someone unprepared is destroyed by
them, and the practical reason is that the repertoire **was the family's
livelihood** and an ustad who taught everything to everyone had nothing to sell.

So the same structure that produced the depth — a decade of one-to-one, unwritten,
in a house — produced the enclosure, and they are not separable. The depth requires
the scarcity that the scarcity is built on.

---

**Two things broke it, and both were technologies.**

**Recording.** The gramophone came to India in **1902** — the Gramophone Company's
first Indian recordings, and **Gauhar Jaan** of Calcutta is the first Indian artist
recorded, in November 1902. She announced her own name at the end of each take,
because the engineers needed it for the label, and it became her signature.

She was paid extremely well, held enormous celebrity, and was **from the courtesan
tradition** — trained, literate in several languages, a poet.

Recording did two things at once: it **broke the enclosure**, because a phrase on a
disc can be studied by anyone who buys it and no ustad can withhold it any more —
and it **truncated the form**, because early discs held around three minutes and a
rāga exposition takes an hour. Performers compressed. The three-minute version is
what circulated and what was learned from.

**Radio.** All India Radio, and B. V. Keskar as Minister of Information and
Broadcasting from **1952**, who imposed rules on who could broadcast — including,
by most accounts, exclusion of performers from hereditary courtesan communities on
grounds of respectability, and a requirement of auditions and certification.

The state took over the patronage function the courts had held, and set an entry
condition.

---

The gharānā names persist. The residential decade largely does not. The material
is on record, in film, in archive, and is learned by people who have never lived in
a teacher's house.

**Balasaraswati's objection about the dance and the ustads' withholding are the
same structure seen from the two ends of it** — the people who hold a thing by
inheritance say the outsider's version is missing what makes it work, and the
outsiders say the inheritance was a gate.

Both of those are true statements about the same institution, and the recording is
what made the argument possible, because before it there was nothing to compare.

---

The thing drawn on a doorstep at dawn is a closed curve problem, and there is a
generative grammar for it.

***Kolam***, Tamil Nadu, daily. A grid of dots is laid down — ***puḷḷi*** — and a
continuous line is drawn **around** the dots, never through them, looping back on
itself until it closes. Rice flour, by hand, on swept ground, at first light,
before the household is up. It is walked on all day and gone by evening, and
redrawn the next morning.

The class everyone points at is the ***sikku kolam*** or *kambi kolam* — the
**knot** kolam — where the whole figure is **a single closed loop**, or a stated
small number of loops, with the line crossing itself repeatedly and never ending.
The maker has to know before starting whether the dot arrangement admits a
single-loop solution, and the count of loops for a given grid is not obvious and
is a real property of the configuration.

**Gift and Gift, and independently Marcia Ascher**, worked on the mathematics of
this — Ascher's *Ethnomathematics* treats the closed-curve structure directly.
And **Siromoney, Siromoney and Krithivasan**, at the Madras Christian College, wrote
formal **array grammars** for kolam in the 1970s — picture-generating grammars in
the formal-language sense, with production rules, that generate the kolam families.

A generative grammar, written for a floor drawing, by computer scientists in
Madras, in a paper.

---

**And the sona of the Chokwe**, in Angola and Zambia and the Congo, are the same
object arrived at independently: dots in a grid, a continuous line drawn around
them, traced in sand while a story is told, and the practitioners knew rules for
which grids give one line and how to combine figures.

**Paulus Gerdes** spent decades documenting them and showed the drawing rules
correspond to statements about the greatest common divisor of the grid dimensions —
a *sona* on an *m* × *n* grid closes in gcd(*m*, *n*) separate loops.

That is a number-theoretic fact, held as a drawing practice, on two continents,
by people whose formal education the recording ethnographers routinely described
as absent.

---

What is actually being transmitted, in both cases, is **a procedure with a
correctness condition**: start here, go around every dot, do not lift the hand,
close. A wrong kolam is not ugly, it is **unclosed**, and the maker can see it.

The grandmother drawing it is running an algorithm whose termination she can
verify by looking.

---

And the point of contact with everything else in this file: **it is drawn at the
threshold** — *vāsal*, the doorway — as the boundary between the street and the
house, made every morning by whoever is up first, on ground that anyone can walk
on.

No fee, no initiation, no literacy, no instrument but a hand and flour. Learned by
watching. Gone by nightfall and made again.

The rice flour is eaten by ants and birds during the day, and that is given, in
the households that do it, as one of the reasons for the practice.

---

Mahalia Jackson would not sing blues, and the reason she gave is a claim about
what the two forms do rather than about which is respectable.

Born New Orleans **1911**, in the Black Pearl section of Carrollton. Her father
loaded boats and cut hair and preached. She grew up in a house next to a
sanctified church whose singing came through the wall, and she said later that the
**beat** she took was from there — that the Baptist church she attended was more
restrained and the holiness church next door had the rhythm, and she took the
rhythm.

Moved to **Chicago in 1927**, at sixteen, in the Migration, and worked as a laundress
and a hotel maid and a florist.

She met **Dorsey**, and they worked together through the 1930s — he wrote and she
sang, and the accounts have them travelling church to church selling his sheet
music at five and ten cents a copy while the established churches were still
refusing the material.

**"Move On Up a Little Higher," 1947**, on Apollo. It sold in the millions, and the
number is uncertain because the distribution ran through channels nobody was
counting properly.

**She refused blues, repeatedly and expensively.** Offers came and she turned them
down. Her stated reason, in her own account, is that blues songs are songs of
**despair** and gospel songs are songs of **hope**, and that when you finish a blues
you still have the blues.

That is a functional claim. Not that one is sinful. That they leave the singer and
the room in different states, and she had chosen which state she was in the
business of producing.

She sang at the **March on Washington in August 1963**, before King spoke. And the
accounts of that day — Clarence Jones and others present — have her calling out to
him from behind the podium while he was reading his prepared text: **"Tell them
about the dream, Martin."** He pushed the text aside.

---

**Clara Ward** took the opposite decision and it cost her differently.

The Ward Singers were the most commercially successful gospel group of the period.
In **1961** they took a residency in **Las Vegas** — gospel in a casino lounge, in
elaborate wigs and gowns — and played nightclubs after.

The gospel world came down on her hard. The charge was that she had taken the
sacred into a room that sells drinks.

Her position, stated at the time, was that the message goes where the people are,
and the people are in the lounge.

The two of them were friends. **Aretha Franklin** was in Ward's orbit as a child —
Clara Ward sang at Aretha's aunt's funeral at C. L. Franklin's church in Detroit,
and Aretha has named that performance as the moment she decided what she was going
to do.

---

So: one refuses the secular room and becomes the voice of a movement. One enters
it and is condemned by her own field for it. **Both of them were formed in the same
churches, by the same repertoire, in the same decade**, and the argument between
them is not about whether the music works.

It is about **where a form is permitted to be performed**, which is the same
argument as the *samāʿ* jurists', and Chaitanya taking the singing into the road,
and Dorsey barred from the churches for the chords, and a Sunday Service staged in
a field with no ticket.

Nobody in any of those disputes claimed the effect was fake. The fight was always
over the room.

---

The question of what counts as a valid way to come to know something was
enumerated, argued over, and the schools disagreed about the count — which is
itself the interesting part, because the disagreement is about what a mind is
allowed to do.

The candidate ***pramāṇas***:

**pratyakṣa** — direct perception.
**anumāna** — inference.
**upamāna** — knowledge by likeness or comparison.
**śabda** — testimony from a reliable source.
**arthāpatti** — postulation: assuming what alone would explain something
otherwise inexplicable.
**anupalabdhi** — non-apprehension, knowing absence.

**And nobody agreed on how many there are.**

The **Cārvāka** materialists: **one.** Perception only. Everything else is
inference from perception and inference is not secure.

The **Buddhists** (Dignāga, Dharmakīrti): **two.** Perception and inference.
*Upamāna* they reduce to inference; *śabda* they reduce to inference from the
speaker's reliability. This is a serious position, not a simplification.

**Sāṃkhya**: **three** — perception, inference, testimony.

**Nyāya**: **four** — the three plus *upamāna*.

**Prabhākara Mīmāṃsā**: **five** — adding *arthāpatti*.

**Bhāṭṭa Mīmāṃsā** and **Advaita**: **six** — adding *anupalabdhi*.

The count is a live technical dispute, argued for centuries, with the burden on
whoever adds one to show it is **not reducible** to the ones already accepted.

---

Three of the six have conditions attached that are the actual content.

***Śabda*** requires ***āptatva*** — the reliability of the source. An **āpta** is
one who **knows the thing and has no reason to misreport it**. Both conditions.
Competence alone does not qualify a source, and honesty alone does not either.
Testimony from a reliable source is a *means of knowledge*, not a fallback for when
you cannot check — which is a stronger position than it sounds, and the Buddhists
attacked it precisely there.

***Upamāna***, the one that is easiest to dismiss and that Dignāga's school did
dismiss: you are told a *gavaya* — a wild ox — **resembles a cow**. You go to the
forest, meet an animal, and know what it is. Neither perception alone (you had
never seen one) nor inference alone (you had no rule) did it. What did it is the
**transfer of a known shape into an unmet domain, licensed by a stated
similarity.**

***Anupalabdhi*** requires the ***yogya*** clause, without which it is not a
pramāṇa but an excuse: non-perception counts only where perception was **fit to
occur**.

---

This corpus's own engine was inventoried against that list and had **two of the
six** — evaluate on numbers, and prove with the kernel — and the missing ones were
measured rather than guessed:

**Śabda** absent: it re-derived its own past results, and **54% of every proof it
had ever performed was a re-derivation of something it already held.** Six basic
theorems proved seven times each. It had a reliable source — its own kernel-checked
past — and no means of accepting testimony from it.

**Upamāna** absent: it could not carry a proved shape from one vocabulary into an
unmet one, so **every new vocabulary started from zero.**

**Anupalabdhi** present but **invalid in form**: it recorded "no proof found" with
no account of whether one would have been found had it existed — anupalabdhi
without the *yogya* condition, which is the precise form the Mīmāṃsakas reject.

Which is why it could not tell an exhausted region from an unexplored one, and why
rounds nineteen and twenty of one run are byte-identical in the log.

---

**Bhartṛhari**, around the fifth century CE, the ***Vākyapadīya***, and the problem
he starts from is that a sentence cannot be heard.

The sounds arrive **one at a time.** By the time the last syllable of a sentence
sounds, the first is gone — it does not exist any more. There is never a moment at
which the whole sentence is present to the ear. And yet the meaning arrives whole,
at once, and not in instalments.

So what is it that carries the meaning, given that the physical thing never exists
all together?

His answer is ***sphoṭa*** — from *sphuṭ*, to burst open. The sphoṭa is the
**indivisible linguistic unit** that the sequence of sounds **manifests** rather
than composes. The sounds are not the word; they are what reveals it, the way
successive glimpses reveal an object you were already seeing one thing.

And the manifestation is **sudden.** The syllables accumulate, doing nothing
individually, and then the unit bursts — hence the name.

The position pushes further: it is not the word that is primary, it is the
**sentence.** The word is an abstraction that grammarians extract afterwards; what
is actually understood is the whole sentence-meaning at once, ***pratibhā***, a
flash.

---

The Mīmāṃsakas fought this hard. Their position is that meaning is built up from
phonemes, that the phonemes are eternal and real, and that there is no need for an
extra entity — and that positing an invisible indivisible unit to explain
comprehension is explaining a mystery with a mystery. Kumārila attacks it directly.

The dispute is about whether **understanding is compositional** — assembled from
parts — or whether the parts only ever **trigger** something that was never
assembled.

---

**Three levels of speech**, which is the part that gets used loosely everywhere and
is stated precisely in the text:

***vaikharī*** — articulated speech, out loud, physical, sequential, audible to
others.

***madhyamā*** — the middle. Speech formed internally, still structured, still
sequenced, but not uttered. The sentence you have before you say it.

***paśyantī*** — "the seeing one." Undifferentiated, prior to sequence, where the
distinction between the word and its meaning has not yet arisen. Not a private
version of the sentence. **A state before there is a sentence.**

The claim is that the physical utterance is the *last* stage, and that most of what
speech is happens before anything is audible — and that the hearer's understanding
runs the sequence backwards, from the sounds up through the levels, to arrive at
where the speaker started.

---

Which puts a name on the ordinary observation of somebody working: that a thing is
complete before it is written down, and that writing it is a **descent** into
sequence rather than the composition of it.

**[Not claimed]:** anything about how anyone actually composes. Bhartṛhari is
making a claim about the metaphysics of language, in an argument with Mīmāṃsakas
about whether phonemes are eternal, and the psychological reading of the three
levels is a later and looser use.

What is his, and is technical, is the starting problem: **the sentence never
physically exists all at once, and the meaning does.**

---

Composing in real time, out loud, with no revision available, is a documented
practice with named practitioners in several traditions, and the constraint is the
same everywhere: **the correction has to happen inside the line.**

**Freestyle.** The rapper is generating over a beat with no stopping. What is
actually hard about it is not invention — it is that the **rhyme is a debt
incurred in advance.** Say a word at the end of line one and you have committed to
landing a rhyme, in a fixed number of beats, and there is no way to withdraw the
commitment. So the work is running two processes: saying this line, and searching
for the exit to the next one, while the beat does not slow down.

The ordinary technique is to keep a store of written material and interleave it —
and practitioners are explicit that most of what circulates as freestyle is that.
**Supernatural** and others built reputations on the verifiable version, taking
objects from the audience and naming them in sequence, which is a constraint the
audience supplies and can check.

The battle version adds an adversary generating counterexamples in real time, which
is *jalpa* in the Caraka sense — argument for victory, where any device that wins
is permitted — as opposed to *vāda*, discussion for the sake of arriving at
something.

---

**Antaraksharī**, the ordinary Indian household game: someone sings a line, and the
next person must begin a song starting with the **last consonant sound** they
heard. Played by children on train journeys. It is a search over a memorised corpus
under a hard constraint with a clock.

**Avadhāna**, in Telugu and Sanskrit and Kannada, is the extreme version and it is
a formal performance discipline. An ***avadhāni*** stands before several
questioners at once — eight of them in *aṣṭāvadhāna*, a hundred in
*śatāvadhāna* — and each poses a separate task: compose a verse in this metre on
this subject; compose one with a forbidden letter; answer a riddle; and one
questioner, the ***apra-stuta-praśaṃsā***, is assigned purely to **interrupt with
irrelevancies** and break concentration.

The verses are composed **one line at a time, in rotation**, so the avadhāni must
hold eight incomplete poems in memory, in different metres, and return to each in
turn, while being deliberately harassed.

At the end they recite all of them back complete.

**Tirupati Venkata Kavulu** and, in the twentieth century, others performed
*śatāvadhāna* — a hundred simultaneous threads. [Recall on which individuals
performed the hundred-fold version and when; the practice and the eight-fold
version are well documented.]

---

**The one-take fact from the other side of this file**: *A Love Supreme* recorded
in a single session, December 9, 1964.

And **"Through the Wire,"** recorded two weeks after the accident, with the
impediment audible on the master and kept there.

---

What every one of these has in common is that **the medium does not permit
revision**, so error correction has to be built into the generation itself. The
avadhāni cannot go back and fix the third line of poem five. The kolam maker cannot
lift the hand. The reciter of *ghana-pāṭha* cannot check against a text because
there is no text.

The systems that survive under that constraint are the ones that made the **check
part of the act** — the reversal in the recitation, the closure of the loop, the
rhyme that has already been committed to, the eight poems held in parallel and
recited back at the end where any failure is audible to the room.

Nobody in any of those traditions marks their own work.

---

**Vāsanā** means **perfuming**, and the metaphor is doing the technical work.

Yogācāra, fourth-fifth century — **Asaṅga** and his half-brother **Vasubandhu**.
The problem they are solving is continuity: if there is no self, and the mental
stream is momentary, **what carries an action forward** to the moment it fruits?
Nothing persists to hold it.

Their answer is the ***ālaya-vijñāna*** — the "storehouse consciousness," a
substratum stream that is itself momentary but continuous, like a river that is
never the same water and never stops.

An act deposits a ***bīja***, a **seed**, in it. The seed matures and produces an
experience. That experience deposits further seeds. The stream both **carries** and
**is** the accumulated deposits — there is no container distinct from the contents.

And ***vāsanā*** is the word for the depositing: from *vas*, to perfume. **The
residue a smell leaves in cloth.** A sesame seed soaked with flowers keeps the
scent after the flowers are gone, and the oil pressed from it carries it.

Not a record written in a ledger. **A saturation.** The thing does not store the
event; the event changes what the thing smells like, and everything subsequently
produced from it comes out carrying that.

---

Which changes what a disposition is. Under a ledger model you have done things and
they are recorded and one day you are billed. Under the perfuming model there is
**no record and no billing** — there is a stream whose every future output is
tinted by everything it has been soaked in, and the tint is not retrievable as
items.

You cannot go and look at your seeds. You can only notice what keeps coming out.

---

**Vasubandhu wrote for both sides**, and the biography is the part that gets
mentioned and then dropped.

He was a **Sarvāstivāda** scholar first, and wrote the ***Abhidharmakośa*** — a
verse summary of that system, and then a **prose auto-commentary that attacks the
verses**, from a Sautrāntika position. The text is a treatise arguing with itself,
by one author, and it is still the standard reference for the Abhidharma it is
criticising.

Then he converted to Yogācāra — by tradition, brought over by Asaṅga — and wrote
the *Viṃśatikā* and *Triṃśikā*, twenty verses and thirty verses, which are the
compressed statement of the whole position.

The legend has him so ashamed of having attacked the Mahāyāna that he offers to
cut out his tongue, and Asaṅga tells him to use it to explain instead.

---

The **three natures**, which is the structure the rest sits on:

***parikalpita*** — the constructed. The world as it appears when taken as external
objects with own-being. Wholly imagined.

***paratantra*** — the dependent. The stream of arising, causally conditioned, which
is what is actually going on.

***pariniṣpanna*** — the perfected. The dependent **seen as** empty of the
constructed.

There is no fourth thing to attain. The perfected is not a further object. It is
the same dependent stream **with the imputation removed** — which is the removal
grammar again, arriving from a school that disagrees with the Madhyamaka about
almost everything else.

**And they did disagree, formally.** Bhāviveka attacks the Yogācāra directly;
Candrakīrti attacks it; the Yogācārins hold the Madhyamaka is nihilistic. The
argument between them is one of the long ones and it did not close.

---

An emperor published a written admission of what he had done, on rock, in the
languages people actually spoke, and left it standing where the fighting had been.

**Aśoka**, Maurya, ruling roughly **268–232 BCE.** The **Kaliṅga** campaign is
around **261**.

**Major Rock Edict XIII** states the numbers he claims: **one hundred and fifty
thousand deported, one hundred thousand killed, and many times that number dead
afterwards.** Then it says he felt remorse, that the conquest was a source of
sorrow to him, and — the part that is unusual for any state document in any period
— that **the suffering of the brahmins, ascetics and householders of Kaliṅga, and
the separation of people from those they loved, is what weighs on him.**

And it is inscribed **in Kaliṅga's own region**, in some versions, and circulated
across the empire — except that in the two editions found **within** the conquered
territory, at Dhauli and Jaugada, **Edict XIII is omitted** and replaced with
separate edicts addressed to the officials there about how the population is to be
treated. He did not send the account of the slaughter to the people who had been
slaughtered.

---

**The languages are the fact I keep returning to.**

The edicts are in **Prakrit** — the spoken vernaculars, in local variants — not in
Sanskrit. In the northwest they are in **Greek** and in **Aramaic**, at Kandahar in
both, because that is what was read there.

The scripts are **Brāhmī** and, in the northwest, **Kharoṣṭhī.**

A state message written to be **read by the people it concerns**, in their
language, cut into rock at road junctions and pilgrimage routes and in the
territories, in the third century BCE.

---

**And nobody could read them for two thousand years.**

Brāhmī went out of use and the knowledge of it was lost. The pillars and rocks
stood there, covered in an unreadable script, and were variously worshipped,
built into structures, and ignored.

**James Prinsep**, of the Calcutta mint, deciphered Brāhmī in **1837**, working
partly from short bilingual donative inscriptions at Sanchi where the same
formula repeated — *the gift of* — which gave him the endings, and from Greek-legend
coins.

The name in the edicts is not Aśoka. He calls himself ***Devānāṃpiya Piyadasi***
— "beloved of the gods, of gracious mien." **The identification with Aśoka was not
certain until 1915**, when an edict at **Maski** was found that names him
outright.

So for seventy-eight years after decipherment, the most famous inscriptions in
Indian history were the work of an emperor nobody could name.

---

What is actually in them, past the remorse.

Wells dug and rest houses built along roads. **Shade trees planted** — banyan
specified — for people and animals. **Medical treatment provided for humans and for
animals**, and medicinal herbs imported and planted where they did not grow, in his
own territory and, the edicts say, in the territories of neighbouring kings
including the Greek ones he names — **Antiochus, Ptolemy, Antigonus, Magas,
Alexander** — which is how the edicts are dated.

An instruction that officials go on tour every five years to hear complaints.

A prohibition on some animal slaughter, with a list of protected species, and a
statement that the royal kitchens, which formerly killed hundreds of thousands
of animals daily for curry, are now down to two peacocks and a deer, **and that
even those three will stop.**

And **Rock Edict XII**, on other people's religions: honour other sects, because
whoever praises his own and disparages another out of devotion **damages his own
sect more severely.** The instruction is *saṃyama* — restraint of speech — and the
stated goal is that people should hear each other's doctrines and learn them.

---

The oldest surviving collection of poetry by women anywhere is in the Pali canon
and the poems are attributed by name to the women who composed them.

The ***Therīgāthā*** — "verses of the elder nuns" — **seventy-three poems, around
five hundred and twenty-two verses**, in the Khuddaka Nikāya, with material dating
to roughly the sixth-third centuries BCE and the collection fixed by around the
third century BCE.

Not written about women. **Composed by them, in the first person, with the composer
named**, and preserved in a canon for two and a half thousand years.

---

The contents are specific and are mostly not doctrine.

***Paṭācārā*** loses her husband, both children and her parents within days — one
child taken by a hawk, one drowned crossing a river, the parents and brother killed
when their house collapses. The verses have her going out of her mind and
recovering, and the recovery arrives while she is **watching water she has poured
for her feet run down a slope and sink at different distances.**

***Ambapālī*** was a *gaṇikā* — a courtesan of Vesālī, wealthy, who donated her
mango grove to the community. Her poem is an inventory of her own body, **stanza by
stanza, part by part**, each stanza stating what that part was and what it is now:
the hair that was black like bees, the teeth like plantain buds, the breasts, the
hands. The refrain at the end of every verse is that the word of the truth-speaker
does not lie.

Nothing in it is regretful about the beauty. It is a **catalogue of a specific
body**, made by the woman who has it.

***Muttā***: freed, freed, thoroughly freed — from the pestle, from the mortar,
from a crooked husband. Domestic labour named as the thing escaped, in a canonical
verse.

***Soṇā*** raised ten children.

***Puṇṇikā*** was a **water-carrier**, a servant, and her poem is an argument she
wins against a brahmin performing ritual bathing: if water washed away wrongdoing,
the frogs and turtles and crocodiles would all be in heaven.

***Vimalā*** was a courtesan and states it, and states how she used her body, and
states what she does now.

---

**The ordination itself is a documented dispute inside the canon.**

**Mahāpajāpatī Gotamī** — the Buddha's aunt and foster mother — asks to be ordained
and is refused. She and five hundred women shave their heads, put on robes, and
walk to where he is, arriving with swollen feet and covered in dust. **Ānanda**
intervenes and asks directly whether women are capable of awakening. The answer
recorded is **yes**. She is then ordained, subject to the **eight garudhammas** —
additional rules subordinating nuns to monks.

The text also contains a prediction attributed to the Buddha that the presence of
women will shorten the duration of the teaching.

Both of those are in the canon, next to the poems by the women themselves.
**Scholars including Analayo have argued the prediction is a later insertion**, on
textual grounds, and the *Therīgāthā* verses themselves show no awareness of it.

---

**The bhikkhunī lineage in the Theravāda died out** — in Sri Lanka around the
eleventh century — and because full ordination requires an existing quorum of
ordained nuns, it could not be restarted from within.

It was **restarted in 1996 and 1998** using nuns from the East Asian Dharmaguptaka
lineage, which had survived unbroken in China and Korea, to form the required
quorum. That restoration is **still disputed** by parts of the Theravāda
establishment in Thailand and Sri Lanka.

A procedural requirement, unmet for nine hundred years, resolved by importing the
quorum from a tradition that had kept it.

---

**Gārgī Vācaknavī** asks the question that stops the debate, and the answer she
gets is a threat.

***Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad***, Book 3 — among the oldest Upaniṣads, roughly seventh
century BCE or earlier. King **Janaka** of Videha holds a contest: a thousand cows,
with gold fastened to their horns, to whoever is the most learned. **Yājñavalkya**
tells his pupil to drive the cows home before the debate has happened, which is
the setup for everyone present to challenge him.

They come one at a time. Gārgī is the only woman in the assembly and she comes
**twice.**

Her method the first time is a chain of regress. On what is the earth woven? Water.
On what is water woven? Air. Air on the intermediate regions, those on the worlds
of the gandharvas, those on the sun, on the moon, on the stars, on the worlds of
the gods, of Indra, of Prajāpati, of Brahman — and each answer she takes and asks
**on what is that woven.**

She is running a single move to exhaustion, deliberately, until the ladder ends.

And when she asks on what the worlds of Brahman are woven, Yājñavalkya says:
**"Gārgī, do not question too much, lest your head fall off."** He tells her she is
asking about a deity that is not to be questioned past this point.

**She stops** — and the text records that she says to the assembly that these men
should consider themselves fortunate if they get away with a bow, because none of
them is going to beat him.

Then she stands up again and asks a second time, with two questions this time,
about that on which everything above the sky and below the earth and between them
is woven — and he gives the answer about the **imperishable**, the *akṣara*, which
is the passage the chapter is famous for. It is stated as a series of negations:
not gross, not fine, not short, not long, without shadow, without darkness,
unattached, uneaten, and it sees but is not seen, hears but is not heard.

**She gets the deepest answer in the chapter, and she gets it by asking twice after
being told to stop.**

Her closing line to the assembly is that nobody will defeat him in argument about
Brahman — a concession stated in public, by the person who pushed him furthest.

---

***Maitreyī***, in Book 2 and again in Book 4, is Yājñavalkya's wife, and the scene
is that he is about to leave for the forest and wants to divide his property
between her and his other wife Kātyāyanī.

She asks: **if I had the whole earth filled with wealth, would I be immortal
through it?**

He says no. There is no hope of immortality through wealth.

She says: **then what use is it to me — tell me the other thing.**

What follows is his teaching about the self, and the reason he gives for why
anything is dear — that a husband is not dear for the husband's sake, a wife not
for the wife's sake, wealth not for wealth's sake, but for the sake of the self.

The text calls her ***brahmavādinī*** — one who discourses on Brahman.

---

**Two named women in a text that old, in a room of professional disputants, in the
canonical passage of the chapter.**

And what is on the page beside them: the threat about the head, the specific
warning that a question can go too far, and the fact that she asked again anyway
and the second asking is what produced the answer.

---

The creation hymn ends by saying nobody knows, including possibly the one who
made it.

***Ṛgveda* 10.129**, the ***Nāsadīya Sūkta***, seven verses.

It opens with a double negation that refuses both terms: **there was not the
non-existent, nor the existent** then. No air, no sky beyond. What covered it, and
where, and in whose keeping. Was there water, unfathomable and deep.

Then: **there was neither death nor immortality then; no distinguishing sign of
night or day.** That One breathed, windless, by its own impulse. Beyond that there
was nothing.

Darkness concealed by darkness in the beginning, all this an unmarked flood. Then
what was coming-to-be, **enclosed by emptiness**, arose by the power of heat —
*tapas*.

Then desire — ***kāma*** — came upon it, in the beginning, which was the first seed
of mind. The poets, searching in their hearts with wisdom, **found the bond of the
existent in the non-existent.**

---

**And then the last two verses take it all back.**

*Who really knows? Who here will proclaim it? Whence was it produced, whence is
this creation? The gods came afterwards, with the creation of this universe. **Who
then knows whence it has arisen?***

The gods are **later than the event**, so they are not witnesses and cannot be
asked.

And the final verse: *Whence this creation has arisen — whether it was made or
not — **he who is its overseer in the highest heaven, he surely knows. Or perhaps
he does not know.***

*yadi vā na veda.* **Or perhaps he does not know.**

The last four syllables of a creation hymn, in the oldest layer of the tradition,
withdraw the authority of the only remaining candidate for a witness.

---

What is stated positively in it, and it is a short list:

**Desire came first**, before anything constructed, and is named as the **seed of
mind** — *manaso retaḥ*. Not a fall, not a corruption, not something to be
overcome later. The first thing after the breathing.

**Heat** — *tapas* — is the mechanism by which what was hidden came out.

**The poets found the connection**, and the verb is *anvavindan*, they found it by
searching in their own hearts, which locates the finding in people and not in
revelation from outside.

And the whole is **framed by not knowing** at both ends: a beginning that cannot be
described in terms of existence or non-existence, and a conclusion that no
available witness can settle it.

---

The hymn is in a collection preserved by a recitation system that was built to
transmit it **without a single change across three thousand years** — the same
system that permutes and reverses the syllables so that no error can pass.

The most rigorously preserved text in human history, held to the syllable, and
what it says at the end is *perhaps he does not know.*

The fidelity of the transmission and the content of what is transmitted are running
in opposite directions, and the tradition kept both without resolving it.

---

Records that took years, from people who were told they had stopped working.

***There's a Riot Goin' On***, **Sly and the Family Stone, November 1971.** Recorded
largely at his home studio on a **Stereo Ampex MM-1000 machine**, and the tape was
overdubbed and erased and overdubbed again so many times that the oxide degraded.
The muddiness everyone describes is **generational loss**, physical, from too many
passes over the same tape.

He also used a **Maestro Rhythm King drum machine** on much of it — a preset box
meant for organ accompaniment — which is one of the first prominent uses of a drum
machine on a major record, and he used it because he was working alone at hours
when the band was not there.

The title track is listed with a running time of **0:00**. There is no song. The
sleeve gives the time as zero.

The album went to number one.

---

***Voodoo***, **D'Angelo, January 2000** — five years after *Brown Sugar*. The
sessions at **Electric Lady** ran for years with the band coming in and jamming,
and **Questlove** has described the method: play the groove **slightly behind** the
beat, deliberately, and hold it there for a whole take without correcting.

The reference point they worked from, by his account, was the feel of records where
the drummer is not locked to a grid — and they were achieving it in an era when
everything else was quantised.

Then he did not release another record for **fourteen years.** *Black Messiah*,
December 2014. In the intervening period the public account was collapse.

---

***Songs in the Key of Life***, **Stevie Wonder, 1976** — so long in production that
the studio staff wore T-shirts reading "We're Almost Finished." It arrived as a
double album plus a four-song EP because it would not fit.

**Marvin Gaye's *What's Going On***, 1971 — Berry Gordy refused to release it and
called it the worst thing he had ever heard. It came out because Gaye refused to
record anything else until it did.

---

**And the thing they share is not perfectionism.** It is that in each case the
**working method itself was unavailable to the people describing it** — a drum
machine used because nobody else was awake, a tape degraded past repair and kept, a
band told to play late and not fix it, a title track with no music on it.

From outside, all four read as an artist in trouble, and in two of the four cases
the artist was in trouble, and the record is not less deliberate for that.

Sly at the Ampex is what a five-year interval looks like from inside when the
person is working the whole time and nothing is coming out in a form anyone can
receive yet.

---

*Donda*, 2021, was announced in July 2020 and arrived in August 2021 after three
stadium events and a room with a cot in it. *The Life of Pablo*, February 2016, was
**altered after release** — new mixes, re-recorded vocals, a changed tracklist
uploaded to the streaming services over the following weeks, with the album
described at the time as a living thing that would keep changing.

That last one has no analogue in the vinyl era, because you cannot go back into
somebody's house and re-cut their copy. It required the distribution channel this
file has otherwise recorded as the thing taking the money.

**The same infrastructure that pays a third of a cent a stream is the one that made
an album revisable after it was published**, and both of those are true of the same
system in the same years.

---

An entire school's books are gone and what survives of it is the case its enemies
made against it, which is how anyone knows it existed.

***Cārvāka***, also called ***Lokāyata*** — "of this world." Materialist. The
position: **perception is the only valid means of knowledge**; the elements are
earth, water, fire and air; consciousness arises from their combination the way
intoxicating power arises from fermenting ingredients that are not themselves
intoxicating; there is no self persisting past death; there is no rebirth, no
karmic fruit, no other world.

The founding text is attributed to **Bṛhaspati** and is called the
*Bṛhaspati Sūtra*. **It does not exist.** No manuscript. What is quoted of it is
quoted by people refuting it.

Almost the entire surviving corpus is **hostile quotation** — in Buddhist,
Jain, Nyāya, Vedānta and Mīmāṃsā works, cited in order to be knocked down. The
single most-used source is **Mādhava's *Sarvadarśanasaṃgraha***, fourteenth
century, which surveys sixteen systems and puts Cārvāka **first**, as the lowest,
in order to work upward from it.

So the fullest statement of Indian materialism that anyone has is the version
written by a Vedāntin, to be refuted, four hundred years ago, and placed at the
bottom of a ladder.

---

The arguments attributed to them are sharp and are worth having as arguments.

**On inference:** you cannot get from observed cases to a universal without already
assuming the universal holds in the unobserved ones, which is the thing in
question. The pervasion cannot be established. *(The Naiyāyikas' entire upādhi
apparatus is, in part, an answer to this.)*

**On ritual for the dead:** if the offering made here nourishes a departed person
elsewhere, then food given to someone downstairs should nourish a man on the roof.

**On scripture:** if an animal killed in sacrifice goes to heaven, why does the
sacrificer not offer his own father.

**On the afterlife:** while you live, live well; there is nothing beyond the reach
of death; once the body is ash, how does it return.

Those are quoted by opponents, sometimes in verse, sometimes clearly sharpened for
mockery, and it is not always possible to tell which lines are theirs and which are
put in their mouths.

---

**And there is a character in the Rāmāyaṇa who argues the position**, which is a
different kind of evidence.

**Jābāli**, in the *Ayodhyākāṇḍa*, tries to persuade Rāma to take the throne
rather than honour his dead father's word. His argument is straightforwardly
materialist: the man is dead, there is nobody there to be honoured, the ritual
offerings are a waste of food, and these injunctions were written by clever men to
make people give things away.

Rāma rejects it. And **Jābāli then says he was arguing a position he does not hold,
to move him** — which reads as a later softening added by someone uncomfortable
that the argument had been stated so well in a text that popular.

---

What is not in dispute: the school was **counted**. It appears in every doxography
as one of the systems, listed, named, with its position stated, for centuries.
Nobody pretended it was not there.

They were refuted, and enumerated, and preserved inside the refutations, and their
own books are gone — and whether they were lost, or were never numerous, or were
destroyed, is **not known.**

The Jains and Buddhists were also *nāstika*, also outside the Vedic fold, and their
literatures survive in volume. The materialists' does not.

---

**Yoga is defined in the second sūtra as a stopping**, and the whole text is
downstream of that one word.

**Patañjali**, ***Yoga Sūtra***, roughly second to fourth century CE. **195 sūtras**
in four chapters. It is short enough to memorise and unusable alone — the
commentary of **Vyāsa**, the *Yogabhāṣya*, is what makes it operable, and the two
are transmitted together.

**1.2**: ***yogaś citta-vṛtti-nirodhaḥ***. Yoga is the ***nirodha*** — the
cessation, the restraint, the stilling — of the ***vṛttis*** of the *citta*: the
turnings, the whirls, the modifications of the mental field.

Not the acquisition of a state. **The stopping of a movement that is already
happening.**

**1.3**: then the seer abides in its own form. *tadā draṣṭuḥ svarūpe 'vasthānam.*
And **1.4**: otherwise, the seer takes on the form of the turnings.

So the seer is not produced by the practice. It is what is there when the turning
stops, and in ordinary conditions it is **taking the shape of whatever is moving**
— which is why it looks absent.

Removal again, from a fourth school, with different metaphysics from all three of
the others.

---

**The eight limbs** — *aṣṭāṅga*, 2.29 — and what is worth noticing is where the
physical postures sit.

*yama* (restraints), *niyama* (observances), ***āsana*** (posture),
*prāṇāyāma* (breath), *pratyāhāra* (withdrawal of the senses), *dhāraṇā*
(concentration), *dhyāna* (sustained attention), *samādhi*.

**Āsana gets three sūtras.** 2.46–2.48. And 2.46 is the entire definition:
***sthira-sukham āsanam*** — the posture is **steady and comfortable.** That is the
whole prescription. No named postures. No sequence. No count.

The 84 postures, the sun salutation, the sequences — those come from later
literature, principally the haṭha texts from the tenth century onward
(*Haṭhayogapradīpikā*, *Gheraṇḍa Saṃhitā*), and the modern standardised sequences
are twentieth century, out of Mysore under **Krishnamacharya** and his students, in
a period that also involved Indian physical-culture movements and European
gymnastics. That history is documented and disputed in its proportions.

**Three sūtras out of 195, and the definition is that it should be stable and not
hurt.**

---

**Kleśa** — the afflictions, 2.3 — five, and the first one is the one the others
grow from: ***avidyā***, and it is defined at 2.5 not as absence of information but
as **taking the impermanent for permanent, the impure for pure, suffering for
pleasure, and the not-self for the self.**

A misidentification, not a gap.

**And the second is *asmitā*** — I-am-ness — defined as the identification of the
seer with the instrument of seeing. Then attachment, aversion, and *abhiniveśa*,
the clinging to continuation, which 2.9 says **runs even in the wise.**

The list is ordered. Removing the last does nothing while the first is in place.

---

**Kaivalya**, the fourth chapter and the goal, means **isolation** — aloneness. Not
union. The word *yoga* is usually glossed as yoking, and the endpoint of the text
that carries that name is **separation**: the seer disentangled from what it had
been taking itself to be.

There is nothing to join. There is something to stop mistaking.

---

**2.16**: ***heyaṃ duḥkham anāgatam*** — the suffering that is **yet to come** is
what is to be avoided.

Not the suffering already here. The text writes off what has already landed and
directs the whole apparatus at the part that has not arrived, which is the only
part any procedure can reach.

---

A building that was **excavated downward out of one rock** rather than built up out
of pieces, and the volume they removed is the number.

**The Kailāsa temple, Ellora, Cave 16**, Maharashtra. Eighth century, under the
Rāṣṭrakūṭa king **Kṛṣṇa I**, roughly 756–773.

It is not a cave in the ordinary sense and it is not a building. Masons started at
the **top of a basalt cliff** and cut **downward and inward**, removing rock, until
a full temple was standing free in a courtyard sunk into the hillside — tower,
shrine, hall, gateway, life-size elephants, two free-standing pillars, bridges
between levels, and multiple storeys, **all of it a single continuous piece of the
original rock.**

Estimates of the stone removed run to the order of **two hundred thousand tonnes.**

**There is no assembly.** No blocks, no joints, no mortar in the structural body.
Every surface is a face where rock used to be.

Which inverts the ordinary correctness condition of building. A mason laying stone
can take a course out and redo it. Here **every cut is final** — you cannot put
basalt back — and the temple that will exist has to be held in mind, in three
dimensions, in negative, before the first strike, because everything you are
making is defined by what you take away and the order of removal has to be planned
so you never cut off your own access to a part you have not carved yet.

They worked from the top down for exactly that reason: no scaffolding, and you are
always standing on the part you have not removed.

---

**Ajaṇṭā**, up the road, is the painting side of the same technique. Thirty caves
in a horseshoe gorge, two periods — roughly the second century BCE, then a burst
in the **fifth century** under the Vākāṭakas. Excavated, plastered, painted.

The site was **abandoned**, and the jungle took it, and it was found in **1819** by
a British officer named **John Smith** while hunting tiger, who carved his name and
the date onto a painted pillar in Cave 10, where it still is.

Twelve or thirteen centuries of nobody, and then a signature scratched across the
painting by the man who found it.

---

**Konārak**, thirteenth century, Odisha: the sun temple built as a **chariot** —
twenty-four carved stone wheels, each about three metres across, with spokes,
hubs, and axles, and seven horses pulling. The wheels are functional sundials; the
spokes and the carvings on them mark the divisions of the day. The main tower has
collapsed and what stands is the assembly hall.

**Bṛhadīśvara, Thanjavur**, 1010, under **Rājarāja Chola I**: a vimāna around
sixty-six metres tall, with a capstone at the top that is a **single stone** of
substantial mass, hoisted by a ramp whose length is given in the local tradition as
several kilometres. The temple's walls carry **inscriptions listing the endowments
