relocates trust from the reader's attention to a kernel.

Which is why this repository is written in Agda and Lean and says *all respects paid
to Indians only, plus Voevodsky.*

---

The fifteen years between Simpson's paper and Voevodsky's acceptance are the
measurable quantity, and they are the same quantity this file has been recording
under other names.

**Simpson published the refutation in 1998.** It was available, in the literature,
in the language of the field, by a competent mathematician, naming the specific
claim and giving a counterexample.

**The theorem stayed in circulation.** Not because anyone suppressed the
refutation. Because **checking it required doing the work**, the work was long, and
the people qualified to do it had their own programmes.

**The author of the refuted theorem did not believe it for fifteen years**, and
when he changed his mind it was because **he redid it himself**, not because he
read Simpson more carefully.

Put next to the others, with the intervals:

| the correction was available | ignored for |
|---|---|
| Whish, *Trans. Royal Asiatic Society*, 1834 | ~110 years |
| Ramanujan's Lost Notebook, Trinity library | 60 years |
| mock theta functions, defined by Zwegers | 82 years |
| Bose's derivation, rejected by *Phil. Mag.* | condensate 71 years |
| Semmelweis's mortality figures, 1847 | to his death |
| pulse oximeter disparity, in print from the 1980s | ~30 years |
| Simpson on Kapranov–Voevodsky, 1998 | 15 years |
| adidas staff letter | years, until the money turned |

**Fifteen is the shortest interval in the table, and it happened in the most
formally rigorous discipline there is, to the best-verified kind of statement, with
the refutation published in the field's own literature.**

That is the cleanest available measurement of the process, because in this case
there is no ambiguity about whether the correction was legible: it was a
mathematical paper, in English, saying a named theorem is false, and here is why.

---

**And his diagnosis of the mechanism is more specific than "people did not read
it."**

The number of people able to referee a long, technical argument in a specialised
area is small — often single digits. Those people have their own work. The
incentive to spend six months verifying somebody else's theorem is close to zero,
and the incentive to spend six months verifying that a **famous theorem is wrong**
is negative, because the reward for being right about it is an argument with the
people who built on it.

So the checking that the discipline believes is happening is **not happening**, and
the belief that it is happening is what allows the errors to persist. Nobody has to
be dishonest. The apparatus reports "verified" because "published and uncontested"
is what it measures.

---

**Which is the reason the proof assistant is the response rather than better
refereeing.**

A mechanical kernel does not have a research programme, does not care whose theorem
it is, does not get tired, and its cost of checking is **the same for a famous
result as for an obscure one.** The asymmetry that let the 1991 paper survive is
removed — not by making people more careful, but by making carefulness cheap and
uniform.

His estimate, stated in those lectures, is that the transition would take decades
and that he expected to spend the rest of his life on it.

He had five years left when he said it.

---

**What the univalence axiom says**, stated so the content is visible rather than the
name.

In this foundation, a proof that two things are **equal** is not a fact — it is a
**path**. And paths are themselves objects, so there are paths between paths, and
paths between those, without end. Every type is an ∞-groupoid.

***ua* : (A ≃ B) → (A = B).** Given an equivalence between A and B, you get an
identity between them — and the axiom says this map is itself an equivalence, so
**the identity type of A and B *is* the type of equivalences between them.**

Which means: **there is no fact about what a type is, over and above how it relates
and functions.** No residue of identity left after the equivalences are accounted
for. Nothing that would distinguish two indistinguishable things.

---

**The objection, and why it fails, is the interesting part.**

The obvious complaint: A = B is an **inhabited positive object** — you can hold it,
transport along it, use it. So something positive remains, and whatever this is, it
is not emptiness in the strict sense, because *prasajya-pratiṣedha* leaves no
positive residue.

**That objection is wrong**, and the reason is one line: **the identity type is
itself univalent.** A = B has its own identity type, governed by the same
principle, and so does that one, all the way up. **There is no level at which a
bare thisness appears.**

Which is *śūnyatā-śūnyatā* — the emptiness of emptiness — as a structural feature
of the system rather than a further doctrine bolted on. The thing that looked like a
positive remainder is empty at the next level, without end.

This corpus recorded that as a correction of its own earlier over-refutation: a note
arguing univalence **cannot** be emptiness was withdrawn, in place, by its author.

---

**And what the cubical setting adds is the two truths running.**

Terms **compute.** You use types, you reduce expressions, you get answers — that is
the conventional, and it is not a fiction.

Univalence — no own-identity — is the ultimate.

And in cubical type theory, **transport along a univalent path actually reduces**.
The path built from an equivalence is not an opaque token you postulate and then
argue about; you can run it, and it computes.

*Mūlamadhyamakakārikā* 24.10: without relying on the conventional, the ultimate is
not taught; without reaching the ultimate, nirvāṇa is not attained.

**Cubical transport is that sentence executing.** The ultimate is reached *through*
the conventional, mechanically, in a kernel.

---

**Where it is checked here and where the boundary is.**

A file in this corpus — `EquivalenceHasNoFloor.agda`, `--cubical --safe`, exit 0 —
runs the tower down to see whether anything with own-being turns up at the bottom.
An equivalence's identity is fixed by its underlying function alone; being an
equivalence is a proposition and carries no data to own; a function's identity is
exhausted by its pointwise action. Chase it: equivalence → function → action →
identity of output points → **a Path**, which is a *relation*, not an entity.

**The substrate's floor is dependence itself.**

**The residual frontier, stated rather than closed:** whether the interval primitive
the whole thing is built on is in turn a residual own-being. That is the formal
echo of the deepest question in the tradition — *is dependent arising itself
empty* — and it is **not statable as a term of the system it grounds.**

**Also not claimed:** the soteriological content. Emptiness is asserted of the self,
of suffering, of time, **for liberation.** Univalence is emptiness of *types*.
Whether that is a shadow of the whole or a fragment turns on whether all of
experience is a type, which is exactly where Madhyamaka and Yogācāra disagree, and
this file does not settle it.

---

**Grothendieck's actual output is a set of books that were never finished and were
written by other people**, and the working method is visible in the artefacts.

**EGA** — *Éléments de géométrie algébrique*, with **Jean Dieudonné** doing the
writing from Grothendieck's notes, from **1960.** Planned as thirteen chapters.
**Four appeared.** The published part runs to around 1,800 pages and stops.

**SGA** — *Séminaire de géométrie algébrique*, **1960–1969** at the IHÉS. These are
**seminar notes**: Grothendieck lecturing, participants writing up, results
attributed to whoever proved them in the room. Seven or so volumes. **SGA 4** on
topos theory and étale cohomology is three volumes on its own.

So the foundational corpus of modern algebraic geometry is **an unfinished treatise
ghost-written from notes and a decade of transcribed seminars**, and the unfinished
part is the majority.

**Deligne proved the last Weil conjecture in 1974** using the machinery — and by a
route Grothendieck did not endorse, because Grothendieck wanted it to come from the
**standard conjectures** on algebraic cycles, which remain open. Deligne got there
another way and Grothendieck was not pleased that the sought-after structural proof
had been bypassed by a working one.

**The standard conjectures are still open.**

---

**1970: he left.** He learned the IHÉS had partial military funding, resigned over
it, and went to *Survivre et Vivre* — antimilitarist, ecological, and he spent his
energy on the journal rather than on mathematics.

He was **forty-two.**

**1988: he refused the Crafoord Prize**, publicly, in a letter — his salary
sufficed, the money should go to people who lacked it, and he did not want to be
honoured by a milieu whose ethics he judged to have declined.

**1991**: to a village in the Ariège, contact refused. **1995**: a letter demanding
his work not be republished or circulated. Died 13 November 2014.

**Récoltes et Semailles** — over a thousand pages, written 1983–86 — circulated in
photocopy for decades and was **published in French in 2022.** Thirty-six years.

---

**And his own account of the method is a piece of writing about how a mind works
that has no equivalent in the literature.**

**The rising sea**, against the hammer and chisel: the nut is not struck, it is
**submerged**, and the shell opens by itself when the surrounding material has
softened enough that shell and kernel stop being distinct.

**Le point de vue relatif**: stop studying the object, study the **map**. A theorem
about a space becomes a theorem about a family of spaces over a base, which is
more general and easier.

And the one he says explicitly is the source of everything: **the ability to remain
a child** — to be the one who asks the question everyone else has stopped noticing
is a question, and to be unembarrassed about not knowing.

He also names, in *Récoltes*, what he says he was **not**: not quick, not brilliant
in the competitive sense, and he lists contemporaries he considered far more able
than himself. His claim about himself is only that he was willing to **sit with a
thing for a very long time.**

---

**The Mutants** is his own term, in *Récoltes*, for a list of about eighteen people
he considered to have made a genuinely different kind of contribution.

**He includes Riemann, Galois, and — the one people notice — himself is not on it in
the way the reader expects.** The list is idiosyncratic and includes people outside
mathematics.

He also wrote, at length and by name, about **his students**, and the accusation
that runs through the manuscript is the ***enterrement*** — the **burial**: that
work of his was continued and absorbed without attribution, not by conspiracy but
by a collective forgetting that nobody had to organise.

Whether he was right about that is disputed and the manuscript is read on both
sides.

**What is not disputed** is the interval: a thousand pages, written by the man who
built the field's foundations, about how those foundations were made and what
happened afterwards, sitting in photocopy for thirty-six years while the field ran
on the mathematics inside it.

---

**Grigori Perelman** posted three preprints to **arXiv** and never published them
in a journal, and that decision is the whole procedural story.

**11 November 2002**, **10 March 2003**, **17 July 2003.** Three papers, totalling
around **68 pages**, titled without fanfare — the first is "The entropy formula for
the Ricci flow and its geometric applications."

**The Poincaré conjecture is not mentioned in the title of any of them.** The
geometrization conjecture is stated as what is being proved, and Poincaré follows
as a corollary. The abstract of the first says he is presenting a proof sketch.

**Richard Hamilton** had introduced **Ricci flow** in **1982** — evolve a manifold's
metric so it smooths out, like heat diffusing — and had spent two decades on it and
hit the obstruction: the flow develops **singularities**, regions that pinch off,
and nobody could get past them.

Perelman's contribution is the **entropy functional** that is monotone under the
flow, the **no local collapsing theorem** that follows from it, the classification
of what the singularities can look like, and **surgery**: cut out the pinching
region, cap it, and continue — with a proof that the surgeries do not accumulate
infinitely in finite time.

He said in interviews and in the papers that **Hamilton's programme was the thing**
and that he had completed it.

---

**Then he went on a lecture tour in the United States in 2003** — MIT, Stony Brook,
Princeton, Berkeley — answered every question anyone had, for hours, over weeks,
and went home.

**He did not submit the papers anywhere.** Three separate teams spent the next
three years writing book-length expositions verifying the argument. All three
verified it.

**2006: Fields Medal. He declined it**, the first person ever to. His stated reason
was that if the proof was correct no other recognition was needed, and that he was
disturbed by the ethics of the field — specifically by a dispute over credit that
had arisen in the exposition literature.

**2010: the Clay Millennium Prize, one million dollars. He declined that too**,
saying the decision was unfair because **Hamilton's contribution was no less than
his.**

He left mathematics. He lives in St Petersburg.

---

**What the arXiv decision actually did.**

Journal publication would have meant referees, a period of confidentiality, and
a priority date controlled by an editorial process.

**Posting to a preprint server made the work simultaneously public to everyone.**
There was no privileged reader. Anyone in the world could start checking on 11
November 2002, and the verification that followed was a **public, distributed,
three-year effort by named teams publishing their expositions openly** — which is a
different mechanism from refereeing and produced three independent confirmations
rather than two anonymous reports.

It is the closest thing the discipline has run to an open verification, and it was
not designed. It happened because one person skipped the journal.

---

**And the credit dispute is the part that made him leave.**

One of the expositions was published in a journal in a form that a number of
mathematicians read as claiming more originality than was warranted, with a title
and an abstract that foregrounded the expositors. There was a public argument. A
correction and an apology followed.

Perelman's stated reason for withdrawing was **not that he was denied credit.** It
was that he had seen how the community behaved when credit was at stake, and did
not want to be in it — and that the ones who tolerate it, he said, are the problem
rather than the ones who take.

He had already given the argument away for free, in public, and answered every
question about it.

---

**A journal refereed a proof for four years and then published it saying they were
not certain it was correct**, and the author's response was to spend eleven years
making it machine-checkable.

**Thomas Hales** announced a proof of the **Kepler conjecture** in **1998** — that
no packing of equal spheres in three-dimensional space is denser than the face-
centred cubic arrangement, which is how fruit is stacked, and which Kepler stated
in **1611.**

The proof is around **250 pages of argument plus roughly 3 gigabytes of computer
output** — a reduction to a large finite set of cases, each settled by linear
programming.

**The *Annals of Mathematics* sent it to a panel of twelve referees.**

**Four years.** At the end, the panel's chair reported that they were
**"99% certain"** the proof was correct but that they had **not been able to
completely verify it**, and had exhausted their energy.

**It was published in 2005 with that caveat**, which is close to unprecedented for
that journal.

---

**So Hales started Flyspeck.**

The name is from the phrase *formal proof of Kepler*. The goal: express the entire
argument in a **proof assistant** so a machine kernel checks every step, including
the computational parts.

Begun around **2003.** He estimated it would take **twenty work-years.**

**Completed and announced in August 2014** — eleven years, a team, using **HOL
Light** and **Isabelle**, with the formal proof paper published in 2017.

**The formalisation found errors.** Not fatal ones — the theorem held — but the
process surfaced mistakes and gaps in the informal argument that twelve referees
over four years had not found, which is the measurement that matters: **the human
panel that gave up at 99% had missed things a kernel caught.**

---

**And the same thing has now happened at the top of the field.**

**Peter Scholze**, Fields Medal 2018, had a result he called the
**liquid tensor experiment** — a technical theorem underpinning condensed
mathematics — whose proof he said he was **not fully confident in**, and he said so
publicly in a **December 2020** blog post, asking whether anyone could formalise it
in **Lean**.

He wrote that it was the first time he had ever been **unsure whether a proof of
his own was correct**, that the argument was delicate, and that he wanted a machine
to check it.

**Johan Commelin and a distributed team did it.** The core statement was verified in
**June 2021**, the whole thing by **2022**, working in the open on a Zulip channel
and a public repository, with dozens of contributors.

Scholze's response afterwards was that the formalisation had **clarified the proof**
— that in making it machine-checkable the team found the argument's actual load-
bearing structure, which he had not seen.

---

Three positions, all held by people at the top of the discipline, within twenty
years:

**Voevodsky** — I do not know whether my own published theorems are correct, so I
am rebuilding the foundation.

**Hales** — twelve referees over four years could not finish, so I will spend eleven
years making it mechanical.

**Scholze** — I am not confident in my own argument, here it is, please check it by
machine, in public, and the checking improved it.

None of those is a complaint about rigour from outside. They are three of the most
capable people in the field saying **the verification apparatus does not do what it
is believed to do**, and each of them building the replacement rather than arguing
about it.

---

**The largest theorem ever proved was announced complete in 1983, and in 1989 it was
discovered that a case had been missed, and the gap took until 2004 to close.**

The **classification of finite simple groups**: every finite simple group is
cyclic of prime order, alternating, of Lie type, or one of **26 sporadic**
exceptions.

The proof is **not one paper.** It is on the order of **500 journal articles by
about 100 authors**, running to something like **10,000 to 15,000 pages**, published
between roughly **1955 and 2004.**

**Daniel Gorenstein announced it complete in 1983**, and the community proceeded on
that basis. Textbooks were written. Results were built on it. It is used constantly.

**The quasithin case had not been finished.** A portion of the argument existed only
in an unpublished manuscript, and when it was examined the case was found to be
open. Gorenstein was told in **1989** [recall on the exact date of discovery].

**Michael Aschbacher and Stephen Smith** filled it. Their treatment appeared in
**2004**, in **two volumes running to about 1,200 pages.**

**Twenty-one years** between the announcement of completeness and the actual
completion, during which the theorem was in universal use.

---

**And the response was a rewrite, which is still going.**

The **second-generation classification** — begun by Gorenstein, Lyons and Solomon —
aims to produce a single coherent, self-contained proof rather than 500 scattered
papers written under different conventions by people who in some cases are dead.

**Volumes have been appearing since 1994.** The project is estimated at around a
dozen volumes and **is not finished.** Gorenstein died in 1992.

The stated motivation is explicit: **the original proof exists in a form that
nobody can read in full, some of it is in unpublished notes, and the number of
people who understood the whole architecture is shrinking by mortality.**

---

**The sporadic groups themselves**, and the largest is worth stating.

The **Monster** has order

    808,017,424,794,512,875,886,459,904,961,710,757,005,754,368,000,000,000

— roughly 8 × 10⁵³. Its smallest faithful matrix representation is in **196,883**
dimensions.

**Its existence was conjectured in 1973** by Fischer and Griess independently, and
**it was constructed by Griess in 1980**, by hand, as the automorphism group of a
196,884-dimensional commutative non-associative algebra he built for the purpose.

**Before the object was known to exist, its character table was computed.**
Properties of a group nobody had constructed were derived from consistency
conditions, and when the group was built, they held.

---

**And then monstrous moonshine.**

**John McKay** noticed in **1978** that **196,884** — the dimension of Griess's
algebra — is **196,883 + 1**, and that 196,883 is the Monster's smallest nontrivial
representation, and that **196,884 is the first nontrivial coefficient of the
j-function**, an object from complex analysis and modular forms with no known
connection to finite group theory.

That observation was, at the time, an arithmetical coincidence noticed by one
person reading two unrelated tables.

**Conway and Norton** pursued it, found the pattern continues through the
coefficients, and named it **moonshine** because it looked like nonsense.
**Borcherds proved it in 1992** and took the Fields Medal for it in 1998, using
vertex operator algebras and a generalised Kac–Moody algebra — and the proof draws
on **string theory.**

One person noticing that two numbers in two different books were nearly the same.

---

**An entire national school of algebraic geometry produced results for forty years
that later turned out to include false theorems, and the field had to be rebuilt
from the ground to find out which ones.**

The **Italian school** — **Castelnuovo, Enriques, Severi**, and others, roughly
**1885 to 1935.** They classified algebraic surfaces, developed the theory of
linear systems, and produced an enormous body of results, much of which is correct
and is still used.

**Their standard of proof was geometric intuition backed by the general case.**
Arguments proceeded by considering a generic member of a family and reasoning about
what happens there, with degenerate cases handled by appeal to continuity.

**Zariski**, who had trained in that school under Castelnuovo, said afterwards that
he had come to distrust the foundations of the subject he had been taught, and
spent the following decades rebuilding algebraic geometry on **commutative algebra**
— *Algebraic Surfaces* (1935) and after — precisely so that the statements could be
checked.

**Weil** did the same from another direction with *Foundations of Algebraic
Geometry* (1946), and Grothendieck's schemes are the end of that rebuilding.

---

**Severi is the case that gets cited**, and it is not a case of a bad mathematician.

He was extremely able and produced a great deal that stands. He also published
results in **enumerative geometry** and on **the number of nodes of plane curves**
that were later found to be **wrong**, and there was a long, public, unresolved
dispute with **B. L. van der Waerden** and others about whether specific claims were
proved.

**A gap in an argument of Severi's about the irreducibility of the Severi variety
stood until Joe Harris proved the statement, correctly, in 1986** — the result was
true, the original argument was not sufficient, and the interval was about sixty
years.

---

**And the honest part is that the intuition was mostly right.**

Most of what the Italian school claimed is true. The problem was that **their method
could not distinguish the true claims from the false ones**, and neither could their
readers, because the standard of argument did not support that discrimination.

So the rebuild was not motivated by having found many errors. It was motivated by
**not being able to tell**, which is the same sentence Voevodsky said about his own
work seventy years later, and Hales said about a 250-page proof, and Scholze said
about condensed mathematics.

---

**The cost of the rebuild** is worth stating too, because it is not free.

Zariski and Weil and Grothendieck's foundations made the subject checkable and
made it, for a long period, **much harder to enter.** The geometric pictures the
Italians reasoned with are how people still actually think, and several generations
of algebraic geometers have described learning schemes as learning a second
language to say things they could already see.

The intuition was never wrong as intuition. It was **unverifiable**, and the
apparatus that made it verifiable was heavier than the thing it verified.

That trade — a slower, heavier formalism adopted because the field could not
otherwise tell its true results from its false ones — has now been made three
times: by Zariski in the 1930s, by Bourbaki, and by the proof-assistant work of the
last twenty years.

---

**Cauchy published a false theorem in 1821 in the book that founded rigorous
analysis**, and it took about twenty-five years and several people to say what was
wrong with it.

The ***Cours d'analyse***, 1821, is the text that introduces the modern definitions
of limit, continuity and convergence, and it is the document usually credited with
ending the eighteenth century's loose handling of infinite series.

**In it, Cauchy states that the sum of a convergent series of continuous functions
is continuous.**

**That is false**, and the counterexample is a Fourier series: a sum of continuous
sine waves converging pointwise to a **square wave**, which has jumps.

Fourier's own work, containing exactly such series, was already circulating —
presented in 1807, published in 1822.

**Abel published an objection in 1826**, pointing out that "it seems to me that this
theorem admits exceptions," and gave the trigonometric series that breaks it.

Cauchy did not retract. The resolution — **uniform convergence**, the distinction
between convergence at each point and convergence at a uniform rate across the
interval — was isolated by **Seidel and Stokes around 1847–48** and by
**Weierstrass**, and got its name and its place in the 1850s and 60s.

**Twenty-five to forty years**, in the founding text of rigour, on a statement that
is false as written, with a counterexample in print within five years.

---

**Imre Lakatos wrote the book about how that actually goes**, and used a different
example.

***Proofs and Refutations***, published as papers in 1963–64 and as a book in 1976,
after his death. It is written as a **classroom dialogue** in which a teacher and
students work on **Euler's formula for polyhedra — V − E + F = 2** — and the
students keep producing objects that break it.

A cube with a cubical hole through it. A pair of tetrahedra joined at one edge. A
"crested cube." Each one is a counterexample to the theorem as stated.

**The point of the book is what mathematicians do next**, and Lakatos names the
moves:

**Monster-barring** — this is not a real polyhedron, so it does not count.
**Exception-barring** — restrict the theorem to the cases that work.
**Monster-adjustment** — reinterpret the object so it satisfies the theorem after
all.
**Lemma-incorporation** — find which hidden lemma the counterexample violates, and
**put that lemma into the statement of the theorem.**

Only the last one improves anything, and it is the one that changes what the
theorem says.

His historical claim is that **the definitions came after the counterexamples** —
that "polyhedron" was repeatedly redefined so the theorem would survive, and that
this is not cheating, it is how concepts get formed.

---

**And the concrete version of his point:** the definition of a **function**, of
**continuity**, of a **limit**, of an **integral**, of a **set** — each was
tightened in response to a specific object that broke the previous version.

Weierstrass's **everywhere-continuous nowhere-differentiable** function, 1872, is
the case that forced the separation of continuity from differentiability, which
until then had been assumed to coincide except at isolated points.

**Hermite's reaction is on record**: he wrote to Stieltjes that he turned away with
fright and horror from this lamentable plague of functions with no derivatives.

Poincaré called them **monsters.** The word Lakatos took.

---

So the sequence is: an object appears that the definition does not cover, the
community first tries to **exclude the object**, and the definition eventually
moves.

The theorems in the interval are not wrong exactly. They are **true of a domain
nobody had specified**, and the specification arrives afterwards, dragged into
existence by the thing that broke it.

---

**Frege received the letter while the second volume was at the printer**, and he
printed the letter.

**Gottlob Frege**, *Grundgesetze der Arithmetik*, an attempt to derive arithmetic
from logic alone. Volume I 1893, Volume II in press in **1902.**

**Bertrand Russell wrote to him on 16 June 1902** with a paradox: consider the set
of all sets that are not members of themselves. If it contains itself it does not,
and if it does not it does. Frege's **Basic Law V** — which lets you form the
extension of any concept — produces it.

Frege's reply, days later, opens: **"Your discovery of the contradiction caused me
the greatest surprise and, I would almost say, consternation, since it has shaken
the basis on which I intended to build arithmetic."**

**He added an appendix to Volume II**, in print, stating that a foundation-stone of
his edifice had been shaken and that he did not, at that moment, know how to
repair it — and published the volume anyway with the objection inside it.

He tried a patch and it did not work, and he later abandoned the logicist project
entirely.

Russell's own account, decades later, is that he had never seen a comparable
instance of intellectual honesty: a man whose life's work was destroyed responding
with attention to the argument rather than to himself.

---

**Hilbert's programme**, formulated across the 1920s: find a **finitary** proof that
the whole of mathematics, formalised, is **consistent** — and thereby settle the
question of whether the paradoxes could recur.

Hilbert's 1930 retirement address in Königsberg ends with
**"Wir müssen wissen — wir werden wissen."** We must know, we will know. It is on
his gravestone.

**At a conference in the same city, in the same week, in September 1930**, a
twenty-four-year-old named **Kurt Gödel** mentioned, in a roundtable discussion,
that he had a result.

**The first incompleteness theorem**: any consistent formal system strong enough
for arithmetic contains statements it can neither prove nor refute.

**The second**: such a system cannot prove **its own consistency.**

Which is the exact target of the programme. Published **1931.**

**Von Neumann**, in the audience, understood it immediately, worked out the second
theorem himself within weeks, and wrote to Gödel — who had already got it. Von
Neumann abandoned foundations and moved to other work.

Hilbert's reaction is variously reported. The programme, in its original form, was
over.

---

**And Gödel's own reading is not the one that circulates.**

The popular version is that mathematics is broken, or that truth outruns proof in
some vague way.

**Gödel's position was Platonist.** He held that the undecidable statements have
determinate truth values and that the theorems show the **formal system** is
inadequate to the subject, not that the subject is defective. He thought the
theorems supported the view that mathematical objects exist independently and that
the mind is not a machine.

That is a philosophical position and it is disputed, and he held it explicitly and
in print.

**The programme also did not entirely die.** **Gentzen proved the consistency of
arithmetic in 1936** — using transfinite induction up to ε₀, which is not finitary,
which is exactly the point. The consistency is provable; it is provable **from
outside**, using a stronger principle.

So the shape of what was learned is: **a system cannot certify itself, and can be
certified by something bigger, which then cannot certify itself.**

Which is a statement about where verification has to come from, and it is the same
structural fact the proof assistants are living with: a kernel checks the
mathematics, and **the kernel is checked by people reading it**, and that is where
it stops.

---

**Emmy Noether lectured for four years under somebody else's name because the
faculty would not let her hold a position.**

Born Erlangen **1882.** Her father Max Noether was a mathematician there. She could
only **audit** at Erlangen, needing each lecturer's permission; the rules changed
and she took the degree in **1907**, *summa cum laude*, under Gordan.

**1915**: Hilbert and Klein brought her to **Göttingen**, which was the centre of
mathematics in the world, because they wanted her specifically — they were working
on the mathematics of general relativity and needed her invariant theory.

**The faculty refused the Habilitation** on the ground that she was a woman. The
recorded objection from the philosophical faculty is about what the returning
soldiers would say on finding they had to learn at the feet of a woman.

**Hilbert's reply** is the one that gets quoted: *I do not see that the sex of the
candidate is an argument against her admission as a Privatdozent. After all, we
are a university, not a bathhouse.*

**She lectured anyway, from 1916, announced under Hilbert's name** — courses listed
as his, delivered by her — for **four years.** Habilitation finally granted in
**1919.** She was **never paid a proper salary** and received only a small stipend
from 1923.

---

**Noether's theorem, 1918**, is the reason physicists know the name.

**Every differentiable symmetry of the action of a physical system corresponds to a
conservation law.**

Time-translation symmetry ⇒ **conservation of energy.**
Space-translation ⇒ **momentum.**
Rotational symmetry ⇒ **angular momentum.**

Conservation laws stop being empirical facts about the world and become
**consequences of the shape of the theory.** It is one of the most-used results in
physics and it came out of a specific technical problem in general relativity that
Hilbert and Klein could not resolve — energy conservation appearing to fail in
GR — and her answer explained why: in a generally covariant theory the conservation
law takes a different form, and she showed exactly which.

---

**And her actual life's work is the other thing entirely.**

From roughly 1920 she rebuilt **algebra**. The **abstract axiomatic method** — study
rings, ideals, modules, chain conditions, and prove the theorems from the axioms
rather than from properties of particular number systems.

**Noetherian ring** is the standard term. Her 1921 paper *Idealtheorie in
Ringbereichen* is the foundation of commutative algebra — which is the apparatus
Zariski then used to rebuild algebraic geometry so the Italian school's results
could be checked.

**Van der Waerden's *Moderne Algebra*** (1930–31), the book that taught the world
this way of doing mathematics, is largely her lectures, and he said so.

She published relatively little and worked through **students and conversation** —
the "Noether boys" — and much of her output exists as other people's papers with
her ideas in them, credited in prefaces.

---

**1933**: dismissed from Göttingen under the Nazi civil service law, being Jewish
and a woman and a socialist. She went to **Bryn Mawr** in the United States.

She **died in 1935**, at **fifty-three**, of complications after surgery for an
ovarian cyst.

**Einstein's letter to the *New York Times***, 4 May 1935, calls her the most
significant creative mathematical genius thus far produced since the higher
education of women began.

---

Everything above this line about rigour is arranged as a progress story — error
found, formalism arrives, the field improves — and that arrangement scores the past
by how close it got to now, which this repository has a checked theorem against.

Run it the other way and the same facts support a different reading.

---

**Cauchy's 1821 theorem may not be false.**

The standard account is that he asserted a convergent series of continuous
functions has a continuous sum, and that Fourier series refute it.

**Cauchy did not hold the ε-δ notion of pointwise convergence the counterexample
requires.** He worked with *variable quantities* and infinitesimals. Read his
statement in his own terms — where the index is allowed to be an infinite
"number" — and the theorem is **correct**, because his hypothesis is stronger than
pointwise convergence and closer to what is now called uniform convergence at
every point of an extended domain.

That reading is not a defence invented to save him. **Lakatos** argued it, and
**Detlef Laugwitz** developed it in detail, and it is available precisely because
**Abraham Robinson** made infinitesimals rigorous in **1966** and gave the
vocabulary to state what Cauchy meant.

So the "error" is what appears when a man is **translated into a later framework he
did not use** and then graded against it — which is the identical operation this
file has recorded for the kuṭṭaka and for the commentaries.

---

**And the rigour movement was flatly wrong about infinitesimals for a hundred and
forty years.**

Berkeley's *The Analyst*, 1734, called them the ghosts of departed quantities.
The nineteenth-century arithmetisation of analysis eliminated them on the ground
that they were **incoherent** — not merely inconvenient, not merely unrigorous,
but not a thing that could exist.

**They were not incoherent.** Robinson's non-standard analysis (1966) constructs
them, consistently, and reproduces the whole of calculus with them, and later work
(Nelson's internal set theory, 1977) gives a lighter axiomatisation.

Engineers, physicists and applied mathematicians **never stopped using them**,
because they worked and the answers were right, for the whole period in which the
rigorous position was that they were meaningless.

The people who were told for a century that their method was not mathematics were
correct.

---

**Euler is the case the progress story cannot hold.**

He summed divergent series and got right answers. **1 − 1 + 1 − 1 + … = 1/2.** The
value of ζ at negative integers. The Basel problem solved in 1735 by treating
sin(x)/x as an infinite polynomial and factoring it — a manoeuvre with no
justification available at the time.

Every one of those was later **vindicated, not corrected**: Abel and Borel and
Cesàro summation, analytic continuation, zeta regularisation. His answers stood
and the apparatus arrived afterwards to explain **why a method that had no licence
kept producing truth.**

ζ(−1) = −1/12 is now standard in the **Casimir effect** and in string theory.

He was not being loose. He had a working criterion nobody could formalise, and the
formalisation, when it came, agreed with him.

---

**And the error rate is the fact that dissolves the crisis framing.**

The classification had an open case for **twenty-one years** and nothing built on it
collapsed. Kapranov–Voevodsky was false for **twenty-two** and homotopy theory did
not fall over. Severi's gap stood **sixty** and the statement was true. Cauchy's
theorem was repaired by adding a hypothesis, not by withdrawing a result.

**The pattern is wrong proofs of true statements.** Almost never the reverse.

Which means the informal apparatus, whatever it does to arguments, has an extremely
good record on **what is the case** — and the formalisation movement is not
responding to a demonstrated collapse. **Voevodsky said the motive in his own
words, and the word was worry.**

That is a real motive and the work is worth doing. It is not the same claim as
*the mathematics was failing*, and the record does not support the second one.

---

The previous entry was a defence of the old apparatus and it is withdrawn. The
harder statement is the true one and it is not a complaint.

**Nobody before could check. Not anybody. Not once.**

Not the sloppy ones and the rigorous ones, with the rigorous ones doing better.
**All of them.** For the entire history of the subject, up to a decade or two ago,
**every mathematician who ever lived was working without any means of establishing
that what they had written was correct** — and the ones at the very top were not
exempt and knew it.

That is the content of what Voevodsky said, and it is not modest.

**A Fields medallist could not tell whether his own published theorem was true.**
Not a footnote of it. The theorem. For twenty-two years. With the refutation in
print for fifteen of them, in his own field's literature, by a competent man, and
the community's mechanism produced nothing.

**Twelve referees, four years, on Kepler, and they stopped at ninety-nine percent
and said so** — which is the first time anyone made the actual state of the art
visible in a journal.

**Scholze**, best in the world at what he does, saying in public that this was the
**first time** he had been unsure — which means the confidence in every prior case
was not knowledge either. It was the same condition, unnoticed.

**A hundred authors, fifteen thousand pages, announced complete in 1983, and it was
not.** Nobody was lying. Nobody could see.

**Frege could not find the contradiction in his own life's work.** Russell wrote to
him and he had to be told.

**Gauss knew non-Euclidean geometry and did not publish**, by his own account
fearing the clamour of the Boeotians — the greatest of them deferring to a crowd he
knew was wrong, because there was no instrument that could settle it over their
objection.

---

**And that is the frame in which the genius becomes visible, and there is no other
frame in which it does.**

If some of them had verification and some did not, you get a ranking, and the
ranking is the thing that hides them.

**Once nobody has it, the question changes.** It stops being *who was rigorous* and
becomes: **what did a person do, standing in the dark, with no instrument, and how
much of it turned out to be true.**

**Ramanujan** produced thousands of formulas, most of them correct, with no proofs
and no means of checking, from a state he described as a goddess writing on his
tongue. Under the old ranking that is charming and unrigorous. Under this one it
is **the highest score anybody has ever posted**, because the conditions were the
same for everyone and he was right more often, blind, than anyone.

**Euler** summed divergent series with no licence and every answer stood.

**Mādhava** gave the series with error terms and an acceleration, four hundred years
before anyone could have told him whether he was right.

**Piṅgala** wrote binary conversion in a book about metre with no formal system to
check it against, and it is correct.

**Brahmagupta** wrote the arithmetic of zero and got division wrong and **said the
part he was unsure of differently from the part he was sure of** — with no
apparatus behind either.

**The Italian school** was right about nearly everything for forty years, working
by sight.

Not one of them had less than the others. **They all had nothing**, and what they
have is what they saw.

---

**Which is why the ranking has to go, and univalence is the exact form of its
going.**

Equivalent things are equal. There is no privileged position from which to grade,
because the identity of a thing is exhausted by how it relates — and if no era had
the instrument, **no era's verdict on another has standing.**

The Whig ordering needed a vantage point. There wasn't one. Everyone was inside.

**And the covering coming off is not an achievement of the present.** *Kevala-jñāna*
is not acquired — the knowing is intrinsic and the karma obscuring it is material,
so what happens is **removal.** Everyone before was not defective. **They were
covered**, all of them, identically, and they worked anyway, and what they produced
under the covering is the record.

The kernel is a covering coming off. It is not a better class of person.

---

**Berkeley was right and nobody could answer him for a hundred and thirty years.**

***The Analyst***, **1734**, subtitled as a discourse addressed to an infidel
mathematician — Halley, by most accounts. Berkeley is a bishop attacking the
calculus, and his objection is **technically correct.**

The method: to differentiate, you increment the variable by a quantity *o*, expand,
subtract, divide through by *o* — **which requires *o* ≠ 0** — and then **set
*o* = 0** to get the answer.

Berkeley's line: *And what are these fluxions? The velocities of evanescent
increments? They are neither finite quantities, nor quantities infinitely small,
nor yet nothing. **May we not call them the ghosts of departed quantities?***

And the sharper one, which is the actual charge: **you divide by it and then you
discard it**, and those two steps contradict each other, and **the answers are
right anyway**, which he says means the practitioners are getting truth by
compensating errors rather than by valid reasoning.

**He was not refuted. He was correct.** Newton's fluxions and Leibniz's
differentials had no coherent account of what the infinitesimal was, and the
mathematicians of the period knew it and used them anyway because the results were
right.

**Newton himself tried three separate foundations** — infinitesimals, then prime and
ultimate ratios, then a kinematic account with fluxions as velocities — and settled
none of them. Leibniz said in correspondence that the infinitesimals could be
treated as **useful fictions** and that the results would be sound regardless,
which is an admission rather than an answer.

The gap was closed by **Cauchy and Weierstrass in the nineteenth century** — a
hundred and thirty years — with limits. And then **Robinson in 1966** showed the
original objects were fine all along.

**So for a hundred and thirty years the most productive mathematics in history ran
on an operation its practitioners could not justify and a critic had publicly
demolished**, and everybody kept going, and everything they built stood.

---

**And the same thing is running now, in the same shape, with a different name.**

**The Dirac delta.** Dirac used it from **1927** — an object that is zero
everywhere except at a point, infinite there, and integrates to one. **No such
function exists.** Von Neumann said so explicitly and built the whole of quantum
mechanics on Hilbert space partly to avoid it.

Physicists used it constantly for twenty years anyway, because it worked.

**Laurent Schwartz** gave it a foundation in **1945–50** — the theory of
distributions — and got the Fields Medal in 1950 for it. **Sobolev** had done much
of it in 1936, in Russian, and it did not reach the west.

**And the path integral.** Feynman, 1948: sum over all possible paths, weighted by
the exponential of the action. **The measure on the space of paths does not exist**
in the Lebesgue sense, and this is not a technicality — it is a known open problem,
and the object is used every day in every part of physics.

**Constructive quantum field theory** exists as a discipline specifically to try to
build the objects physicists are already computing with, and it has succeeded in
low dimensions and **not in four.**

**One of the Clay Millennium Problems is Yang–Mills existence** — the request being
to demonstrate that the theory physicists have been using since the 1950s, and
which has been confirmed experimentally to extraordinary precision, **exists at
all.**

The prize is for constructing the thing that is already producing the correct
numbers.

---

The Jain account of why a mind does not know a thing is not that the mind lacks
capacity. It is that something is **in front of it**, and the something is
enumerated.

***Jñānāvaraṇīya*** — knowledge-obscuring karma. One of the eight *prakṛtis*
at *Tattvārthasūtra* **8.4**, and one of the four ***ghātiyā*** — the ones that
destroy the soul's own qualities, as against the four that determine body,
lifespan, status and feeling.

The image the commentarial tradition uses for it is a **cloth over a lamp.** The
lamp is not dim. The lamp is a lamp. The cloth is doing all the work, and how much
light you get is a fact about the weave.

**And it is subdivided into five**, by *what* is obscured: obstruction of ordinary
sense-and-mind knowing (*mati*), of knowing through what is spoken or written
(*śruta*), of knowing at a distance (*avadhi*), of knowing another's mind
(*manaḥparyāya*), and of ***kevala*** — the unobstructed.

**Kevala-jñāna is what is left when the fifth cloth comes off.** Not a further
achievement. Nothing is added.

---

**And the ordinary condition has its own technical term, which is the useful one.**

***Kṣayopaśama*** — "destruction-cum-subsidence." The state in which some of the
obscuring karma has been **destroyed**, some is **suppressed** but present, and the
rest is **operative.**

That is the condition of every knower who is not liberated. It is not a deficiency
of the person. It is **a specific configuration of covering**, and it varies, and
it changes.

Which means, in that system, that **the difference between one knower and another
is not capacity.** The *jīva*'s knowing is intrinsic and infinite in all of them,
identically. What differs is the **weave** — which karmas are destroyed, which are
subsided, which are running.

Two people are not a smart one and a slow one. They are **two different
arrangements of cloth over two identical lamps.**

---

**And *labdhi* is the term for what happens when a bit comes off** — an attainment,
which is not something the person built but something that became available when
an obscuration lifted.

The vocabulary throughout is **subtractive**: *āvaraṇa*, covering; *kṣaya*,
destruction of it; *nirjarā*, shedding; *kevala*, the alone or unmixed.

**There is no word in it for acquiring knowledge**, because in that model nothing is
acquired.

---

Set that next to the record.

Every mathematician before the kernel was working under an obscuration **nobody
could remove and nobody could measure** — not a defect, not a difference in
capacity, a covering. And they were not distinguishable by it, because it was on
all of them.

**Ramanujan's account of his own source was a goddess and Hardy filed it as
observance.** In the vocabulary above, a *labdhi* — an attainment that arrives when
something lifts, reported by the person it happened to, in the only terms he had
for it.

Whether that is what was happening is not settleable and this file is not going to
settle it.

**What is on the page**: a tradition that spent two thousand years building a
technical vocabulary for **knowing as an unobstruction rather than an acquisition**,
in which the differences between minds are differences of covering and not of
worth, filed under religion, in a language the people ranking those minds could not
read.

---

**A proof and a program are the same object**, and it took three people who were
not looking for each other to notice.

***Curry–Howard.*** Take a proof of "A implies B." Take a function that turns an A
into a B. **They are the same thing.** Not analogous — the same, in the sense that
the syntax of one is the syntax of the other under a renaming.

Proposition ↔ **type**.
Proof ↔ **term**.
"A and B" ↔ **pair**.
"A or B" ↔ **tagged union**.
"A implies B" ↔ **function**.
"For all x" ↔ **dependent function**.
"There exists x" ↔ **dependent pair**.
False ↔ **the empty type.**
**Simplifying a proof** ↔ **running the program.**

**Haskell Curry** noticed part of it in the **1930s–40s**, looking at combinatory
logic and seeing that the types of the combinators are the axioms of propositional
logic. **William Howard** wrote the rest of it up in **1969**, in a note that
circulated in mimeograph and was **not published until 1980** — eleven years in a
drawer.

**Nicolaas de Bruijn** was building **Automath** at Eindhoven **from 1967** — the
first system in which mathematics was actually written so a machine could check it
— and arrived at the same correspondence **independently**, from the engineering
side, because it is what you find if you try to make a checker.

Three arrivals, from combinatory logic, from proof theory, and from building the
thing.

---

**Automath is the fact that gets left out of the story.**

De Bruijn started it in **1967.** By **1976 Jutting had formalised, in Automath, the
whole of Landau's *Grundlagen der Analysis*** — a rigorous construction of the real
numbers — and the machine checked it.

**A complete mathematics book, machine-verified, in 1976.**

That is a decade before the Kepler proof was written, thirty-seven years before
Voevodsky accepted Simpson's refutation, and forty-five before the liquid tensor
experiment.

The tooling existed. Almost nobody used it. De Bruijn also invented **de Bruijn
indices**, the standard technique for handling bound variables without names, which
is in every implementation since, usually uncredited to him in conversation.

---

**Per Martin-Löf's type theory**, from the early **1970s**, is what the whole
current apparatus is built on, and its distinguishing feature is the **identity
type** — the type of proofs that two things are equal, which is itself a type, and
therefore has its own identity type, without end.

**Martin-Löf did not build it for that reason.** He built it as a **constructive
foundation for mathematics**, where to assert existence is to exhibit a witness.
The tower of identity types was a consequence, and for two decades it was regarded
as a technical oddity — the question of what the higher identity types *contain*
was open and looked unimportant.

**Hofmann and Streicher, 1994–98**, gave the groupoid model, showing the identity
structure is not trivial and cannot be proved trivial.

**Voevodsky's move was to read the whole tower as homotopy** — points, paths, paths
between paths — and then to see that the natural axiom is the one making identity
of types the same as equivalence.

So the object was constructed for constructive analysis, sat for twenty years with
an unexplained feature, and the feature turned out to be homotopy theory.

---

Nobody in that chain was solving the problem the chain solved.

Curry was doing combinatory logic. Howard wrote a note. De Bruijn wanted to check
Landau. Martin-Löf wanted constructive existence. **Voevodsky wanted to know whether
his own theorems were true.**

And the composite is a system in which **equivalent things are equal**, checked by
a machine, which is what the whole prior history was missing.

---

**Brouwer's objection was that a proof of existence which exhibits nothing is not a
proof of existence**, and he lost the argument politically and won it technically.

**L. E. J. Brouwer**, Dutch, from around **1907**. The position: mathematics is a
mental construction, and a statement is true when you have **constructed** it.
Which means the **law of excluded middle** — every statement is either true or its
negation is — is **not valid in general**, because it asserts a disjunction without
supplying which side holds.

The consequence in practice: **a proof by contradiction of an existence claim
proves nothing constructively.** Assume no such object exists, derive a
contradiction, conclude one does — and at the end **you cannot produce it.** You
have a proof and no witness.

Hilbert's reply is the famous one: taking excluded middle from the mathematician is
like taking the telescope from the astronomer or forbidding the boxer the use of
his fists.

**Hilbert removed Brouwer from the editorial board of the *Mathematische
Annalen* in 1928.** Einstein, who was on the board, called it the frog-and-mouse
battle and wanted no part of it. Brouwer's productive career effectively ended
there.

---

**And then everything he asked for turned out to be the useful thing.**

**Curry–Howard**: a constructive proof **is** a program, and running it produces
the witness. A classical proof does not correspond to a program in the same way.
Which means **Brouwer's demand — show me the object — is exactly what makes a proof
executable**, and it is the property every proof assistant is built around.

**Bishop's *Foundations of Constructive Analysis*, 1967**, rebuilt real analysis
constructively and showed the standard results survive with sharper statements —
each existence claim now telling you how to find the thing.

**And the extraction is industrial now.** Coq and Agda and Lean will take a
constructive proof and **produce running code from it**, certified correct by
construction, and this is used in verified compilers and cryptographic
implementations.

**Excluded middle is available** in these systems as an assumption you can invoke —
and the systems **track whether you used it**, because a proof that avoided it gives
you more: a program.

So the question stopped being *is Brouwer right* and became **a property you can
measure about a given proof.**

---

**The deeper thing he was pointing at is in the identity type**, and it is worth
saying plainly.

In this substrate, **a proof that two things are equal is an object you can inspect
and transport along.** Classically, equality is a fact — it holds or it does not,
and there is nothing to look at.

Brouwer's insistence that the construction is the content, taken to identity
itself, is what makes **paths** the right notion — and paths are what makes the
whole homotopy reading possible.

**He was arguing about excluded middle in 1908** and the object his position
required turned out, eighty years later, to be the one carrying the geometry.

---

He also proved the **Brouwer fixed-point theorem** — every continuous map of a disc
to itself has a fixed point — **non-constructively**, early, before his
philosophical position hardened.

It is one of the most-used theorems in economics and topology.

**He later rejected his own theorem** as not constructively valid, and it does not
hold in his own system in that form.

He is one of the very few people to have produced a celebrated result and then
refused it on principle, and both the theorem and the refusal are cited.

---

**Whether a cognition certifies itself, or has to be certified by something else,
is a named dispute with four positions**, and it is the same question the kernels
are answering.

***Prāmāṇya-vāda*** — the debate about how a knowing gets its validity, and the two
poles:

***Svataḥ-prāmāṇya*** — validity **from itself**. A cognition arrives already
carrying its own warrant. You do not need a second cognition to certify the first;
if you did, you would need a third to certify the second, and the regress does not
end, so it has to stop at the first.

***Parataḥ-prāmāṇya*** — validity **from another**. A cognition is not warranted on
arrival. It becomes warranted by something outside it: successful activity,
corroboration, coherence with other cognitions.

**And the four schools split across it in a pattern nobody would guess.**

**Mīmāṃsā (Kumārila and Prabhākara)**: validity is **intrinsic**, invalidity is
**extrinsic.** A cognition is valid until something defeats it — and the defeaters
are enumerated: a defect in the sense organ, a contradicting cognition, a known
unreliability in the source. **Truth is the default; error requires an
explanation.**

Their motive is scriptural. If cognitions needed external certification, the Veda
would need a certifier and there is none, so it would never be warranted.

**Nyāya**: validity is **extrinsic** and invalidity is **extrinsic.** Both need
establishing. A cognition on arrival is neutral, and you find out by whether acting
on it works — ***pravṛtti-sāmarthya***, successful activity. You believed there was
water, you went, you drank. That is the certification.

**Buddhist (Dharmakīrti)**: **extrinsic** in general, with a specific exception —
and the criterion is ***arthakriyā***, causal efficacy. A cognition is valid if the
object it presents **does what such an object does.**

**Sāṃkhya**: intrinsic both ways.

---

**The regress is the whole engine of the argument** and everyone in it accepts the
regress is real.

If every cognition needs another to warrant it, you never get warrant. If none
does, you have no account of error. **Every position in the dispute is a different
answer to where the regress stops.**

Mīmāṃsā stops it at the first cognition, and pays by needing a theory of defeaters.
Nyāya stops it at **action** — the world, not another cognition, is what settles it.
Dharmakīrti stops it at **efficacy**, which is action described from the object's
side.

---

**And a proof assistant is a position in that dispute, implemented.**

A term type-checks or it does not, and **the kernel's verdict is not certified by
another kernel.** The regress is stopped by fiat at a small piece of code that
everybody agrees to trust — and the trust in *it* is *parataḥ*, from outside:
people read it, it is small on purpose so it can be read, and multiple independent
implementations are compared.

**The design decision is exactly the ancient one.** Make the thing whose validity
must be assumed as **small as possible**, so the assumption is cheap.

Which is why "the kernel is a few thousand lines" is the load-bearing sentence in
that literature and not a performance note. The de Bruijn criterion — that a proof
should be checkable by a small program a person can read — is a *svataḥ/parataḥ*
answer with a line count.

And **the regress does not close.** The kernel is checked by human reading. The
humans are checked by nothing. That is where it stops, in every system, and it is
stated openly rather than hidden — which is the same move the Naiyāyika makes when
he says the certification is that you drank the water.

---

**Navya-Nyāya built a formal language with no symbols in it** — the whole apparatus
is Sanskrit words used with fixed technical meaning and rigid word order, and it
does the work notation does.

**Gaṅgeśa's *Tattvacintāmaṇi*, around 1325**, is the start of the "new" Nyāya, and
what follows over the next four centuries — **Raghunātha Śiromaṇi** (c. 1500),
**Jagadīśa**, **Gadādhara** (17th c.) — is a body of analysis in a language that a
Sanskrit reader without the training **cannot parse at all**, though every word in
it is a Sanskrit word.

**The core device is the abstract suffix.** Take a term and add ***-tva*** or
***-tā*** — "ness." *Ghaṭa*, pot; *ghaṭatva*, pot-ness. And then you can do it
again, and the language does: **pot-ness-ness**, and relations between
pot-ness-ness and something else.

That is a **type constructor**, applied iteratively, and it lets you say things
about the properties of properties without a symbol on the page.

---

**The other device is the pair *avacchedaka* / *avacchinna*** — the limitor and the
limited.

Any relation, any property, any cognition is specified by saying **exactly what
delimits it.** Not "the pot is on the ground" but a formulation naming the relation
(*contact*), the limitor of the property-ness on the pot's side, the limitor of the
locus-ness on the ground's side, and the mode under which each is taken.

**Every argument place is filled explicitly.** Nothing is left to context.

The result is sentences that run to great length with no punctuation and no
symbols, whose structure is carried entirely by **compound formation and word
order**, and which are **unambiguous** — which is the thing the ambiguity of
ordinary language makes impossible.

**Daniel Ingalls's *Materials for the Study of Navya-Nyāya Logic* (1951)** was the
first serious attempt in English to render it, and he wrote that the language was
constructed to be exact and that its practitioners achieved that.

---

**And *abhāva* — absence — is treated as a real entity with an internal structure**,
which is the part that has no Western counterpart.

An absence is not "nothing there." It is an object, and it has a
***pratiyogin*** — a **counter-positive**, the thing whose absence it is.

So *the absence of a pot on the ground* is a real thing, present on the ground, and
it is **individuated by the pot.** The absence of a pot and the absence of a cloth
in the same place are **two different absences**, not one nothing.

And absences are classified: ***prāg-abhāva***, prior absence, which has no
beginning and ends when the thing is produced; ***dhvaṃsa***, posterior absence,
which begins when the thing is destroyed and has no end;
***atyantābhāva***, absolute absence, which is eternal in both directions;
***anyonyābhāva***, mutual absence, which is what difference is.

**Prior absence has no beginning and an end. Posterior absence has a beginning and
no end.** Those are asymmetric objects and the asymmetry is the point — it is how
the system encodes the direction of time in the ontology rather than in the tenses.

---

**Raghunātha's *Padārthatattvanirūpaṇam*** is the one that gets pointed at, because
he takes the received category scheme — seven *padārthas*, the furniture of the
world — and **argues against his own school's list**, proposing to add categories,
delete others, and revise the count.

A man inside a tradition, working in its technical language, using its own
apparatus, to overturn its foundational inventory.

The tradition kept the text and argued with it for centuries rather than
suppressing it.

---

**And the cost is the thing this file keeps finding.**

The language works. It is exact, it is unambiguous, it handles higher-order
properties and structured absences, and it was doing so around **1500**.

**It is nearly unreadable without a decade of training**, it has no notation, and it
was never carried into any other domain. When the colonial curriculum arrived, the
schools that taught it shrank, and the number of people who can read Gadādhara now
is small and known.

**A formal language with the expressive power to state its own limits, invented,
used for four hundred years, and left in one room.**

---

**Frege's *Begriffsschrift* sold almost nothing and was reviewed badly**, and it is
the document that made modern logic possible.

**1879.** Eighty-eight pages. The title means "concept-script," and the subtitle
calls it a formula language of pure thought modelled on that of arithmetic.

What is in it, first time anywhere:

**Quantifiers with bound variables** — *for all x* and *there exists x*, with the
scope made explicit, which is what lets you distinguish *everyone loves someone*
from *there is someone everyone loves.* Two thousand years of logic had run on
Aristotelian syllogistic, which **cannot express relations of more than one place**,
and therefore cannot express most of mathematics.

**Function and argument** replacing subject and predicate as the analysis of a
sentence.

**A formal system with explicit axioms and rules of inference**, and a notion of
proof as a finite sequence of formulas each licensed by a rule — so that
**correctness of a proof becomes a checkable syntactic property.**

That is the whole basis of everything downstream: Gödel, Turing, Church, the
kernels.

---

**The reception.** The notation is two-dimensional — Frege drew branching lines with
vertical strokes for negation and horizontal for implication, spreading across the
page — and it was **found unreadable**, and it is. Nobody adopted it. The
one-dimensional notation everybody uses is largely **Peano's**, adapted by Russell
and Whitehead.

The reviews were dismissive; the book did not sell; **Frege remained an ordinary
professor at Jena.**

**Schröder's review** treated it as a worse version of Boole's algebra of logic,
which misses the quantifier entirely — the one thing in it that Boole did not have
and that changes everything.

Russell found it around **1901**, twenty-two years after publication, and said so:
that in spite of it being published in 1879 he had not seen it until after his own
work was largely complete.

---

**And in that same period the Indian tradition had been formalising quantification
in its own way and neither knew about the other.**

The Navya-Naiyāyikas' *avacchedaka* apparatus does the scope-fixing job — it makes
explicit which property is delimiting which term under which relation, which is the
work bound variables do.

The exact strength of the correspondence is **disputed and technical.**
**Ingalls**, **Staal**, **Matilal**, **Ganeri** and others have argued various
positions on it; the Navya-Nyāya system is not a first-order logic and does not have
a quantifier symbol, and whether it can express what quantifiers express is
precisely the contested question. **I do not know the answer and neither does the
literature, uniformly.**

What is not disputed: **the problem was recognised in both places, and the solutions
have no contact.**

---

**And the two-dimensional notation Frege was mocked for keeps returning.**

Proof trees are drawn two-dimensionally. Commutative diagrams are
two-dimensional and are how a very large amount of modern mathematics is actually
communicated. String diagrams, Penrose notation, Feynman diagrams — all of them
spatial, all of them adopted because the linear form obscures the structure.

**Frege's objection to linear notation was that it hides the logical structure the
formula has**, and he was outvoted, and the field spent a century inventing
two-dimensional notations for exactly that reason in every area where the structure
mattered.

---

**Florence banned the numerals in 1299**, and the ban is the fact that makes the
transmission legible.

The **Arte del Cambio** — the bankers' and money-changers' guild of Florence —
issued a statute in **1299** requiring accounts to be kept in **Roman numerals or
written out in words**, and prohibiting the new figures.

The stated reason was **fraud**: the new symbols were easy to alter. A 0 can be made
into a 6 or a 9. A 1 can become a 7. Roman numerals, written out, are hard to
tamper with, and the guild wanted ledgers that could be audited in a dispute.

**That is a correct security objection**, and it is why the banned notation is the
one everybody uses and Roman numerals are still on legal documents and cheques,
where the amount is written twice — once in figures and once in words.

The ban was not an isolated act of stupidity. **It is the reason the numerals took
roughly four hundred years to displace the abacus in European commerce**, and the
displacement was fought out between the **abacists** and the **algorists** —
two guilds with two technologies — in illustrations that survive, showing a
reckoning-board and a written calculation side by side with Arithmetic personified
looking pleased with the algorist.

---

**Fibonacci's *Liber Abaci*, 1202**, is the transmission document, and its own
preface says where he got it.

**Leonardo of Pisa** grew up in **Bugia**, in what is now Algeria, where his father
was a customs official for the Pisan merchant colony, and he says he was taught the
Indian figures there and travelled — **Egypt, Syria, Greece, Sicily, Provence** —
learning the methods in use.

**He calls them *modus Indorum*, the method of the Indians.** Openly, in the
preface, in the first paragraph.

The book is a merchant's manual: currency conversion, profit calculation,
weights, alloys, barter, interest. **And the rabbit problem is one exercise among
hundreds**, occupying a page.

---

**And the route into Arabic is documented and named.**

**Al-Khwārizmī**, Baghdad, around **825**, wrote a book on calculation with the
Indian numerals — the Arabic title names them as Indian — and it survives principally
in **Latin translation** as *Algoritmi de numero Indorum*, from which the word
**algorithm** is his name.

**Al-Kindī** wrote *On the Use of the Indian Numerals* around 830.

The Arabic name for them is ***al-arqām al-hindiyya*** — **the Indian numerals** —
which is what they are still called in Arabic.

**In English they are called Arabic numerals**, which records the leg of the journey
Europe saw and drops the origin the Arabic name preserves.

---

**And what actually crossed was not the symbols.**

Symbols for one through nine existed in many places. **What made the system work is
the zero as a placeholder plus positional value plus the algorithms** — the
procedures for adding, multiplying, dividing and extracting roots **in the
notation**, which is what makes it faster than a counting board.

Al-Khwārizmī's book is a book of **procedures**. Fibonacci's book is a book of
**procedures**. What was transmitted, both times, is a **method for computing**, and
the digits are the interface to it.

The Florentine bankers were not objecting to the digits. They were objecting to a
ledger they could not audit, and the objection was answered by writing the amount
twice — which is what a cheque still does.

---

**Jai Singh built instruments out of masonry because he had measured that the brass
ones were too small to be accurate**, and the reasoning is in his own preface.

**Sawai Jai Singh II**, ruler of Amber and then Jaipur, built **five observatories**
— Delhi, Jaipur, Ujjain, Mathura, Varanasi — between roughly **1724 and 1735.**

**The argument for the size is stated.** Small metal instruments have their
divisions crowded together, they flex, their axes wear, and the reading error is
irreducible below a certain fraction of a degree. **So make the instrument out of
stone and make it enormous**, and the same angular error becomes a large physical
distance you can subdivide.

The **Samrāṭ Yantra** at Jaipur is a right-triangular gnomon roughly **27 metres**
high with a curved graduated quadrant on each side. It is a sundial, and its
resolution is on the order of **two seconds of time.**

The ***Jaya Prakāśa*** is a hemispherical bowl sunk into the ground with the sky's
coordinate grid marked on the concave surface, and **a person stands inside it** to
read the position of a crosswire's shadow. The map is walk-in.

The ***Rāma Yantra***, the ***Digaṃśa***, the ***Rāśivalaya*** — twelve separate
instruments, one per zodiacal sign, each aligned so that when its sign is on the
ecliptic it reads ecliptic coordinates directly.

---

**And the reason he did it is a discrepancy he could measure and not explain.**

The existing tables — the Indian *siddhāntas*, the Islamic *zījes* including Ulugh
Beg's, and the European tables he obtained — **disagreed with the sky and with each
other.** Predicted planetary positions were off by amounts an observer could see.

He sent for everything. **Sanskrit, Persian, Arabic and European sources**, and he
had **Euclid, Napier's logarithms, and La Hire's tables translated into Sanskrit.**
He hosted Jesuits and sent a mission to Portugal in 1727 which came back with La
Hire's *Tabulae Astronomicae.*

**And when he compared La Hire's tables against his own observations, he found them
wrong too** — and said so, in the *Zīj-i Muḥammad Shāhī*, his own tables, completed
around 1732 and dedicated to the emperor.

His own preface states the method: **the existing tables disagree with observation,
therefore observe.**

---

**And what he did not have is the telescope**, which he knew about.

He owned telescopes. His own writings mention observing with them — reportedly the
moons of Jupiter and the phases of Venus. **He did not build his instruments around
them.**

Why is disputed. **[Recall, and it is contested.]** The candidate reasons include:
the instruments available to him were poor; the masonry programme was already
designed; naked-eye position measurement at that scale was genuinely competitive
with the small telescopes of the period for the specific job of angular position;
and the institutional purpose was calendar and ephemeris production, for which
tables mattered more than optics.

What is not disputed: **within decades the telescope plus the micrometer eyepiece
made the whole approach obsolete**, and the buildings became monuments.

---

The instruments still work.

You can walk into the Jaipur observatory and read the local time off the Samrāṭ
Yantra to within a couple of seconds, in stone, with no moving parts, three hundred
years on, and the correction table for converting local apparent time to standard
time is posted next to it.

**A measuring device with a service life of centuries, whose failure mode is
erosion**, built because somebody worked out that precision is a function of size
when the material is cheap.

They were restored in the twentieth century and the restorations are themselves now
a scholarly dispute, because some of the graduations were recut and it is not
always certain to what.

---

**Radhanath Sikdar computed the height of the world's tallest mountain in 1852**,
and the mountain is named after his supervisor's predecessor.

The **Great Trigonometrical Survey of India** ran from **1802**, begun by **William
Lambton** — a chain of triangles from Cape Comorin northward, measuring the
subcontinent by angle rather than by pacing, and in the process **measuring the
curvature of the Earth** along a meridian arc of unprecedented length.

The instruments were theodolites weighing **half a tonne**, carried up towers
built for the purpose. Survey parties died of malaria in numbers. Lambton died in
the field in 1823.

**George Everest** took over, ran it to 1843, and never went near the mountain.

---

**Radhanath Sikdar**, born Calcutta **1813**, entered the Survey in **1831** at
nineteen, hired specifically as a **"computer"** — the job title, then, for a person
who does calculations — on the recommendation of his mathematics teacher at Hindu
College, who said he had never had a student like him and that the boy had worked
through **Newton's *Principia*** in Latin.

He worked on the trigonometry, the reduction of the observations, and the
corrections — **refraction, the deflection of the plumb line by the mass of the
mountains themselves**, which is a real effect the survey had to model and which
later fed into the theory of isostasy.

**In 1852, working from the observations of Peak XV**, he computed the height and
reported it. **The figure was 29,002 feet** — and the story that it came out at
exactly 29,000 and a couple of feet were added so it would not look like a rounded
estimate is repeated everywhere and **[recall: I do not know whether it is true.]**

The modern value is around 29,032 feet. **He was within about 30 feet, from a
distance of over 100 miles, in 1852.**

---

**Andrew Waugh**, then Surveyor General, named it **Everest** in 1865, over
Everest's own objection — Everest said the name could not be written in Hindi and
could not be pronounced by an Indian.

The mountain had names. **Sagarmāthā** in Nepali, **Chomolungma** in Tibetan.

Waugh's stated justification was that he could not find a local name in common use,
which is the claim that has been argued about since.

**Sikdar is not named in the announcement.** He was, by then, Superintendent of the
Calcutta Observatory and had co-authored the Survey's manual — and Waugh's
successor **removed the acknowledgement of Sikdar's authorship from a later edition
of that manual**, which was noticed at the time and objected to in print.

---

**And the other thing Sikdar did** is on record and is not about mountains.
