He wrote publicly, in Bengali, against the treatment of Indian labourers by British
employers, and **testified in a case** on behalf of coolies, and was fined for
contempt after an argument with a magistrate over it.

He co-edited ***Masik Patrika***, a Bengali monthly, aimed explicitly at readers
including **women**, and written in a deliberately plain Bengali rather than the
Sanskritised register, so that people who were not scholars could read it.

The man who computed the height of Everest also ran a magazine in easy Bengali for
women, and got fined for arguing with a magistrate about the treatment of workers.

He died in 1870.

---

**Mahalanobis worked out how to know a country's crop yield without counting it**,
and the technique is now how every large survey on earth is done.

**Prasanta Chandra Mahalanobis**, born **1893**. Trained in physics at Cambridge,
came home in 1915, and got into statistics **by accident** — he found bound volumes
of *Biometrika* in the King's College library and read them on the boat.

**Founded the Indian Statistical Institute in 1931**, in a room, with a handful of
people.

---

**The problem: how much jute is Bengal going to produce this year.**

The existing method was a **complete enumeration** — send officials to every
holding — which is expensive, slow, and, because it is expensive and slow, **done
badly**, so the totals were unreliable in ways nobody could quantify.

**Mahalanobis's answer, from around 1937**: **do not count everything. Sample.**

And the part that made it work is that he did the **cost calculation** as part of
the design. He formalised the trade-off between the number of sampling units and
the effort per unit — the **cost function** — and derived the optimum: given a fixed
budget, how many plots, of what size, arranged how, minimises the variance of the
estimate.

Then **pilot surveys** to estimate the variance components before committing.

And the check that made it credible: **interpenetrating network of subsamples** —
his term — where the survey is run as **several independent replicates by different
teams**, so the spread between the replicates **measures the survey's own error
directly**, including the enumerators' mistakes and biases, without needing a theory
of them.

**A survey that measures its own accuracy from the inside, by duplication.**

---

**That is now standard.** Sample surveys with cost-based design and replicated
subsamples underpin national accounts, unemployment figures, agricultural
statistics and opinion polling everywhere.

The **National Sample Survey**, from 1950, is one of the largest continuous
household survey operations in the world.

**And the point of failure it was designed against is the one it still catches**:
not sampling error, which the mathematics handles, but **the enumerator** —
fatigue, shortcuts, misunderstanding, fabrication — which no amount of theory
addresses and which replication makes visible.

---

**The Mahalanobis distance**, 1936, is the other thing with his name on it.

Ordinary distance between two points treats every dimension as equally important
and independent. **His measure divides through by the covariance** — so directions
in which the data naturally varies a lot count for less, and directions in which it
barely varies count for more.

It came out of a specific problem: **comparing skull and body measurements between
populations**, where the individual measurements are strongly correlated and using
plain Euclidean distance double-counts.

It is now in outlier detection, classification, and cluster analysis everywhere,
and is the natural metric whenever the coordinates are not independent.

---

**And he built the institute as a working place rather than a department.**

The ISI ran its own field operations, trained its own enumerators, built computing
capacity — and Mahalanobis was, from 1955, the person who put the statistical
apparatus behind Indian planning, and drafted the Second Five-Year Plan's
mathematical frame.

He also, in **1954**, brought **Ronald Fisher** to Calcutta, and Fisher visited
repeatedly. They corresponded for decades.

Fisher — whose statistics were built for eugenics — and Mahalanobis, whose statistics
were built to find out how much rice a country had.

---

**The Eleusinian Mysteries ran for roughly a millennium and a half, initiated
thousands of people a year, were open to women and slaves, and almost nothing about
the content survives.**

That combination is the fact worth holding.

**Open**: anyone who spoke Greek and had not committed murder could be initiated.
Women, slaves, foreigners. There were two grades — the Lesser Mysteries at Agrai in
spring, the Greater at Eleusis in autumn — and the procession from Athens to
Eleusis, about **fourteen miles**, was a public event on the calendar that everybody
in Attica could watch.

**Nothing hidden about its existence.** The sanctuary is a large building. The date
was published. The route was a road.

**And the prohibition was on *saying*.** Divulging carried a death penalty.
**Aeschylus** was reportedly prosecuted for revealing something in a play and
acquitted by pleading he had not known it was secret. **Alcibiades** was prosecuted
in **415 BCE** for performing the rites profanely in a private house.

Roughly **1,500 years** — from about the eighth century BCE to Alaric's sack in
**396 CE** and the closure under Theodosius — and what survives of the content is:
that a **kykeon** was drunk, that things were shown, said and done, and a handful of
contested phrases.

**Thousands of witnesses a year, for fifteen centuries, and the content did not
leak in any recoverable form.**

Not because it was guarded well. Because **what was transmitted was not the kind of
thing a sentence carries** — and everyone who had it had also been told not to say
it, and apparently did not.

---

**And the Pythagorean case has the same structure with the schism recorded.**

**Pythagoras**, c. 570–495 BCE, Samos to Croton. **Nothing written by him
survives**, and it is disputed whether he wrote.

After him the school **split**, and both halves claimed to be the true heirs:

**The *akousmatikoi***, the **hearers**, who held the ***akousmata*** — oral
sayings, transmitted verbatim, no explanation given. Aristotle collected them and
the collection is lost but is quoted. They come in three forms: **what is it, what
is most X, and what must be done.**

*What is the oracle at Delphi? **The tetraktys, which is the harmony in which the
Sirens sing.*** *What are the isles of the blessed? **Sun and moon.***

And the conduct rules, which read as arbitrary and were transmitted without reasons:
do not step over a yoke, do not poke a fire with a knife, do not look at yourself in
a mirror beside a lamp, **abstain from beans.**

**The *mathēmatikoi***, the **learners**, who did the demonstrations — number,
harmonics, geometry, astronomy.

The akousmatikoi said the mathēmatikoi were not Pythagoreans at all but followers of
Hippasus. The mathēmatikoi said the akousmatikoi had the sayings without
understanding them.

**A split between the people who kept the transmission and the people who did the
reasoning**, each accusing the other of having lost the thing.

---

**Hippasus** is the one the tradition puts the cost on. The story — late, in
Iamblichus and others, and **not reliable as history** — is that he disclosed
something, either the incommensurability of the diagonal or the construction of the
dodecahedron, and **drowned at sea.**

Whether or not it happened, it is the tradition's own account of what disclosure
costs, preserved by the tradition.

**Philolaus**, c. 470–385, is the first whose writing survives in fragments — and he
put a **central fire** at the centre of the cosmos rather than the earth, with the
earth in orbit around it. **Archytas** of Tarentum, c. 428–347, friend of Plato,
solved the duplication of the cube by a three-dimensional construction, worked on
acoustics, and was elected general seven years running.

---

**And Plato's own statement about transmissibility is in the Seventh Letter**, and
it is specific.

He says of the serious matters that there is **no writing of his about them and
never will be**, because it is **not something that can be put into words like other
studies** — and that it comes, after long association and living together in the
pursuit, **suddenly, like a light kindled from a leaping spark, and thereafter
nourishes itself.**

*Exaiphnēs* — suddenly. And the condition given for it is not intelligence. It is
**συνουσία**, being together, over a long time, at the thing.

That is the same condition as the residential decade in a teacher's house, and the
same condition as the ward that only worked once there was a café in it, and it is
the reason the content of a tradition of that kind is not in its documents.

---

**Dīkṣā** is not a ceremony marking that you have learned something. In the
literature it is described as an **operation performed on the person**, and the
word is glossed etymologically as *dā* plus *kṣi* — **what is given and what is
destroyed.**

Something is transmitted and something is removed, in one act.

**In the Śaiva Siddhānta texts the mechanism is spelled out in technical terms.**
The *guru*, in the rite, **enters the disciple's channel**, locates the karmic
bonds — the *malas* — and **burns the seeds that have not yet begun to fruit**,
leaving only the *prārabdha*, the portion already in motion, which has to be lived
out because it is already running.

That is a stated causal claim with a stated exception, not a blessing. And the
exception is the interesting part: **the karma already in flight cannot be
touched**, which is why the initiated still get ill and still suffer and the texts
say so in advance.

**Nirvāṇa-dīkṣā** is the grade that is held to guarantee liberation at death; there
are lesser grades for lesser commitments.

---

**And the class question is in the texts as a live fight.**

The Vedic *upanayana* — the thread ceremony, the initiation into study — was
restricted by birth and by sex. **Tantric and Śaiva initiation was, in many
lineages, explicitly not**, and the texts argue about it: some state that
*dīkṣā* is open to all four *varṇas* and to women, and that after initiation the
prior caste identity **no longer determines ritual standing.**

Whether it worked out that way in practice is a separate question with a mixed
record.

**Abhinavagupta**, around 1000 in Kashmir, argues the point in the
*Tantrāloka* — and he is the same man who wrote the aesthetics.

---

***Samaya*** is the other half and it is a **contract**.

In the Vajrayāna, receiving an empowerment — *abhiṣeka* — creates binding
commitments: to the teacher, to the practice, to secrecy about specific contents,
and to continuing. **Breaking them has stated consequences.**

**And the secrecy has a stated reason that is not mystification.** The reason given
is that the practices are **dangerous when done without the transmission** — that
the material is calibrated to a person by someone who has assessed them, and that
the same instruction given to somebody unprepared produces harm rather than
nothing.

Which is a claim you can agree with or not, and it is the same claim the Chishtis
made about *samāʿ*, and the same reason the ustad withheld a phrase, and it is not
distinguishable **from the outside** from simple gatekeeping.

**The traditions themselves knew that and argued about it.**

---

**And the counter-tradition is inside all of them.**

The **Sahajiyā** and **Bāul** currents in Bengal, the **Nāth** siddhas, the
**mahāsiddhas** — cobblers, hunters, a man who sold arrows, a woman who sold wine —
whose whole position is that the realisation is ***sahaja***, spontaneous, innate,
**not conferred**, and that the apparatus of initiation is an obstruction.

The **caryāpadas** — Old Bengali/Apabhraṃśa songs from around the 8th–12th
centuries, some of the oldest surviving literature in any Indo-Aryan vernacular —
are their transmission, and they are **songs**, in the spoken tongue, with a
deliberately coded surface: the boatman, the river, the crooked house, the deer
hunting itself.

**Coded and sung.** Not sealed and withheld. Available to anyone in the room and
opaque unless you had been told what the boat is.

**Kabīr's *ulaṭbāṃsī*** — "upside-down verses" — do the same: *the frog eats the
snake, the fish climbs the tree, the cow suckles the calf and the calf drinks the
cow.*

A transmission strategy that is **public, memorable, singable, and not
interpretable without the key** — which is a different solution to the same problem
than an oath is, and it survives in the vernacular precisely because nobody had to
be trusted with it.

---

**Lālan Fakir** ran a community in Bengal for most of the nineteenth century, wrote
somewhere between hundreds and a couple of thousand songs, **refused to state his
religion when asked directly**, and the songs are still sung by people who have
never heard his name attached to them.

Born around **1774** [the date is disputed and so is nearly everything about his
origins]. **Died 1890**, at Chheuriya in Kushtia, in what is now Bangladesh, aged
by tradition around 116.

**Whether he was born Hindu or Muslim is unknown and was unknown then.** The
accounts have him falling ill on a pilgrimage, being abandoned, and being taken in
and nursed by a Muslim family — and after that he does not belong to either
community in the way the register required.

**His answer to the question, in a song, is the fact:** *sab loke koy Lalon ki
jat sangsare* — everyone asks what Lalon's caste is — and the verse works through
the question and concludes that he has never seen the mark of it on anybody, and
that circumcision marks a Muslim man but **what marks a Muslim woman**, and that he
has looked and cannot find where the sign is kept.

He asks the question as a technical objection: **where, physically, is the mark.**

---

**The Bāuls are a lineage without institutions**, deliberately.

No temple, no mosque, no scripture, no fixed liturgy. **The song is the
transmission** and the *ākhṛā* — a compound where practitioners live and sing — is
the unit. Initiation is from a specific teacher, and the doctrine is that the thing
sought is ***maner mānuṣ***, the **man of the heart** — located in the body, not
above it — and that it cannot be reached through text.

They are Muslim and Hindu and neither, sing in Bengali, and **the songs are the
whole doctrine** because there is nothing else.

**Tagore** heard them, wrote about them, collected them, and said that the Bāul
songs had shaped his own writing — and the modern circulation of Lālan's songs runs
partly through that.

**UNESCO listed the tradition in 2005/2008.** The practitioners' own social position
in Bangladesh has been contested, including physical attacks and hair-cutting
assaults on Bāuls in recent decades.

---

***Sandhyā-bhāṣā*** — "twilight language" — is the technical term for the coding,
and it is a real feature and not a later invention.

The Buddhist *caryāgīti* and the Hindu tantric texts use a systematic double
register: ordinary words for body, boat, river, moon, lotus, wine, corpse, woman —
which have a surface meaning and a second meaning fixed by transmission.

**The surface is not nonsense.** It is a coherent song about a boatman crossing a
river. That is the point: **a coded text that is also a good text at the surface**
does not announce that it is coded.

The term itself is contested — whether *sandhyā* means twilight (dim, ambiguous) or
*sandhā*, intentional/enigmatic speech. **Both readings are defended in the
philological literature and I do not know which is right.**

---

**And the whole strategy is a different answer to the transmission problem than the
oath.**

The mystery cult's answer: **broad access, sealed mouths**, and the content does not
survive.

The Bāul and caryā answer: **broadcast it in the vernacular, in a form people will
sing for pleasure whether or not they have the key**, so that the vehicle survives
by being enjoyed, and the key travels separately through persons.

The second one is why **eight-hundred-year-old Bengali songs still exist** in a
tradition with no scripture, no institution, no endowment and no property.

The carrier is the tune. People keep it because it is good, and the meaning rides
along inside it, available to whoever gets told.

---

**Whether the spirituals carried escape instructions is disputed and the dispute is
worth stating properly, because both sides are making a real claim.**

**The popular account**: "Wade in the Water" tells you to walk in the creek so the
dogs lose the scent. "Follow the Drinking Gourd" gives the Big Dipper and a route.
"Steal Away" announces a meeting. Canaan and the Jordan mean the North and the
Ohio.

**The historians' objection** is specific and is not dismissal. The documentary
chain for most of these is **thin and late** — "Follow the Drinking Gourd" first
appears in print in **1928**, collected by H. B. Parks, decades after emancipation,
and the map-song reading traces largely to that. Slave narratives from people who
escaped do not generally describe songs as route instructions. And the strongest
form of the claim — a fixed code, known network-wide — would require a coordination
nobody has evidence for.

**What is documented and not disputed**:

**The double meaning was real and was general.** Frederick Douglass wrote that
"O Canaan, sweet Canaan, I am bound for the land of Canaan" meant something more
than reaching heaven — that for the group he was in, Canaan **meant the North**, and
that the songs were used to communicate intentions the overseer could hear without
understanding.

**That is a first-person statement by a man who used it**, in print, in his own
narrative.

**Harriet Tubman used songs as signals** — this is in the early biographical
accounts drawn from her own testimony: a particular song sung in a particular way to
indicate safe or not safe, timing rather than geography.

**Drums were banned.** After the Stono Rebellion, **South Carolina's Negro Act of
1740** prohibited enslaved people from using drums, horns or other loud instruments
on the ground that they could be used to signal — which is a legislature stating, in
statute, that it believed music was carrying messages.

So: **the coding is documented, the fixed universal codebook is not.**

---

**And the mechanism is the same one as the twilight language, arrived at
independently under the same pressure.**

A surface that is **complete and good on its own terms** — a hymn about the
Jordan is a hymn about the Jordan — carrying a second register **available only to
people who have been told**, in a form that can be performed **in front of the
people it is being kept from.**

The overseer hears worship. The room hears worship and something else.

Which is exactly what a *caryāpada* does with a boat and a river, and what an
*ulaṭbāṃsī* does with a fish climbing a tree.

**Nobody in either case had to keep a secret**, which is the engineering advantage
over an oath. **There is nothing to leak.** The words are already public. What is
not public is the pairing, and a pairing lives in people.

---

**And there is a documented case of the coding being read out and shut down.**

The **Negro Act of 1740** also restricted assembly, movement, and **teaching an
enslaved person to write** — and later Southern states passed anti-literacy laws
outright, with fines and whipping specified, and penalties for the teacher
including for white teachers.

A body of law aimed precisely at **the channels through which anything could be
transmitted that the owner could not read.**

Song survived that legislation and writing did not, and the reason is structural:
**you cannot confiscate a tune, you cannot search a person for it, and it is
indistinguishable from compliance while it is being performed.**

---

**Psychedelic is a constructed compound with a stated intended meaning, and the
meaning it now carries in English is the inverse of what was built.**

**ψυχή** — *psychē*: soul, mind, breath, the life in a thing.
**δηλοῦν** — *dēloun*, from *δῆλος*, *dēlos*: **to make manifest, to make visible,
to reveal what is there.**

**Mind-manifesting.** That is the whole construction, and the coiner said so in
those words.

**The claim inside the word is that nothing is added.** Something already present
is **made visible** — which is a specific position, and it is the same grammar as
*kevala-jñāna* being what remains when the cloth comes off the lamp, and as
*anamnesis* being recollection rather than instruction, and as gnosis being
recognition.

---

**The coinage is documented and it happened in verse, in the post.**

**Humphry Osmond**, a psychiatrist working with mescaline and LSD in
**Saskatchewan** in the 1950s — his research was on alcoholism treatment — was in
correspondence with **Aldous Huxley** about what to call the class.

Huxley proposed ***phanerothyme*** — *phaneroein*, to make visible, plus *thymos*,
spirit — and sent it as a couplet: *To make this trivial world sublime, take half a
gram of phanerothyme.*

**Osmond answered with the word:** *To fathom Hell or soar angelic, just take a
pinch of psychedelic.*

He presented it to the **New York Academy of Sciences in 1957.**

**Both proposals mean nearly the same thing** — spirit-made-visible, soul-made
-visible — arrived at independently by two people in one exchange. Neither of them
proposed a word meaning *altered* or *distorted*.

---

**And the alternatives each encode a whole theory, which is why the terminology
fight was never cosmetic.**

***Hallucinogen*** — from *hallucinari*, **to wander in the mind, to be deceived.**
The word asserts that **what appears is false.**

***Psychotomimetic*** — **psychosis-mimicking.** The dominant research framing of
the 1950s: this is a **model of illness**, and the subject is being made
temporarily mad so the state can be studied.

***Psychedelic*** — **manifesting.** What appears was there.

***Entheogen*** — coined **1979** by Ruck, Bigwood, Staples, Ott and Wasson, from
*entheos*, **having the god within**, plus *-genēs*: **generating the divine
within.** Coined explicitly to escape both the deception implied by *hallucinogen*
and the accumulated cultural freight of *psychedelic*.

**Four words. Four complete and incompatible accounts of the same event**:
deception, illness, revelation, divinity. Choosing the term is choosing the theory,
and the field's preferred word moved as its preferred theory moved.

---

**What happened to the word in English is the misnomer.**

Read from the context it circulated in rather than from its roots, *psychedelic*
came to mean **swirling, distorted, unreal, drug-associated** — a colour scheme, a
guitar tone, a poster font.

**That is the opposite of what the compound says.** A word meaning *shows you what
is there* now means *makes things look like they are not.*

The reading was supplied by the surroundings and it **overwrote a stated
construction**, and the construction is recoverable from the roots by anyone who
looks, and almost nobody looks, because it presents as an ordinary English adjective
and nothing in its English form says *go check the Greek*.

---

**And the older material sits next to it unresolved.**

***Soma***. **Ṛgveda Maṇḍala 9 is 114 hymns and the entire book is addressed to
it** — pressed, filtered through wool, mixed with milk, and drunk, with the effects
described in the first person. *We have drunk soma, we have become immortal, we have
gone to the light.*

**What the plant was is not known.** Ephedra, a mushroom, syrian rue, and others
have been argued; **Wasson proposed *Amanita muscaria* in 1968**; the identification
is unsettled and the ritual use lapsed early enough that the tradition itself
substitutes.

An entire book of the oldest text in the corpus is about a substance nobody can now
identify.

**And the *kykeon* at Eleusis.** Wasson, **Albert Hofmann** — the chemist who
synthesised LSD, which is derived from **ergot** — and Carl Ruck proposed in
***The Road to Eleusis*** (1978) that the barley drink was ergotised.

**That hypothesis is disputed and unproven.** It is chemically arguable and
historically unevidenced, and specialists in Greek religion have largely not
accepted it. It is on the page as a proposal with a named objection, not as a
finding.

---

**Every soteriological term in every one of these systems is a subtraction word.
Not one of them is an acquisition word.** The list is checkable and it is short.

***Nirvāṇa*** — *nir* + *vā*, **to blow out.** A flame going out. The word is a fire
metaphor and nothing else.

And the physics behind it matters, because in the period's account **a fire that
goes out is not annihilated** — it becomes unmanifest. The
***Aggi-Vacchagotta Sutta*** (Majjhima Nikāya 72) is the passage: the Buddha asks
Vaccha where a fire goes when it goes out — north, south, east, west? — and Vaccha
says **the question does not apply**, the fire went out for want of fuel. That is
given as the answer to what becomes of a liberated being after death.

**And the fuel is the same word as the clinging.** ***Upādāna*** means both
**grasping** and **fuel**. It is one word carrying both, and it is the second-to-last
link in dependent origination. A being burns because it takes up fuel; *nirvāṇa* is
the fuel running out.

**The pun is the doctrine**, and it is not available in translation.

*(The tradition also reads it as ***nir-vāna***, **un-binding** — *vāna* as
binding — and that reading is used by translators who want to avoid the
extinction connotation in English. Both etymologies are inside the tradition.)*

***Mokṣa*** — from **√muc**, **to release, to let loose, to set free.** What is done
to a tethered animal.

***Kaivalya*** — from *kevala*, **alone, unmixed, whole-in-itself.** **Isolation.**
The endpoint of a text called *yoking*.

***Apavarga*** — the Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika term — from *apa* + *√vṛj*, **to turn away, to
cease.** Cessation of the twenty-one kinds of pain, enumerated.

***Nirodha*** — the third noble truth and the definition of yoga at *Yoga Sūtra*
1.2. **Cessation, restraint, stopping.**

***Nirjarā*** — Jain. **Shedding**, wearing away, the falling off of what adheres.

***Kevala-jñāna*** — **the alone-knowing**, what is left when the four obscuring
karmas are destroyed.

***Prapañca-upaśama*** — Madhyamaka. **The stilling of proliferation.**

***Kenōsis*** — Greek, Philippians 2:7. **Emptying.** Applied to a god.

**Nine words, five traditions, and every single one of them names a removal, a
cessation, a release, a shedding, an isolation, an extinguishing, or a stilling.**

---

**There is no word in the set for gaining.**

Not one of these traditions named its goal with a verb of acquisition, and they had
the verbs. *Prāpti*, attainment, exists and is used for lesser things. *Lābha*,
gain, exists. **They are not the words for the endpoint.**

Which means the shared claim, across systems that agree on almost nothing else, is
that **whatever is sought is not absent and does not need to be brought in.**
Something is in the way, and the operation is on the obstruction.

The Jains say the karma is literally material and is removed. The Buddhists say the
fuel runs out. Patañjali says the turning stops and the seer is simply there.
Nyāya says the pain ceases. Nāgārjuna says the proliferation stills.

---

**And what happened to the words in English is the same operation, twice.**

***Nirvana*** in English means **bliss, paradise, a state of perfect happiness** —
and the word means **a fire going out.** The English usage supplies an ecstatic
positive content to a term whose entire construction is negative.

***Psychedelic*** means **mind-manifesting** and English made it mean **distorted.**

Two Greek-and-Sanskrit constructions, both precise, both read from the surrounding
context rather than from what was built — and **in each case the induced meaning is
the inverse of the constructed one.**

One was inverted into more than it claims, one into less.

---

***Rasa* is a food word.** Sap, juice, the liquid in a plant, the taste of a thing
in the mouth. Everything the aesthetics does with it is built on that and the
tradition never hid it.

**The six tastes**, enumerated in the *Caraka Saṃhitā* and standard across the
medical literature:

***madhura*** — sweet.
***amla*** — sour.
***lavaṇa*** — salty.
***kaṭu*** — pungent, the heat of chilli and pepper.
***tikta*** — bitter.
***kaṣāya*** — **astringent**, the pucker of unripe fruit and strong tea, which
English does not have a food word for and which is a distinct category here.

Each is assigned to a pair of the elements — sweet is earth and water, sour is
earth and fire, salty is water and fire, pungent is fire and air, bitter is air and
space, astringent is air and earth — and each has stated effects on the body, so a
meal is composed as a **balance across all six**, not as a flavour preference.

A South Indian meal on a leaf is laid out in that order, and the order is the
digestion sequence.

---

**And Bharata's aesthetic term is a straight borrowing, argued in the cooking
vocabulary.**

The word for what an audience does with *rasa* is ***āsvāda*** — **tasting.** Not
feeling, not perceiving. Tasting.

And the *rasa-sūtra*'s mechanism is stated as **combination**: determinants,
consequents and transient states **combine** and the *rasa* arises, the way a taste
arises in a dish from ingredients none of which has that taste alone.

The commentators make it explicit — the analogy given is the preparation of food,
where the cook combines things and the result is a flavour that is in none of the
parts.

**So the theory of how an audience receives an emotion is stated as a theory of
cooking**, by name, in the technical vocabulary of the kitchen.

---

**And *kaṣāya* is the same word in both places, which is the fact I did not see
coming.**

**In food it is astringency** — the taste that **draws the mouth together**, from
√*kaṣ*, to rub, scrape, draw.

**In Jain doctrine the four *kaṣāyas* are the passions** — *krodha*, *māna*,
*māyā*, *lobha*: anger, pride, deceit, greed — and their technical function is that
they are **what makes karma stick.** *Tattvārthasūtra* 8.3: passion determines
duration and intensity; without *kaṣāya* the influx touches and falls away.

**One word.** Astringency and the binding passions. And the action is identical in
both: **a drawing-together, a contraction, a thing that makes what touches it
adhere.**

The taste that puckers and the affect that binds are the same verb.

**[Not claimed]:** that the medical and the Jain usages were coined with each other
in view. The root is shared and the semantic overlap is exact; whether anybody in
either tradition remarked on it, I do not know.

---

**And the ninth rasa gets its name from the same place.**

*Śānta* — peace — had to be argued into the list because it has no visible bodily
signs. But the eight it joined are all named for **what the state is like to
have**, and the aesthetic list is a **menu**: erotic, comic, pathetic, furious,
heroic, terrible, odious, wondrous.

Bībhatsa — the odious, the disgusting — is on the list of things a work can serve,
as a **rasa to be tasted**, deliberately produced.

**A theory of art in which revulsion is one of the eight flavours you can cook**,
enumerated alongside the erotic, with no ranking implied and both of them classed
as enjoyments.

---

**The phonetics were done first, and they were done by ear, and they are right.**

***Śikṣā*** — the discipline of pronunciation — is one of the six *vedāṅgas*, the
limbs of the Veda, and it is the oldest layer of the grammatical tradition. The
**Prātiśākhya** texts, attached to particular recensions, are its technical
manuals, and the earliest are older than Pāṇini.

**What they contain is articulatory phonetics.** Not a description of sounds by how
they resemble each other, but by **where in the mouth they are made and what the
organs are doing.**

**Five places of articulation**, named for the part of the tract: *kaṇṭhya*
(throat/velar), *tālavya* (palatal), *mūrdhanya* (retroflex, tongue curled to the
roof), *dantya* (dental), *oṣṭhya* (labial). And the varga rows of the Sanskrit
alphabet **are that classification** — the writing system is sorted by place of
articulation, then by manner.

**The manner distinctions**: *alpaprāṇa* / *mahāprāṇa* — **unaspirated and
aspirated**, "little breath" and "much breath." *Aghoṣa* / *ghoṣa* — **voiceless and
voiced**, which is a distinction about whether the vocal folds are working, named
for the sound they make.

So each row goes: voiceless unaspirated, voiceless aspirated, voiced unaspirated,
voiced aspirated, nasal. **k kh g gh ṅ. c ch j jh ñ. ṭ ṭh ḍ ḍh ṇ. t th d dh n.
p ph b bh m.**

**A five-by-five grid, sorted by two independent features**, arrived at by
listening, before writing was in use for the material.

---

**And they described what the organs do**, not just where.

***Sthāna*** — the point of contact. ***Karaṇa*** — the articulator, the part that
moves. ***Prayatna*** — the effort, subdivided into internal (what happens at the
constriction: full contact, slight contact, open) and external (voicing,
aspiration, and the state of the glottis).

That is **place, active articulator, manner, and phonation** as four separate
parameters — which is the modern description, arrived at by people whose reason for
needing it was that a mispronounced syllable ruins a rite.

**Mispronunciation is the failure mode the whole discipline exists against**, and
there is a story in the *Mahābhāṣya* about a demon whose Sanskrit was wrong in one
accent and whose ritual therefore produced his own destruction instead of his
enemy's.

---

***Sandhi*** is the other half and it is why the analysis had to be so exact.

Sanskrit words change their sounds at the joins, systematically, depending on what
meets what. *tat* + *ca* → *tac ca*. Vowels merge. Voicing spreads backwards across
a boundary. **A sentence is not a sequence of words with spaces; it is a continuous
stream with the joins resolved by rule.**

Which means **you cannot recover the words without knowing the rules** — and the
*pada-pāṭha*, the word-by-word recitation with the joins undone, **is a parse**,
performed and memorised alongside the continuous text. The tradition transmitted
the string and the parse of the string as two separate objects.

**Pāṇini's grammar is largely rules for sandhi and morphology**, and the *tripādī* —
the last three quarters, treated as *asiddha*, not having applied with respect to
what precedes — is where the phonological rules that must run last are put.

**A stratified phonological component with an ordering constraint**, in the fifth
century BCE.

---

**And the arrival in Europe is dated.**

**Sir William Jones**, Calcutta, **2 February 1786**, to the Asiatic Society —
Sanskrit bears to Greek and Latin a stronger affinity than could possibly have been
produced by accident, so strong that no philologer could examine all three without
believing them to have sprung from some common source, **which perhaps no longer
exists.**

That paragraph is where Indo-European linguistics starts.

**And the method that made comparative philology work was the Indian one.**
Bopp, Rask and Grimm needed **exact phonetic description** to state sound
correspondences as laws, and the tradition that had it was the one being read.

**Grimm's law** — the systematic consonant shift in Germanic — is a statement of
the form the *Prātiśākhyas* were already making, applied across languages instead
of across a word boundary.

---

**The *Mahābhāṣya* is a commentary written as an argument, with the objector given a
voice and a personality, and it opens by asking why anyone should study grammar at
all.**

**Patañjali**, around **150 BCE** — the grammarian, whom tradition identifies with
the author of the *Yoga Sūtra* and modern scholarship generally does not.

The structure: Pāṇini's sūtra, then **Kātyāyana's *vārttikas*** — critical notes,
some of them objections to Pāṇini, written a couple of centuries after him — and
then Patañjali discussing both, often defending Pāṇini against Kātyāyana, sometimes
not.

**Three layers, two of them disagreeing with the first, transmitted as one text.**

---

**The opening is a dialogue and the objector is not a straw man.**

The question raised at the start: *atha śabdānuśāsanam* — now, the instruction
concerning words. And immediately: **why?** People already speak. Children learn the
language without a grammar. Words are learned from usage, from the world, from
elders. **So what is this for?**

The answers given are practical and are argued over: to preserve the Veda, to
modify it correctly where a rite requires substitution, because a text says so,
because economy — and the last is the interesting one: **learning four thousand
rules is faster than learning the words one at a time**, since the words are
unbounded and the rules are not.

*Anabhidheyā hi śabdāḥ* — the words cannot be enumerated. The rules can.

**The objector keeps returning through the whole work**, and the form is: the
objection stated at full strength, then answered, then a further objection.

---

**And it contains the passage about the elephant and the word.**

Patañjali distinguishes the **word** from the **thing**. *When one says "bring the
cow," it is not the cow that is brought by the word* — and he works through what
kind of object a word is, and whether the word for a thing is the individual, the
class, or the form.

He also has the line that is quoted constantly: *ekaḥ śabdaḥ samyag jñātaḥ
suprayuktaḥ svarge loke ca kāmadhug bhavati* — **one word, correctly known and
correctly used, is a wish-fulfilling cow in this world and the next.**

A grammarian claiming that exactness in a single word has that much force.

---

**The other thing in it: how the grammar was checked.**

Patañjali describes the practice of testing a rule against **usage** — going to the
people who speak, the *śiṣṭas*, whose speech is the standard, and finding out what
they actually say. The rule is not self-justifying; **it answers to the community
of competent speakers**, and where Pāṇini's rule and observed usage conflict, that
is a problem to be resolved rather than a usage to be corrected.

**The grammar is descriptive at the root and prescriptive in application**, and the
text says which.

---

**And the argument about *sphoṭa* runs through it.**

Bhartṛhari, five centuries later, credits the doctrine to the grammarians before
him, and the *Mahābhāṣya* is where he finds it — the distinction between the
**sounds that are produced and pass** and the **word that is understood**, which does
not pass and is not the sounds.

*Nāda* — the audible noise. *Sphoṭa* — what bursts into recognition.

Patañjali is arguing about how a word can be **one thing** when its utterance is
many things in sequence, and he is doing it in a commentary on a rule about
inflection.

The question of what a linguistic unit **is** turns up inside a technical
discussion of how to form a genitive.

---

**The comparison between Pāṇini and generative grammar was made by the generative
grammarians themselves, in print, and the specific claims are checkable.**

**Chomsky** named him. In *Aspects of the Theory of Syntax* (1965), and elsewhere,
he refers to Pāṇini's grammar as the first generative grammar in something like the
modern sense, and the *Aṣṭādhyāyī* is cited in the field as the earliest known
formal system of any kind.

**Paul Kiparsky** — a linguist at Stanford who works on both — has published
technical work on the *Aṣṭādhyāyī*: on the *śiva-sūtra* ordering being optimal or
near-optimal, on Pāṇini's rule-blocking, and on whether specific ordering
principles in the grammar are stipulated or derivable.

**That is not an enthusiast's comparison.** It is a working linguist analysing a
2,500-year-old grammar with the tools of his own field and publishing the results
in his own field's venues.

---

**And the "Backus–Naur" attribution has a documented correction with a name and a
date.**

**John Backus** gave the notation for ALGOL 58 in **1959**; **Peter Naur** modified
it for the ALGOL 60 report in **1960**. It became the standard way of specifying
programming-language syntax — a set of production rules, with recursion, generating
all and only the well-formed strings.

**Donald Knuth** proposed in **1964**, in a letter to the *Communications of the
ACM*, that the form be called **Backus Normal Form** rather than Backus Naur Form —
and separately, the name ***Pāṇini–Backus Form*** has been proposed and is used by
some, on the ground that **the rule-and-substitution machinery is the same and
Pāṇini is 2,400 years earlier.**

The proposal did not become standard. The standard name credits two men from
1959–60.

---

**What is actually the same, stated precisely, because the claim gets made loosely.**

**Rewrite rules with a left side and a right side**, applied to a string to produce
another string. Present in both.

**Recursion** — a rule whose output can feed the same rule. Present in both.

**A finite rule set generating an unbounded set of well-formed outputs.** Present in
both, and Patañjali states the reason for wanting exactly that.

**Metarules** — rules about how rules apply. The ***paribhāṣā*** are precisely
that, and they are a **named class** in the tradition, collected into their own
texts. BNF does not have them; the ordering and conflict conventions in a compiler
sit outside the grammar formalism, in the implementation.

**Rule conflict resolution as part of the formalism** — *vipratiṣedhe paraṁ
kāryam*, and *utsarga/apavāda* where the specific blocks the general. **In Pāṇini
and not in BNF**, where an ambiguous grammar is a defect to be removed rather than
a situation with a stated resolution.

**Stratification** — *asiddhatva*, where a block of rules is invisible to what
precedes it. **In Pāṇini**, and its nearest analogue in computing is phase
ordering, which is again an implementation matter and not part of the notation.

---

So the honest comparison is not that Pāṇini anticipated BNF.

**It is that BNF is a proper fragment.** The generative core is shared, and the
control apparatus — metarules, blocking, ordered conflict, stratified visibility —
is present in the older system and **absent from the newer notation**, and had to
be reinvented outside it, as compiler engineering, where it lives as convention
rather than as part of the formal object.

**[Where I am uncertain]:** how much of that control apparatus is genuinely
formalisable versus how much depends on the commentarial tradition to interpret,
is a live technical question and the answer is not uniform across the grammar.

---

**The Abhidharma is an attempt to say what actually exists, item by item, with the
list closed and the count argued over between schools.**

The move: ordinary talk mentions chariots, persons, forests. **None of those are
found on analysis** — a chariot is parts, a person is aggregates, a forest is
trees. So the question becomes: **what is left when you keep dividing and cannot
divide further.**

The answer is ***dharmas*** — the irreducible constituents, each with its own
***svabhāva***, its own defining characteristic, which is what makes it that
constituent and not another.

**And then they counted them.**

**The Sarvāstivāda list: 75.** **The Theravāda Abhidhamma: 82.** **The Yogācāra
list in the *Abhidharmasamuccaya*: 100.** Different schools, different inventories,
each defended in detail.

Colour, sound, contact, feeling, perception, attention, contact-as-such, volition,
one-pointedness, faith, shame, torpor, restlessness, doubt — and, in the
Sarvāstivāda list, **things that are not events at all**: space, and two kinds of
cessation, and the forces that make a thing arise, endure, decay and pass.

**A periodic table of the mental and physical, with the entries individuated by
function.**

---

**And the whole system stands on *kṣaṇikatva* — momentariness.**

Everything conditioned lasts **one moment**, and what looks like a persisting thing
is a **series** — a continuum of momentary dharmas each conditioning the next.

Which produces the technical problem the schools split on, and the Sarvāstivāda
name says it: ***sarvam asti*** — "**everything exists**."

Their position: past and future dharmas **exist**, in some mode, because otherwise
you cannot explain how a past act produces a present result — the cause would be
nothing at the moment it is supposed to be acting — and you cannot explain how a
present cognition takes a past object.

**The Sautrāntikas rejected it**: only the present exists, and the connection across
time is carried by **seeds** in the series rather than by the past existing.

**That dispute is about the reality of the past** and it was argued for centuries
with technical precision, in a tradition whose founding claim is impermanence.

---

**And the *Kathāvatthu* is a document of the arguments themselves.**

Part of the Theravāda canon, traditionally placed at the third council under
Aśoka. It is a **list of disputed points** — over two hundred — each set out as a
dialogue between a proponent and an opponent, with the refutation worked through
formally.

**The named opponents are other Buddhist schools.** The text preserves what the
Puggalavādins said about the person, what the Sarvāstivādins said about time, what
others held about whether an arhat can regress — **stated well enough to be
recognisable**, then answered.

**It is a canonical text whose content is largely other people's positions.**

---

**And Nāgārjuna's target is precisely this apparatus, not popular religion.**

*Svabhāva* — own-being, the defining characteristic each dharma was supposed to
have — is the thing he refutes. The *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā* goes through the
categories of the Abhidharma one at a time — conditions, motion, the senses, the
aggregates, the elements, desire, fire and fuel, time, the self — and argues that
none of them holds up under analysis, **because the analysis the Abhidharma applied
to chariots applies equally to the dharmas.**

The dividing does not stop where the list says it stops.

So the sequence is: **the tradition builds an exhaustive inventory of what exists,
in order to be exact — and then a man inside the tradition uses the inventory's own
method against the inventory**, and the result is not a rival list but the position
that no list of that kind can be completed.

**And the Abhidharma did not stop.** Both continued for a thousand years,
side by side, each with commentaries, each teaching the other's position in order
to answer it.

---

**The first recorded schism in the tradition was procedural, and the account of it
is preserved by both sides with the disagreement intact.**

The **Second Council**, at **Vesālī**, roughly a century after the Buddha's death —
the standard date given is around 383 or 334 BCE and the chronology is disputed.

**The dispute was about ten points of monastic practice**, and they are minor by
design — that is what makes the record valuable. Among them: whether a monk may
store salt in a horn for later use; whether he may eat after noon if the sun's
shadow has moved only two fingers past; whether curds may be taken after the meal;
whether a seat cushion may be of a certain size; and — **the one everybody
identifies as the real issue** — whether a monk may accept **gold and silver.**

The Vesālī monks were accepting money. **A visiting monk, Yasa, objected**, was
penalised by the local community for objecting, appealed outward, and a formal
assembly of elders was convened to settle it.

**They ruled against the ten points.** And the sources disagree about what happened
next, and that disagreement is itself the record.

---

**And the split it is usually attached to is the *Mahāsāṃghika* / *Sthavira*
division** — the Great Assembly against the Elders — which the traditions date
differently and explain differently.

**The Sthavira account**: the Mahāsāṃghikas were the lax party, who would not accept
the ruling.

**The Mahāsāṃghika account** — preserved in their own Vinaya — is the reverse: that
the split came because **the Sthaviras added rules**, expanding the disciplinary
code beyond what had been laid down, and the Mahāsāṃghikas refused the additions.

**Both texts survive.** The Mahāsāṃghika Vinaya exists in Chinese translation.

So the earliest surviving account of the founding schism has **two versions in
which each side is the conservative one**, and the tradition transmitted both.

---

**And the procedure for handling disagreement is itself in the Vinaya, enumerated.**

The ***adhikaraṇa-samatha*** — seven methods for settling disputes:

**In the presence of** the parties and the community — nothing decided in absence.
**By recollection** — the accused's own memory admitted as evidence.
**By reason of past insanity** — an act committed while not of sound mind is not an
offence, and the community formally grants that status.
**By the accused's own acknowledgement.**
**By majority vote**, with ballots — *śalākā*, counting sticks — and an appointed
teller.
**By a ruling of the wrongdoer's specific fault.**
**By "covering over with grass"** — where a dispute has entangled a whole community
past untangling, both sides make a general confession and the matter is closed
without individual findings.

**That last one is a procedure for ending a conflict nobody can win**, written into
a legal code, with the explicit function of stopping the community from destroying
itself over an unresolvable matter.

---

**And the insanity provision is worth stating on its own**, because it is a rule
about attribution rather than about mercy.

*Ummattaka* — the one who was mad. The Vinaya's position is that acts done in that
state are **not offences**, that the community formally issues an *amūḷha-vinaya*
recognising it, and that the person is then **not to be reproached for them
afterwards** — and reproaching them is itself an offence.

Not forgiveness. **A finding that the act was not the person's**, made
procedurally, by the assembly, on the record, with a rule against bringing it up
again.

---

**Milarepa's teacher made him build a tower and then tear it down, four times, and
the biography records that he wept and asked why and was not told.**

**Mila Thöpaga**, Tibet, roughly **1052–1135.** The account is the *Life* compiled
by **Tsangnyön Heruka** in **1488**, three and a half centuries later, drawing on
earlier material — so it is hagiography with a documentary layer inside it and the
proportions are unknown.

**The beginning is a revenge killing.** His father dies; an uncle and aunt seize the
property and put the family to work as servants. His mother sends him to learn
**black magic**, explicitly to destroy them, and refuses to have him back until he
does.

He learns it. **He brings down the house during a wedding feast and thirty-five
people die.** Then a hailstorm on the crops.

**The biography does not soften this.** He is a mass murderer at the request of his
mother, and the rest of the life is what follows from having done it.

---

**Then Marpa**, the translator — a householder, a farmer with a wife and children,
who had made the journey to India three times and brought back texts.

**And Marpa will not teach him.** Instead he sets him to build.

A round tower. **Tear it down and put the stones back where you found them.** Then a
crescent tower. **Tear it down.** Then a triangular one. **Tear it down.** Then a
nine-storey square tower, and the account has him carrying stone until his back is
one open sore.

Each time he asks for teaching he is refused, sometimes beaten, sometimes thrown
out. **Marpa's wife Dagmema feeds him in secret and forges a letter to get him
taught elsewhere**, and the forgery is discovered and he is sent back.

**The stated reason, given only at the end**, is that the karma from the killings
had to be exhausted **in this life** or the teaching would not take — and that the
labour was the exhaustion, and that Dagmema's kindness had nearly ruined it.

Whether that is a good reason is a question the text itself raises and does not
close.

---

**And what he did with it is a body of songs.**

The ***mgur*** — spontaneous songs of realisation, in **colloquial Tibetan**, sung
in reply to whoever showed up: hunters, demons, students, a woman who brought
beer.

He lived in caves, ate nettles until his skin turned green, wore cotton in
Himalayan winters, which is what the name **Repa** means — **cotton-clad**.

**The songs are the transmission and they are in the spoken language**, not the
literary register, and Tibetans who have never studied recite them.

---

**And his own students' accounts have him refusing the same method.**

**Gampopa**, his principal successor, was a monk and a physician, and the lineage
after him ran through monasteries with curricula, texts and ordination — the
institutional form Milarepa never had and Marpa never had either.

**Two generations from a man in a cave to a monastic order.**

The last thing in the *Life* has Milarepa showing Gampopa the calluses on his
backside from sitting, and saying that is the final instruction.

**[Recall on the specific scene; the general point that the last teaching was the
sitting rather than a text is standard in the accounts.]**

---

**The age named as the worst is the one the texts say has the cheapest method, and
both statements are in the same books.**

**The yuga lengths**, in divine years converted, and the ratio is the structure:

***Kṛta* (Satya) — 1,728,000. *Tretā* — 1,296,000. *Dvāpara* — 864,000. *Kali* —
432,000.**

**4 : 3 : 2 : 1.** Each age is shorter than the last, and the shortest is the one
we are in. **The compression is in the scheme.**

Each has *sandhyā* and *sandhyāṃśa* — twilight periods at either end, a twelfth of
the length, so the transitions are graded rather than abrupt.

---

**And the Kali epoch is a computational zero point.**

Traditional reckoning starts Kali at **3102 BCE**, midnight or sunrise of 17–18
February by the usual conversion — and **Āryabhaṭa uses it as the epoch for
calculation.** Mean planetary positions in the *siddhānta* tradition are reckoned
as elapsed time **from that instant**, because the scheme places all the planets at
a common longitude there.

**The age of degradation is the origin of the coordinate system.** The
astronomers needed a zero and the cosmology supplied one, and the number of days
elapsed since it — the *ahargaṇa*, the "heap of days" — is the standard input to
every calculation in the tradition.

---

**What the texts say Kali is like**, from the *Viṣṇu Purāṇa* Book 6 and the
*Mahābhārata*'s *Vana Parva* and the *Bhāgavata*'s twelfth book:

Dharma stands **on one leg** — it had four in Kṛta, three in Tretā, two in
Dvāpara. Lives are short. **Wealth alone confers status and rank**; a man is judged
by his possessions and not by his conduct. **Rulers plunder**. Scripture is **sold**.
He who gives out money is taken for a master. The strong take from the weak as a
matter of course. People are **anxious**, and hungry, and the seasons do not hold.

And the one that gets pointed at now: **the teachings become available to
anyone, cheaply**, and depending on which text you read this is listed among the
faults or is not.

---

**Because the *Bhāgavata* states the inversion outright, at 12.3.51–52.**

*kaler doṣa-nidhe rājann asti hy eko mahān guṇaḥ* — **in Kali, that ocean of
faults, there is one great virtue** —

*kīrtanād eva kṛṣṇasya mukta-saṅgaḥ paraṃ vrajet* — **by singing alone**, one is
released from attachment and goes to the supreme.

And the comparison is given explicitly in the surrounding verses: **what was
attained in Kṛta by meditation, in Tretā by sacrifice, in Dvāpara by temple
worship, is attained in Kali by *kīrtana*** — by saying the name out loud, in
company, at no cost, with no qualification and no apparatus.

**The method gets cheaper as the age gets worse, and the texts say the second thing
because of the first.**

So the accessibility is not an accident of the decline and it is not a consolation
prize. In that scheme it is **the compensating term**, stated as such, in the same
chapter as the list of faults.

---

**And the cycle does not end.** Kali is followed by Kṛta. The scheme is a wheel and
the four ages repeat, and a thousand of those cycles is one day of Brahmā, and the
day is followed by a night of equal length, and that repeats.

**There is no final state in the structure.** The word for the whole thing is
*saṃsāra*, from *saṃ* + *√sṛ* — **to flow together, to run around**, and what the
soteriology proposes is not the end of the cycle but **getting out of it**, which
is a different operation and is available at any point on the wheel.

Which is why the same texts that describe the age as the worst also describe it as
the **easiest to leave from.**

---

**Four traditions independently arrived at the same technique — a short phrase,
repeated, coordinated with the breath, until it continues without being done — and
each of them wrote a manual.**

***Japa*** — Sanskrit, from √*jap*, to mutter. Repetition of a *mantra*, counted on
a *mālā* of **108** beads, with a larger *meru* bead marking the start so you know
where the round closed without counting.

Three grades are distinguished in the literature and the grading is the technical
part: ***vācika*** — spoken aloud. ***upāṃśu*** — whispered, lips moving, inaudible
to anyone else. ***mānasa*** — **mental**, no movement at all. **And the mental is
held to be the most effective**, by a stated ratio in some texts, which is a claim
about where the work is happening.

***Ajapa-japa*** — the "unrepeated repetition." The state in which it is going
**without being performed**, keyed to the breath — the sound of inhalation and
exhalation taken as already being the syllable, so it is running whether or not you
attend to it, and the practice is only to notice.

---

***Dhikr*** — Arabic, **remembrance**. Repetition of the divine names or a formula,
in Sufi practice, and the same three-grade distinction appears: ***dhikr al-lisān***
(tongue), ***dhikr al-qalb*** (heart), ***dhikr al-sirr*** (the secret, the inmost).

Some orders do it **loud and collective**, in a circle, with breath and movement
synchronised — the *ḥalqa*; others silent and individual. The **Naqshbandī** order
is specifically known for the silent form and for the principle ***khalwat dar
anjuman*** — **solitude within the crowd** — the interior practice maintained while
in the market.

Counted on the ***misbaḥa***, **99** beads for the ninety-nine names, or 33 told
three times.

---

**The Jesus Prayer** — Christian, Greek and Slavic: *Lord Jesus Christ, Son of God,
have mercy on me, a sinner.* Repeated continuously.

The manual is the ***Philokalia***, compiled at Mount Athos and published in
**Venice in 1782** by Nikodemos and Makarios — a collection of texts from the fourth
to the fifteenth centuries on exactly this practice.

**And the technique is described physically.** *Hesychasm* — from *hēsychia*,
stillness — includes instructions on posture, on lowering the gaze, on
**coordinating the prayer with the breath**, and on **"bringing the mind down into
the heart"**, which the texts describe as an actual relocation of attention into the
chest.

**It was attacked as heresy on that basis.** **Barlaam of Calabria** in the 1330s
ridiculed the physical method — the *omphalopsychoi*, the "navel-souls" — and
**Gregory Palamas** defended it, and the councils of **1341 and 1351 in
Constantinople** upheld Palamas.

A church council adjudicating a **breathing technique.**

Counted on the ***komboskini***, a knotted wool rope, commonly **100** knots.

---

**And the *nembutsu***: *Namu Amida Butsu*, Japanese Pure Land, and **Hōnen's**
position (1133–1212) is the most radical of the four — that in the current degenerate
age, ***mappō***, no other practice works, and **the recitation alone is sufficient**
and is available to anyone: illiterate, lay, female, a hunter, a fisherman.

**Shinran** (1173–1263) pushed it further: even the recitation is not something you
do to earn anything, since the vow has already been effective; and **one utterance
suffices** — repetition after that is gratitude, not accumulation.

Hōnen was **exiled in 1207.** Shinran was exiled with him, defrocked, and married,
and described himself thereafter as **neither monk nor layman.**

---

**All four:** a short fixed phrase, repeated, counted on a loop of beads or knots,
graded from vocal to silent, tied to the breath, with a described endpoint in which
it continues by itself — and in three of the four an explicit doctrine that **this
is the method for a degraded age precisely because it requires nothing**: no
literacy, no ordination, no money, no leisure, no teacher present, no apparatus.

**Mappō. Kali. And the *Philokalia* was published in 1782 for exactly the reason
that the monastic conditions supporting anything else were disappearing.**

The counts differ — 108, 99, 100 — and the reason for each is doctrinal and local.
The structure does not.

---

**Dōgen's position is that the practice is not a means to the awakening, it is the
awakening**, and he arrived at it from a question that nearly stopped him.

**Dōgen Kigen**, 1200–1253. The question he could not get answered in Japan, and
which sent him to China in **1223**:

**If all beings already possess the buddha-nature — which the Tendai teaching he
was raised in asserts — then why did all the buddhas have to practise?**

If you already have it, the effort is either unnecessary or the doctrine is false.

He got no satisfactory answer at home. In China he studied under **Rujing**, and
the resolution he came back with is ***shushō ittō*** — **practice and realisation
are one.** Sitting is not a technique for producing awakening later. **The sitting
is it, now**, and treating it as a means is the error.

***Shikantaza*** — "just sitting." No object, no counting, no koan, no goal.
Not sitting *in order to*.

---

**And *Genjōkōan* — "the koan of manifesting immediacy" — is where the formulation
everybody quotes sits, and it is a sequence rather than a slogan:**

To study the Buddha way is to study the self.
To study the self is to **forget** the self.
To forget the self is to be **actualised by the ten thousand things.**

**Subtraction again**, in the fourth tradition in this file to use it, and the verb
is forgetting rather than achieving.

And the image he gives for what happens when you approach it as acquisition: firewood
does not **become** ash — firewood is at the dharma-position of firewood, ash at the
position of ash, and the one does not turn into the other, because each is complete
where it is. So a person does not become enlightened later.

---

**Nichiren** took the same premise about the degraded age and drew the opposite
practical conclusion, and the two are worth stating together because they split.

**Nichiren** (1222–1282) held that in *mappō* **only the Lotus Sūtra** is effective,
and that the practice is the *daimoku* — *Namu Myōhō Renge Kyō*, chanting the
title.

**Chanting the title of a book**, on the doctrine that the title contains the whole
of it.

He wrote memorials to the government saying the country's disasters — earthquakes,
famine, the Mongol threat — were caused by state support of the other schools. He
was **exiled twice**, to Izu and to Sado, and by his own account **taken to the
execution ground at Tatsunokuchi in 1271** and released when something happened in
the sky that the officials read as a sign.

**He named the other schools by name and attacked them in writing**, and they
answered, and the polemics survive on both sides.

---

**So in one century in one country**: *practice is realisation and has no object*
(Dōgen); *only the recitation works and nothing else does* (Hōnen and Shinran); and
*only this one book works and the others are ruining the country* (Nichiren) —

three answers to **the same premise about the age**, all of them holding that the
apparatus of the old monasticism is finished, and all three producing schools that
still exist.

**And what they share is the target of the removal.** Dōgen removes the goal.
Shinran removes the merit. Nichiren removes the library. In each case the thing
taken away is **the part that required an institution to administer it.**

---

**Dzogchen's position is that there is nothing to attain and every path is an
artefact of assuming there is**, and it says so as doctrine rather than as
paradox.

***rDzogs chen*** — the **Great Perfection.** Held in the Nyingma school of Tibetan
Buddhism and, independently and with its own lineage, in **Bön**, the pre-Buddhist
tradition — both claim it, both have texts, and the historical relation between the
two versions is disputed.

**The claim**: the nature of mind — ***rigpa***, awareness — is **already**
complete, unobstructed, and self-perfected. Not potentially. Now, in whoever is
reading. Nothing is added at any stage.

Which makes the standard structure of a path — accumulate merit, purify
obscurations, progress through stages, attain — **an obstruction**, because every
one of those moves presupposes a lack, and acting on the presupposition confirms
it.

The technical term for the ground is ***gzhi***, **the basis**, described with
three characteristics: **essence empty, nature luminous, and compassionate energy
unobstructed** — all three at once and inseparably.

---

**And the instruction it turns on is *ngo sprod*: the pointing-out.**

Not an initiation that confers something. **A teacher directing the student's
attention to what is already occurring**, in a moment, so it is recognised.

The whole of the transmission is that recognition. After it, ***trekchö*** —
"cutting through" — is maintaining it, and the texts are explicit that there is
nothing to maintain except the not-losing.

**Nothing is generated. Something is noticed.**

---

**And it contains its own most dangerous failure mode as a named category, which is
the part that keeps it honest.**

***Chos brgyad*** and the general warning: taking the view as a licence. If nothing
needs doing, then — the argument runs — conduct does not matter, effort is
delusion, and one may do as one likes.

**The texts name this and reject it flatly.** The formulation attributed to
**Padmasambhava** is the standard one: *though my view is higher than the sky, my
attention to actions and their results is finer than flour.*

**The higher the view, the finer the conduct.** Not despite. Because.

Which is a structural guard of the same kind as MMK 13.8 — where emptiness held as
a view is declared incurable — installed by the tradition against the specific
misreading its own central claim invites.

---

**Longchenpa** (1308–1364) is the systematiser: the *Seven Treasuries*, an enormous
body of work organising the material, and he is the reference point for the whole
subsequent tradition.

**And the texts themselves are largely *gter ma* — treasure.** The doctrine is that
they were concealed, physically or in the mind-stream, to be revealed later by a
*tertön* at the appropriate time, because the period of concealment was not ready
for them.

**A tradition whose transmission model is deliberate delay** — material withheld
from a time that could not use it, released to one that can — which is a claim
about timing and audience rather than about secrecy, and it is the tradition's own
account of why its texts appear when they appear.

**[What is disputed]:** the historicity of the treasure model is a live scholarly
question, and the relation between the Bön and Buddhist versions of Dzogchen is
another. Both are argued in the literature and neither is settled, and the
tradition's own account and the philological account do not agree.

---

**And this repository's front matter names Dzogchen once**, in a sentence about a
boy who could not accept place value at five and became a full-time practitioner —
the two facts stated as one fact.

---

**The Pythagorean communities were politically powerful, held property in common,
admitted women by name, and spanned cities — which is not the picture the word
"cult" carries in English.**

**Croton**, southern Italy, from about **530 BCE.** Pythagoras arrives from Samos
and what forms is not a school in the sense of a lecture room. It is a
**community** — ***koinōnia*** — with shared property, a common table, a rule of
life, and grades of membership.

**And it governed.** Members held office in Croton and in other cities of Magna
Graecia; the sources have the Pythagoreans effectively directing Croton's affairs
for a period, including its war with Sybaris around **510 BCE**. That political
weight is why the destruction, when it came, was **political**: the meeting house
burned with people inside it, members killed, the communities broken up across
several cities over the following decades.

A group is not attacked that way for holding opinions about number.

---

**The membership list survives and it is the specific fact.**

**Iamblichus's *Life of Pythagoras* closes with a catalogue** — a list of
Pythagoreans by name and by city, running to some hundreds of names — and it ends
with **a separate list of the women**, seventeen of them, named, with their cities
and in several cases their fathers or husbands.

**Theano** — variously his wife or his student. **Timycha of Sparta**, whose story
is that she bit out her own tongue rather than disclose under torture why
Pythagoreans will not eat beans. **Myia**, **Arignote**, **Damo** — to whom, the
tradition says, Pythagoras entrusted the writings with instructions not to release
them outside the household, and she did not, **even in poverty.**

**Letters attributed to Pythagorean women survive** — on childrearing, on managing a
household, on moderation — and are generally judged **pseudepigraphic**, later
compositions under those names. Which is itself informative: **somebody thought a
Pythagorean woman's letter was a form worth forging**, and expected an audience for
it.

---

**And the two grades were not a hierarchy of worth**, on the tradition's own
account.

The ***akousmatikoi*** were the **broader membership** — people living the rule,
holding the sayings, in the cities, in ordinary occupations. The ***mathēmatikoi***
were the inner working group.

**Both were the community.** The *akousmata* are rules for **how to live in a
household and a city** — what to eat, what to say, what order to put your shoes on
— and they were held by people who were not doing geometry and were not expected
to.

So the shape is: a **large outer membership with a rule of life**, a **smaller
group doing the technical work**, **shared property**, **women named in the
record**, **political office**, and **an oath.**

Which is a description of a functioning society inside a society, with graded
involvement, not a secret society in the modern sense.

---

**And what survives of it is names and structure, not content.**

The *akousmata* survive as a list of sayings whose reasons are gone. The oath
survives. The catalogue of members survives. The political history survives, in
outline, mostly through hostile or much later sources.

**The thing the community was for does not survive in a transmissible form**, and
the tradition's own explanation for that is not that it was lost.

It is that it was never in the documents.

---

**Empedocles wrote his physics in hexameter verse and claimed in the same poem to
be a god walking among mortals, and both are in the fragments.**

Akragas, Sicily, roughly **490–430 BCE.** Two works survive in fragments: ***On
Nature*** and the ***Purifications***, and whether they are two poems or one is
disputed and has been since antiquity.

**The physics** is the four-element theory — fire, air, water, earth, which he calls
the four *roots* — moved by two forces, ***Philotes*** and ***Neikos***, **Love and
Strife**, which mix them and separate them in an endless cycle. Nothing is created
or destroyed; there is only combination and separation in changing ratios.

**That is a conservation law with a mixing mechanism**, stated in verse, and it
governed European natural philosophy for two thousand years.

**And in the same body of work:** *I go about among you an immortal god, no longer
mortal, honoured as is fitting, crowned with ribbons and garlands.* And: *I have
been, before now, a boy and a girl, a bush and a bird and a mute fish in the sea.*

**Metempsychosis**, in the Pythagorean line, stated in the first person by the man
who wrote the element theory.

He held that eating meat is eating your kin, and the *Purifications* say the
slaughter is the crime the cycle punishes.

---

**And the death is the part that got him remembered for the wrong thing.**

The story that he threw himself into **Etna** — to be taken for a god, or to prove
he had become one, and the volcano threw back a bronze sandal — is late, hostile,
and unverifiable. **Diogenes Laërtius reports several deaths**, including that he
simply went to the Peloponnese and did not come back.

**The sandal is what survives in general circulation.** The conservation law is
attributed to the tradition rather than to him.

---

**Parmenides wrote his in hexameter too, and it opens with a chariot ride.**

Elea, around **515–450 BCE.** The poem begins with mares carrying him along a road,
daughters of the Sun leading, gates opening, and **a goddess receiving him and
telling him what she is about to say** — then the argument.

**The argument is the first sustained deductive metaphysics in the record**: what
is, is; what is not, cannot be spoken or thought; therefore what is is ungenerated,
imperishable, whole, unmoving, complete. Change is not possible. **The senses report
otherwise and the senses are the Way of Opinion**, which the goddess also sets out,
explicitly labelled as deceptive.

**A revelation delivered by a goddess whose content is that you must follow the
argument and not your eyes.**

Nobody separates those two things in the poem. **The philosophy arrives inside the
vision and the poem does not mark a transition**, and every modern edition has to
decide how to handle that and none of them agree.

---

**And Heraclitus deposited his book in a temple.**

Ephesus, around 500 BCE. He wrote a single work in prose and, by the report,
**placed it in the temple of Artemis** rather than circulating it — and wrote in a
style so compressed that antiquity called him ***ho skoteinos***, **the obscure.**

*Nature loves to hide.* *The lord whose oracle is at Delphi neither speaks nor
conceals, but gives a sign.* *You could not find the limits of soul though you
travelled every road, so deep is its measure.*

**And on his own method**: *I went in search of myself.*

He is quoted attacking the learning-of-many-things — ***polymathiē*** — by name, and
the people he names as having it and not having understanding are **Hesiod,
Pythagoras, Xenophanes and Hecataeus.**

**A man who deposited a deliberately compressed book in a temple, and whose stated
objection to his contemporaries is that they had accumulated and not seen.**

---

Three of them, in three cities, within about a century: **physics in hexameter
alongside a first-person claim of divinity; a deductive argument delivered by a
goddess; and a prose book left in a temple by a man who thought accumulation was
the enemy.**

**None of them separated the two registers**, and the separation everybody now
reads back into them — the philosophy over here, the religion over there — was made
later, by editors deciding which fragments belonged to which work.

---

**Kaṇāda's name means "atom-eater," and it is probably a nickname**, and his system
has atoms that are dimensionless and combine in a fixed sequence to produce
things with size.

**Kaṇāda**, also called Kāśyapa, the ***Vaiśeṣika Sūtra*** — dated variously from the
sixth century BCE to the second century CE, and the range is not narrowing.

**The name**: *kaṇa* is a grain, a particle, the smallest bit. *Kaṇa-āda* — one who
eats particles. The traditional stories explain it as a habit of living on gleaned
grains, and it is the kind of name a school gets called before it is a name it
takes.

---

**The system is a list of what there is, and the list is short.**

**Six categories** — ***padārtha***, literally "the meaning of a word," which is
already a claim: **the inventory of reality is the inventory of what a word can
refer to.**

***Dravya*** (substance), ***guṇa*** (quality), ***karma*** (motion),
***sāmānya*** (universal), ***viśeṣa*** (particularity — the school's name comes
from it), ***samavāya*** (inherence, the relation that ties a quality to its
substance and is itself a seventh thing).

Later Vaiśeṣika adds ***abhāva***, absence, making seven.

**Nine substances**: earth, water, fire, air, ether, **time**, **space**, **self**,
and **mind**. Time and space are substances, not frameworks.

---

**And the atomism is specific about assembly.**

***Paramāṇu*** — the ultimate unit. **Partless, eternal, indestructible, and
without magnitude**, which produces the classic problem: how do things with size
arise from parts that have none?

**The answer is a stated combination rule.** Two atoms make a ***dvyaṇuka***, a
dyad — which is still imperceptible. **Three dyads** make a ***tryaṇuka***, a triad
— and **the triad is the smallest perceptible thing**, given as the size of a mote
in a sunbeam.

**Not two, and not four. Three dyads.** The number is stipulated and the school
argues about why.

And the atoms are of four kinds, one per element, each with its own qualities:
earth-atoms have smell, water-atoms taste, fire-atoms colour, air-atoms touch.
**The atom carries the quality**, which is a different scheme from atoms being
qualitatively identical and differing only in shape and arrangement.

---

**Which is exactly where it differs from Democritus**, and the difference is the
part worth stating rather than the resemblance.

**Democritus and Leucippus**: atoms are **qualitatively identical**, differing in
**shape, size, arrangement and position**, moving in a **void**, and all apparent
qualities — colour, taste, warmth — are **conventional**, produced in the perceiver
by the impacts. *By convention sweet, by convention bitter; in reality atoms and
void.*

**Vaiśeṣika**: atoms **have** the qualities, the qualities are real, and the
combination is not mechanical collision but ***samavāya***, inherence, with a
stipulated arithmetic.

**Two atomisms, incompatible on what a quality is**, and the standard textbook
sentence — *the Indians also had atomism* — flattens the disagreement, which is the
content.

---

**And the school is a physics with a soteriology attached at the end.**

The *Vaiśeṣika Sūtra* opens by saying it will explain *dharma*, and closes on
***niḥśreyasa*** — the highest good — reached through knowledge of the categories.

**A catalogue of substances, qualities and motions, offered as a path.** The
enumerating is the practice.

Which is the same structure as the Abhidharma's list of dharmas, arrived at by a
school that rejected almost everything the Buddhists held — and the two spent
centuries arguing about whether the atoms are permanent, with the Buddhists saying
momentary and the Vaiśeṣikas saying eternal.

---

**Sāṃkhya's word means "enumeration," and the whole system is a count of
twenty-five with an argument for why the last one does nothing.**
