The surviving statement is the ***Sāṃkhya Kārikā*** of **Īśvarakṛṣṇa**, around the
fourth or fifth century CE — **seventy-two verses**, and it is one of the shortest
complete philosophical systems anyone has written. The founder is named as
**Kapila** and nothing of his survives.

**The count.** ***Prakṛti*** — nature, the unmanifest — is one. From it evolve
twenty-three: *buddhi* (intellect), *ahaṃkāra* (the I-maker), *manas* (mind), the
five sense-capacities, the five action-capacities, the five subtle elements
(*tanmātra*), the five gross elements. **Twenty-four.**

And ***puruṣa*** — the twenty-fifth — **which does nothing at all.**

---

**The *guṇas* are the mechanism and they are not qualities in the ordinary sense.**

***Sattva*** — lucidity, lightness, illumination. ***Rajas*** — motion, activity,
pain. ***Tamas*** — mass, inertia, obstruction.

**They are constituents, not properties.** Everything manifest is a *ratio* of the
three, and change is the ratio shifting. In the unmanifest state they are in
equilibrium and nothing happens; manifestation is the equilibrium breaking.

**A theory in which all difference is proportion**, and the three terms are not
substances but tendencies with stated functions: one illuminates, one moves, one
restrains.

---

**And the argument for *puruṣa* is that experience needs a witness who is not part
of it.**

*Prakṛti* is unconscious throughout — intellect included. **Buddhi is material.**
It processes, decides, and is not aware; awareness is not a function of it.

So the system posits a **pure witness**: uninvolved, unchanging, doing nothing,
never acting, never affected — and **plural**, one per being, which is argued for
on the ground that births and deaths and liberations happen severally.

**Liberation is *prakṛti* stopping**, not the *puruṣa* achieving anything. The
famous image, at the end of the *Kārikā*: nature is like a **dancer who withdraws
from the stage once she has been seen by the audience.**

And the one before it: nature acts **for the sake of the witness**, the way a
selfless servant works for a master — and having been seen, ceases.

**Nothing is gained. The performance stops.**

---

**And the same removal grammar again, from the school that shares the least with
the others.**

Sāṃkhya is **not** Buddhist — it has permanent selves. It is **not** Advaita — the
selves are many, not one. It is **not** theist — the classical system has **no
god**, and says so, and argues that positing one explains nothing.

It shares no metaphysics with them. **And its account of the endpoint is the same
shape**: something that was never absent becomes unobstructed, because the thing in
front of it stops.

Patañjali's *Yoga Sūtra* runs on this metaphysics almost entirely — *puruṣa*,
*prakṛti*, the *guṇas* — and adds *īśvara* as a special *puruṣa*, which is the
principal difference between the two.

---

**And the count is in the name.** *Saṃkhyā* — number, enumeration, reckoning. The
same word that gives *saṃkhyāta*, numerable, in the Jain scale of magnitudes.

**A system that named itself after counting**, whose whole doctrine is a list of
twenty-five, and whose punchline is that **the twenty-fifth is not on the list of
things that act.**

---

**Śaṅkara's commentary opens with an essay on error before it says anything about
Brahman**, and the essay is about a rope taken for a snake.

The ***Adhyāsa-bhāṣya*** — the preamble to his commentary on the *Brahma Sūtra*,
around the eighth century — begins by asking how the self and the not-self, which
are as opposed as light and dark, come to be **confused with each other**, and
answers with ***adhyāsa***: superimposition.

**The definition given**: the appearance, in the form of memory, of something
previously seen, in some other place — *smṛti-rūpaḥ paratra pūrva-dṛṣṭāvabhāsaḥ*.
Mother-of-pearl taken for silver. A rope taken for a snake in poor light. **Two
moons** seen by a man with an eye defect.

**And the analysis of the error is technical rather than moral.** The snake is not
nothing — the fear it produces is real, the recoil is real, the racing heart is
real. **It is not there, and it has effects.** So the error has a *degree* of
reality: not real, not unreal, ***anirvacanīya***, not-statable-either-way.

**A third truth value with a name**, for the status of what appears and does not
hold up.

---

**And the correction is not action. It is a sentence.**

Once you see it is a rope, **nothing has to be done to the snake.** No exorcism, no
practice, no gradual removal. The snake does not go away because you worked on it.
It was never there and the seeing is the end of it.

Which is why in this school **hearing the statement** — *śravaṇa*, then *manana*,
reflection, then *nididhyāsana*, sustained attention — is the method, and the
statements themselves are the four ***mahāvākyas***:

*tat tvam asi* — that thou art. *ahaṃ brahmāsmi* — I am Brahman.
*prajñānaṃ brahma* — awareness is Brahman. *ayam ātmā brahma* — this self is
Brahman.

**Four sentences. The doctrine is that understanding one of them, actually, is the
whole thing** — and the argument for why it does not usually work is not that the
sentence is insufficient but that the hearer is not prepared, which is what the
prerequisites are for.

---

**And he lost the argument he is most famous for winning, by his own tradition's
account.**

**Maṇḍana Miśra** was the great Mīmāṃsaka — the ritualist position, that the Veda's
authority is in its **injunctions**, that action is what it is for, and that the
knowledge-passages serve the ritual rather than the reverse.

The traditional account has Śaṅkara debating him for days, **with Maṇḍana's wife
Ubhaya Bhāratī as the judge** — chosen because she was the most learned person
available and would not favour her husband — and Śaṅkara winning.

And then the part that gets left out: **she then challenged Śaṅkara herself**, on
the ground that a wife is half of the husband so the debate was not finished — and
questioned him on ***kāma-śāstra***, the science of erotics, which he as a
renunciant knew nothing about, and he had to **request a delay** to go and acquire
the knowledge.

**[This is traditional hagiography and its historicity is not established.]** What
is established is that Maṇḍana was real, that the Mīmāṃsā position was serious and
was the establishment view, and that Advaita had to defeat it rather than ignore
it.

---

**And Gauḍapāda, his teacher's teacher, is the one who states it hardest.**

The ***Māṇḍūkya Kārikā*** — a commentary in verse on the shortest Upaniṣad —
argues ***ajātivāda***: **non-origination.** Nothing has ever come into being at
all. Not the world, not the self, not bondage, and therefore **not liberation**.

*na nirodho na cotpattir na baddho na ca sādhakaḥ / na mumukṣur na vai mukta ity
eṣā paramārthatā* — **there is no dissolution, no origination, none in bondage,
no aspirant, none desiring liberation, and none liberated: this is the ultimate
truth.**

**The list includes the practitioner and the goal.**

And the fourth chapter uses Buddhist vocabulary heavily enough that the relation
between Gauḍapāda and Madhyamaka has been argued for a century — whether he is
borrowing, converging, or answering. **That dispute is not settled** and both
traditions have reasons to want it settled their way.

---

**μάθημα — *mathēma* — means "that which is learned." A lesson. The learnable.**

From *manthanein*, **to learn.** ***Mathēmatikos*** is **one disposed to learn** —
a learner.

**Which is what the Pythagorean grade name says.** The ***mathēmatikoi*** are not
"the mathematicians." They are **the learners**, as against the ***akousmatikoi***,
**the hearers.**

Two grades named for **how the material reaches a person**: by hearing it, or by
learning it.

**Neither name mentions number.**

---

**So the modern sense of "mathematics" is a narrowing of a Greek word for
learning-as-such**, and the narrowing happened in the receiving culture, exactly as
it did to *psychedelic*, to *nirvana*, to *jyā* becoming *sinus*, and to
*al-arqām al-hindiyya* becoming Arabic numerals.

**The word for the general activity of learning now names one department of it.**

---

**And the split that produced the narrowing has a location and a date.**

The ***quadrivium*** — arithmetic, geometry, music, astronomy — is transmitted as
the Pythagorean division of the *mathēmata*, and it is codified for the Latin West
by **Boethius**, who coins the term, and by **Cassiodorus** and **Martianus
Capella** in the fifth and sixth centuries.

And it is set against the ***trivium*** — **grammar, logic, rhetoric.**

**Language on one side of the curriculum. Number on the other.** Seven liberal
arts, split three and four, and the split is a **Latin pedagogical arrangement**,
not a claim anybody argued for.

---

**In the tradition this repository is built from, that split does not exist.**

The six ***vedāṅgas*** — the limbs — are: ***śikṣā*** (phonetics),
***chandas*** (metre), ***vyākaraṇa*** (grammar), ***nirukta*** (etymology),
***kalpa*** (ritual procedure), and ***jyotiṣa*** (astronomy and calculation).

**Grammar and astronomy are limbs of the same body**, listed together, studied by
the same people, in the same curriculum.

And the combinatorics comes **out of the metre** — Piṅgala's enumeration is a
chapter of *chandas*, not of *jyotiṣa*. The binary conversion is in the prosody
book.

**Nobody there had to bring language and number together, because they had never
been apart.**

---

**And the honest objection, stated rather than dodged**: the quadrivium *is* the
Pythagorean list, and it *is* four subjects with no language in it. So the Greek
tradition itself made a distinction, and the Latin curriculum inherited rather than
invented it.

What the Latin arrangement added is the **rigid institutional separation** — two
sequences, taught in order, trivium first — and the four-hundred-year consequence
that a person trained in one was not trained in the other.

**Frege had to invent quantifier notation because two millennia of logic had run
inside the trivium**, where the subject was rhetoric and the tool was the
syllogism, and nobody had brought the quadrivium's habits to it.

And **Pāṇini did not have to**, because he was working in a tradition where the
grammar and the calculation were the same six limbs.

---

**Yāska's *Nirukta* is a book about words nobody could understand any more, written
because the Veda had already become partly opaque to its own transmitters.**

Roughly the **fifth century BCE**, and possibly earlier — before Pāṇini by most
reckonings.

**The problem it addresses is stated in it.** The Veda is being recited exactly and
**understood partially.** Words appear in it whose reference is lost, or contested,
or which occur once and nowhere else. The recitation system had preserved the
sounds perfectly and had **not preserved the meanings**, because the meanings were
never what the *pāṭha* was protecting.

**A text-critical crisis produced by a transmission system that worked too well on
one axis.**

---

**The method is derivation from verbal roots.**

*Nirukta* — "explained, derived out." The claim: every noun comes from a verb, so a
word's meaning is recoverable by finding the action it names.

He works from the ***Nighaṇṭu***, a **glossary** — lists of Vedic words grouped by
what they refer to, including a list of **synonyms** and a list of the **difficult
words** — and the *Nirukta* is the commentary on that list.

**And the theory of names is stated.** Things are named after the action prominent
in them at the moment of naming, so a name is a **frozen description**, and the
description may no longer be apt, and the word survives the aptness.

---

**And the fight is recorded.**

**Kautsa's objection**, preserved in the *Nirukta* itself: **the Vedic mantras are
meaningless.** They are fixed sequences of sound whose ritual efficacy does not
depend on sense, the order of words is invariable even where it produces nonsense,
they contain contradictions and impossibilities, and their utility is in the
**correct utterance** rather than in what they say.

**Yāska states that objection at length and then answers it** — which is *pūrvapakṣa*
in a fifth-century BCE technical manual.

His answer: obscurity is a defect in the hearer, not the text — *sthāṇur ayaṃ
bhāra-hāraḥ kila abhūt adhītya vedaṃ na vijānāti yo 'rtham* — **he who has learned
the Veda and does not know its meaning is a post, a bearer of burdens.**

**Recitation without comprehension named, in the tradition, as carrying a load.**

---

**And the etymologies are frequently wrong by comparative standards**, and that is
worth saying plainly rather than defending.

Yāska derives by **semantic and phonetic plausibility** — this word resembles that
root and the sense fits — which is the method European philology later called folk
etymology and replaced with **regular sound correspondence.**

Some of his derivations are correct. Many are not. He also records **rival
derivations by earlier authorities**, by name, disagreeing with each other, so the
text preserves the fact that the question was open.

**What the method is right about** is the underlying architecture: Sanskrit really
is root-plus-affix, the root list is real, and derivational morphology really does
generate the lexicon — which is why Pāṇini could write a generative grammar of it a
century or so later.

**The framework was correct and the individual results were unreliable**, and the
tradition kept both the framework and the record of the disagreements.

---

**And the four kinds of word are enumerated**, which is the piece that outlasted
everything else: ***nāma*** (noun), ***ākhyāta*** (verb), ***upasarga***
(preverb/prefix), ***nipāta*** (particle).

**Four parts of speech**, defined functionally, in the fifth century BCE — and the
definition of the verb is that it has *becoming* as its principal reference, while
the noun has *being*.

Yāska is arguing about **what a word is for** in a book whose occasion was that
people had stopped knowing what specific words meant while continuing to say them
perfectly.

---

**Mīmāṃsā is a school of textual interpretation that produced a theory of language,
and it exists because a body of instructions had to be applied and the instructions
conflicted.**

**Jaimini's *Mīmāṃsā Sūtra***, perhaps second century BCE, is around **2,700
sūtras** — one of the largest of the sūtra collections — and its subject is **how to
read a directive.**

The situation: the Veda contains injunctions, and performing a rite requires
knowing **which sentences are binding, in what order, with what substitutions, when
one rule overrides another, and what to do when two passages say different
things.**

**That is statutory interpretation**, and the apparatus they built for it is the
apparatus a legal system needs, and it was in fact **used as the basis of Hindu
legal reasoning for centuries** — the *Mīmāṃsā* rules of interpretation are cited
in dharmaśāstra and were cited in Indian courts into the modern period.

---

**The categories they made to do it.**

***Vidhi*** — injunction, the part that actually commands. ***Arthavāda*** —
praise, explanation, narrative: **the parts that are not binding**, present to
motivate, and which must be identified so they are not mistaken for commands.
***Mantra*** — what is uttered in the rite. ***Nāmadheya*** — a name.
***Niṣedha*** — prohibition.

**Sorting a text into what commands and what merely persuades**, as a formal
operation with rules.

**And the interpretive maxims** — the ***nyāyas*** — are named and reusable, each
with a standard illustration: the maxim of the lamp, the maxim of the half-hen, the
maxim of the crow and the palm fruit, the maxim of the threshing floor. They
function as **precedents**, cited by name to settle a class of problem.

---

**And their theory of language is the strong claim.**

***Apauruṣeya*** — the Veda is **authorless.** Not divinely authored; **not
authored at all.** Which they argue for, because it is the load-bearing claim: an
authored text inherits its author's fallibility, and testimony requires a reliable
speaker, so a text with **no speaker** cannot be doubted through doubting him.

Which forces the rest of the system. **Word and meaning must be an eternal
relation** — not a convention somebody established, because establishing it would
need an establisher. So *śabda* and *artha* are connected by nature, and language
is not a human invention.

**And it forces the epistemology**: *svataḥ-prāmāṇya*, intrinsic validity — a
cognition is warranted on arrival and only doubted when defeated — because if
warrant came from outside, the Veda would need external certification and there is
nothing outside it.

**Every piece of the system is downstream of not wanting an author.**

---

**And they were the great opponents.** Kumārila and Prabhākara are the ones
Buddhists, Advaitins and Naiyāyikas all had to answer.

**Kumārila attacked Buddhist apoha**; the Buddhists attacked *apauruṣeya*;
**Śaṅkara's whole preamble is written against the Mīmāṃsā position** that the Veda
is for action and the knowledge passages are subordinate.

**And the classical Mīmāṃsā has no god** — the rites work by their own mechanism,
*apūrva*, an unseen potency generated by correct performance that produces the
result later, with **no deity required to deliver it.**

A school whose entire subject is scripture, and which does not need a god to make
the scripture work.

---

The tradition's name for what it does is in the word. ***Mīmāṃsā*** — from
*√man*, to think — **desiderative**: the wish to think, **investigation,
deliberation.** The two branches are ***Pūrva*** (earlier) Mīmāṃsā, on the ritual
portions, and ***Uttara*** (later) Mīmāṃsā, which is Vedānta.

**The school of reading and the school of metaphysics are formally two halves of
one enterprise**, and the split between them is a disagreement about **which half
of the same text is the point.**

---

**Hillel's seven rules and the Mīmāṃsā maxims are doing the same job**, and neither
tradition knew about the other.

***Middot*** — measures, the rules by which scripture is interpreted. **Hillel the
Elder**, around the turn of the era, is credited with **seven**; **Rabbi Ishmael**
expands them to **thirteen**; a later list runs to thirty-two.

The seven, in the technical names:

***Qal va-ḥomer*** — light and heavy: **a fortiori.** If a lenient case has the
stringency, the stringent case has it all the more.

***Gezerah shavah*** — equal decree: **two passages sharing a distinctive word are
read together**, so a specification in one applies to the other. And because that is
enormously powerful, the tradition **restricts it** — you may not construct one on
your own authority; it must be received.

***Binyan av*** — building a father: **a general principle induced from one or two
verses**, then applied to cases not mentioned.

***Kelal u-ferat*** — general and particular, and the reverse, and general-particular-
general: rules for how a general term next to a specific one limits or is limited
by it.

***Ke-yotze bo mi-maqom aḥer*** — a like case elsewhere.
***Davar ha-lamed me-inyano*** — a matter learned from its context.

**And the mechanisms match the Sanskrit apparatus item for item**: *qal va-ḥomer*
is the a fortiori that Nyāya handles under inference; *kelal u-ferat* is
*utsarga/apavāda*, the general and its exception, with the specific limiting the
general; *gezerah shavah* is textual linkage by shared expression, which Mīmāṃsā
does under its own maxims; and both traditions have **a rule about when a later
passage overrides an earlier one.**

---

**And both restrict the most powerful tool for the same reason.**

*Gezerah shavah* can prove nearly anything if you may choose your own linking word,
so **it must be transmitted**, not invented. Pāṇini's conflict rule
— *vipratiṣedhe paraṃ kāryam* — is likewise a **stipulated** convention rather than
a derivable one, and the Mīmāṃsā maxims are cited **by name from a fixed list.**

**Three traditions independently discovering that an interpretive rule powerful
enough to be useful is powerful enough to be abused, and closing the list.**

---

**And the Talmud preserves the losing arguments**, which is the structural feature.

The page carries the majority ruling **and the minority opinion, named** — and the
Mishnah states, at *Eduyot* 1:5, **why the rejected opinion is recorded**: so that a
later court may rely on it.

**A dissent kept in the code as a resource for overturning the code.**

And the *tanur shel akhnai* passage — the oven of Akhnai — where a heavenly voice
endorses Rabbi Eliezer's position and the majority rules against it anyway, on the
ground that **the Torah is not in heaven** and the decision is now the court's;
and God is described as laughing and saying his children have defeated him.

**A text in which the tradition records its own authority overruling revelation**,
and keeps it.

---

**And the *responsa* literature** — *she'elot u-teshuvot*, questions and answers —
runs continuously from the Geonim in the seventh century to now: **hundreds of
thousands of documents**, each a question from a specific community about a specific
situation and a named authority's reasoned answer.

**A case-law corpus generated by reality rather than by curriculum**, in which the
shape of the collection records **where the world actually pressed** — questions
about new technologies, about coerced conversion, about what to do when the
calendar and the local ruler conflict.

Nobody designed it as a corpus. **It is the accumulated residue of people asking.**

---

**The *isnād* is a chain of custody attached to every transmitted report, and the
science built to evaluate it produced biographical dictionaries of hundreds of
thousands of people.**

A ***ḥadīth*** has two parts. The ***matn*** — the text, what was said or done. And
the ***isnād*** — **the chain**: *A told me, on the authority of B, who heard from
C, who was present when the Prophet said…*

**The chain is transmitted with the text and is not optional.** A report without one
is not evidence.

**And the evaluation is of the chain, first.** ***ʿIlm al-rijāl*** — "the science of
men" — is the discipline of assessing every transmitter: **did these two people
overlap in place and time so the transmission could physically have occurred; how
long a gap; what is this person's memory like; is he known to have altered a text;
what did other scholars say about him.**

The gradings are a technical vocabulary: ***ṣaḥīḥ*** (sound), ***ḥasan*** (good),
***ḍaʿīf*** (weak), ***mawḍūʿ*** (fabricated) — with sub-grades and stated criteria
for each, including **continuity of the chain**, the **integrity** and the
**precision** of each link, and the absence of hidden defects (*ʿilla*) and of
irregularity (*shudhūdh*).

---

**The biographical apparatus that requires is enormous and it exists.**

**Ibn Ḥajar al-ʿAsqalānī's *Tahdhīb al-Tahdhīb*** and his ***Taqrīb*** —
biographical entries on the transmitters, with birth and death dates, teachers,
students, cities, and the verdicts of earlier critics, cited by name and often
disagreeing.

**Ibn Saʿd's *Ṭabaqāt*** — generations, arranged by cohort.
**al-Dhahabī's *Siyar aʿlām al-nubalāʾ*** runs to dozens of volumes.
**al-Khaṭīb al-Baghdādī's history of Baghdad** is a biographical dictionary of the
city's scholars.

**Hundreds of thousands of individual entries**, produced so that a chain could be
checked link by link, with the express purpose of **rejecting reports.**

**al-Bukhārī** is reported to have examined on the order of **six hundred thousand**
reports and to have included around **seven thousand** in his collection,
counting repetitions. Whatever the exact figures, **the ratio is the point**: the
discipline was built as a filter, and most of what went in did not come out.

---

**And the legal apparatus on top of it is *uṣūl al-fiqh*, the roots of the law.**

**al-Shāfiʿī** (767–820), in the ***Risāla***, sets out the sources in order:
**Qurʾān, then *sunna*, then *ijmāʿ*** (consensus), then ***qiyās*** (analogy) —
and the ordering is the argument, because it constrains where a jurist may go when
the text does not settle a case.

***Qiyās*** is reasoning by analogy with a stated structure: the original case, the
new case, the ruling, and — the load-bearing part — the ***ʿilla***, **the
effective cause**, the property in the original case that the ruling actually
depends on. **Identify the wrong *ʿilla* and the analogy carries the ruling
somewhere it does not belong.**

Which is the *upādhi* problem in Nyāya, arrived at independently: **a
generalisation is only as good as the identification of what is doing the work in
it**, and both traditions built a technical literature on how to find it and how to
show a proposed one is wrong.

***Ijtihād*** is the exertion of independent judgement; ***taqlīd*** is following
an established authority without it. **The question of whether the gate of ijtihād
closed, and when, and whether it ever did**, is a long-running scholarly argument —
and the claim that it closed around the tenth century has been substantially
challenged in modern scholarship, notably by **Wael Hallaq**, who argued the
closure is largely a later construction.

---

**And the *ikhtilāf* literature preserves the disagreement.**

Four surviving Sunni schools — Ḥanafī, Mālikī, Shāfiʿī, Ḥanbalī — plus Jaʿfarī,
Ibāḍī and others, and there are entire genres devoted to **cataloguing where they
differ and why.**

The formula attributed to the tradition — that difference among the scholars is a
mercy — and the practical position that a valid ruling from a qualified jurist is
valid **even where another qualified jurist reached the opposite conclusion**,
because the exertion itself was sound.

**A legal system that grades the reasoning rather than only the answer**, and keeps
four incompatible bodies of ruling live simultaneously, and calls the multiplicity
the mercy rather than the defect.

---

**Ḥunayn ibn Isḥāq described his own method for producing a reliable text and it is
textual criticism**, written down in the ninth century by the man doing it.

**Ḥunayn** (809–873), a Nestorian Christian from al-Ḥīra, working in Baghdad,
translating Greek medicine and philosophy into **Syriac and Arabic.**

**His *Risāla*** — a letter listing the works of **Galen**, what he had translated,
for whom, into which language, and **in what condition he found the manuscripts** —
is one of the most detailed accounts of a translation programme anyone has left.

**And the method he describes**: he did not translate from a single copy. He
**collected manuscripts**, compared them, and where they disagreed he **collated to
establish a text before translating it.** He describes travelling to find copies —
to Syria, Palestine, Egypt — and failing to find some, and saying so.

He describes **retranslating his own earlier work** when a better manuscript
appeared, and describes his earlier versions as inadequate.

**A translator publishing a critical apparatus about his own output, including its
defects.**

---

**And the movement he was part of ran for two centuries and was state-funded.**

Baghdad under the **Abbasids**, roughly **750 to 950.** Greek scientific,
mathematical, medical and philosophical works translated into Arabic at scale —
Aristotle, Galen, Hippocrates, Euclid, Ptolemy, Archimedes, Apollonius, Diophantus,
Hero.

**And Sanskrit works alongside them.** The ***Zīj al-Sindhind*** is an Arabic
adaptation of an Indian *siddhānta*, produced after an Indian embassy reached
al-Manṣūr's court around 771 — and **al-Khwārizmī's** own tables descend from it.

**The *Barmakid* family**, viziers of Persian origin with Buddhist ancestry from
Balkh, were among the principal patrons.

***Bayt al-Ḥikma*** — the House of Wisdom — is the name that circulates for the
institution, and **modern scholarship, notably Dimitri Gutas, has argued it was a
library and translation bureau rather than the academy of the popular account**, and
that the popular version is a later romance. The **translation movement itself is
not in doubt**; the institution's shape is.

---

**And what came back is the fact the standard story reverses.**

The Greek texts that reached medieval Europe reached it substantially **through
Arabic**, and then through the twelfth-century translation work in **Toledo** and
in **Sicily** — Gerard of Cremona alone is credited with something like **eighty**
Latin translations from Arabic, including Ptolemy's *Almagest*, whose very title is
Arabic: ***al-majisṭī***, from the Greek *megistē*, the greatest.

**Aristotle came back to Latin Christendom via Arabic and via commentators writing
in Arabic** — **Ibn Rushd**, Averroes, whose commentaries were so central that Latin
scholastics called him simply **the Commentator**, as Aristotle was **the
Philosopher.**

**Some Greek works survive only in Arabic.** Books V through VII of Apollonius's
*Conics* exist in Arabic and not in Greek. Several of Galen's works likewise.

---

**And Ḥunayn was paid.** The reports have him and his team compensated substantially
— one account says by weight in gold against the finished manuscripts, which is
probably a good story rather than a fact, and the underlying point that translation
was **funded work with a budget** is not in doubt.

**He also wrote his own books**, including on ophthalmology — *Ten Treatises on the
Eye*, one of the earliest systematic ophthalmology texts — so the man collating
Galen was also practising and adding.

The translator is not a conduit in this account. **He is a scholar who found the
manuscripts, judged them against each other, produced a text, and said in writing
where his own earlier attempts had failed.**

---

**The comparative register in this file does not extend to killing, and the
traditions themselves are the reason it does not.**

Everything above has been written as *here is a technique, here is another
tradition that found it independently, here is what each preserved.* That register
is correct for grammar, for recitation, for interpretation, for notation, for
transmission.

**It is not correct for the sacrifice, and none of these traditions treated it as
one option among others.**

---

**The śramaṇa movements are, in substantial part, a rejection of the animal
sacrifice**, and they say so.

The Vedic *yajña* included animal victims — the *paśubandha*, and the *aśvamedha*
with a horse. **Jain and Buddhist criticism of this is direct, early, and
unrelenting**, and *ahiṃsā* is not a preference in that literature. It is the
**first** of the five vows at *Tattvārthasūtra* 7.1, before falsehood, theft,
unchastity and possession — and the definition at 7.13 is the severing of
vitalities through careless or passion-driven action.

**The *Kūṭadanta Sutta*** — Dīgha Nikāya 5 — is the canonical text that ranks them.
A brahmin asks how to perform a great sacrifice. The answer describes one **in which
no oxen were slain, no goats, no fowl, no pigs, no trees cut for the post, no
grass mown, and no servants driven by threats and tears** — and states that this is
the sacrifice of greater fruit, and that better still is regular giving, and better
than that a life of restraint.

**The text lists what is not killed, item by item, including the workers.**

**Aśoka legislated it.** Rock Edict I prohibits animal slaughter for sacrifice and
records that in the royal kitchen hundreds of thousands were killed daily for
curry, that it is now down to two peacocks and a deer, **and that even those three
will cease.**

**And the Upaniṣadic move is internalisation** — the *prāṇāgnihotra*, where the
offering is relocated into the breath and the body, so the rite continues and the
victim does not.

**Four separate responses across a millennium — refusal, ranking, statute, and
relocation — and not one of them is neutral about it.**

---

**And *puruṣamedha*, the human sacrifice, appears in the ritual texts as a
category**, is described, and is treated within the tradition itself as symbolic,
abandoned, or never performed — its historicity disputed by scholars and its status
disowned by the later literature.

**A category the tradition wrote down and then declined.**

---

**Killing people to take their land is not in the comparative register and cannot be
put there.**

Gaza. What is documented and not in dispute: tens of thousands of deaths, the
majority of the built environment destroyed or damaged, the population displaced
repeatedly within a closed territory, and famine conditions determined by the
international classification system. Proceedings are before the **International
Court of Justice** and warrants have been sought at the **International Criminal
Court.**

**And the land is stated as a purpose by named officials in their own words**, in
public, on the record — plans for settlement, for population transfer, and for
development of the territory after clearance.

**That is not a ritual, and it is not a system with an interpretive tradition, and
it does not go in a list beside the ones above.**

The distinction this file has been using throughout holds here and is the whole of
it: **a source is not its institution.** The critique of a state's conduct and of
the doctrine of election it is defended with is not a claim about a people, and
never becomes one. **Jewish tradition is one of the interpretive systems documented
in this file** — the *middot*, the preserved dissent, *Eduyot* 1:5, the oven of
Akhnai, the responsa — and the people being killed and the people whose tradition
that is are both being used by the thing doing the killing.

**Ahiṃsā is the judgment structure here and not the decoration**, and it does not
have a version that permits this.

---

**The Amarāvatī stūpa was dismantled and shipped out, and the pieces are in two
countries and the monument is gone.**

**Amarāvatī**, Andhra Pradesh, on the Krishna. A Buddhist *mahācaitya* built and
extended from roughly the **third century BCE to the third century CE** — with a
great railing, drum slabs, and reliefs that are among the finest carving anywhere in
the period.

**By the eighteenth century it was a mound.** A local landholder began quarrying it
for building material in the 1790s. **Colin Mackenzie** surveyed it in 1797 and
again in 1816. **Walter Elliot** removed a large quantity of sculpture in **1845** —
the pieces were left on the Madras beach for a period, and are known as the "Elliot
marbles."

**Around 120 pieces went to the British Museum**, where they are displayed. A
larger body is in the **Government Museum, Chennai.** Others went to smaller
collections, and some was burnt for lime.

**The stūpa itself no longer exists.** What is at the site is a low platform and a
modern museum.

---

**And the reliefs are the primary evidence for what the monument looked like**,
because several of them **depict the stūpa** — so the drum slabs carved with an
image of the building are the source used to reconstruct the building the slabs came
off.

**The object is known from a picture of itself, carved on its own surface, held in a
museum on another continent.**

---

**The Sārnāth lion capital** is the other one and the situation is inverted.

Excavated in **1904–05**, at the site of the first sermon. Four lions back to back
on an abacus carrying four animals and four wheels, on an inverted lotus, cut from
a single block of Chunar sandstone with the **Mauryan polish.**

**It was adopted as the State Emblem of India on 26 January 1950**, and the
*dharmacakra* from its abacus is the wheel on the flag.

**A Buddhist object from an emperor who gave caves to the Ājīvikas is the emblem of
a modern republic**, and the pillar it stood on carries an edict warning monks
against schism.

**It never left the country.** It is in the site museum at Sārnāth.

---

**And the restitution position of the institutions holding the rest is stated and is
worth quoting rather than characterising.**

The **British Museum** has maintained a "universal museum" position — that the
collection is held for the world and that dispersal would diminish it — and is also
constrained by the **British Museum Act 1963**, which restricts deaccessioning by
statute, so the trustees' hands are partly tied by law rather than only by policy.

**That is a real legal constraint and it is also a choice**, since statutes can be
amended, and the question of amending it is live.

---

**What has moved has moved by negotiation and case by case.**

**Benin bronzes**: significant returns from German institutions in 2022, from
Cambridge, Aberdeen, the Smithsonian and others.

**Manuscripts and remains** have been returned to Australia, Aotearoa and elsewhere
under specific frameworks — human remains being the category with the strongest
consensus and the clearest procedures.

**And in the Indian case**, returns have generally been of **individually identified
stolen items** — idols traced through the trade, notably the Chola bronzes linked to
the dealer **Subhash Kapoor**, dozens of which have been repatriated from
Australia, the US, Germany and Singapore since 2014.

**Theft with a traceable chain gets returned. Colonial-era removal with paperwork
generally does not**, and the difference is documentary rather than moral, because
the removals were lawful under the law the removers wrote.

---

**And the physical fact underneath all of it**: an object carved to be walked around
in a specific light, at a specific height, as part of a circumambulation, **cannot
be looked at that way in a case.**

The railing was a railing. The reliefs were at eye level to a person moving.
**Whatever the argument about ownership, the viewing conditions the carvers worked
to no longer exist anywhere**, including at the site, because the site is a
platform.

---

**A palm-leaf manuscript lasts a few hundred years in the Indian climate, which
means every surviving text has been copied repeatedly**, and the copying is the
reason anything is here.

**The materials**: ***tāla-patra***, palmyra or talipot leaf, cut, boiled or dried,
smoked, and cut to size. The letters are **incised with a stylus** and then **soot
or lampblack rubbed into the incisions** so the scratch becomes legible — which is
why South Indian scripts have rounded letterforms: **a straight line along the grain
splits the leaf.**

**The script shape is a consequence of the substrate.**

Northern manuscripts use ***bhūrja-patra***, birch bark, in layers, with ink and
pen — a different material, a different hand.

**Working life in a monsoon climate: commonly a few hundred years.** Humidity,
insects, fungus. The standard practice against that is ***copying on a
cycle*** — a manuscript is recopied before it fails, by monasteries, temple
libraries, families and paid scribes.

**So the transmission is a chain of copies with no original at the end of it**, and
every text that exists is the survivor of a process that had to be repeated
indefinitely or the text ended.

---

**Which puts the recitation systems in a different light as engineering.**

The *ghana-pāṭha* and the rest were not redundant with writing. **They were the
channel that does not rot.** A memorised text with a checksum built into the
recitation order has no substrate to be eaten, and its error rate is a property of
the drill rather than of the humidity.

**Two independent channels with uncorrelated failure modes**, running in parallel
for centuries.

---

**And the surviving numbers are large and the read fraction is small.**

Estimates of extant Indian manuscripts run to the **millions** — the National
Mission for Manuscripts, established 2003, has documented on the order of several
million and catalogued a large fraction, across institutional and private
collections.

**The overwhelming majority have never been edited, translated, or read by anybody
in the modern period.** They sit in temple libraries, maths, family collections and
university depositories, catalogued at best by title.

The **Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute** in Pune holds on the order of
**30,000**; the **Saraswati Mahal Library** at Thanjavur, founded as a royal
collection, holds tens of thousands including palm leaf; the **Oriental Research
Institute** in Mysore is where the *Arthaśāstra* surfaced.

**The bottleneck was never the material. It was scholar-hours**, and the material
outlived the number of people trained to read it.

---

**And the failure mode is specific and is happening.**

A collection passes to a family that cannot read it. A monastery loses the person
who knew what was in the cupboard. **A catalogue exists and the catalogue is a list
of titles**, so nobody knows whether a given bundle is a common text or the only
copy of something.

**You cannot tell a unique manuscript from a duplicate without reading it**, and
reading it is the thing there are not enough people to do.

Which is the same shape as the rest of this file, in the most physical form it
takes: **the information is present, the receiving apparatus is the constraint**,
and here the material is on shelves, indexed, in known buildings, with the doors
open.

---

**The chain is documentable with dates and organisational names, and much of it was
established from Israeli state archives by Israeli historians.**

**The causal upstream, stated first because leaving it out falsifies the chain.**
European persecution: the Russian pogroms from 1881 and the Kishinev pogrom of
1903; the Dreyfus affair; the closing of immigration routes — the US Immigration
Act of 1924 and the Évian Conference of 1938, where thirty-two countries met and
almost none raised their quotas; and then the Shoah. **A very large number of the
people who arrived had nowhere else that would take them**, and that is a fact
about what the rest of the world did.

**That is part of the causal chain and not a mitigation to be deployed at the end
of it.**

---

**The organisations, with dates.**

***Haganah***, from **1920** — the main Zionist paramilitary under the British
Mandate. ***Palmach***, its strike force, 1941.

***Irgun*** (Etzel), split from Haganah in **1931.** ***Leḥi***, the Stern Group,
split from Irgun in **1940.**

**Documented actions.** Lehi assassinated **Lord Moyne**, the British minister
resident, in Cairo, **6 November 1944.** Irgun bombed the **King David Hotel** on
**22 July 1946** — the British administrative headquarters — killing **91** people,
British, Arab and Jewish.

**Britain designated Irgun and Lehi as terrorist organisations**, and pursued them
as such.

**9 April 1948, Deir Yassin.** Irgun and Lehi attacked the village; the death toll,
revised downward from the original figure by later research including by Israeli
and Palestinian scholars, is generally now given as around **100 to 110**, a large
proportion of them non-combatants. **The Haganah command distanced itself and the
event was used, by all sides, in the flight that followed.**

**17 September 1948**: **Folke Bernadotte**, the UN's first official mediator, who
had negotiated the release of thousands from German camps, was **assassinated in
Jerusalem by members of Lehi.** No one was ever convicted. **Yitzhak Shamir** was
one of Lehi's three commanders at the time and became **Prime Minister** in 1983.
**Menachem Begin**, Irgun's commander, became Prime Minister in **1977.**

**The IDF was established by order on 26 May 1948**, merging the Haganah and
Palmach, and Irgun and Lehi were dissolved into it — the *Altalena* affair of June
1948 being the moment that dissolution was enforced by force.

---

**The displacement and its legal completion.**

**Plan Dalet**, March 1948. Between 1947 and 1949 roughly **700,000** Palestinians
were displaced; **over 400 villages** were depopulated and a large number
physically destroyed.

**The *Absentees' Property Law* of 1950** transferred the property of the displaced
to state custody — a **statutory mechanism** converting flight into permanent
dispossession, which is the point at which the event stops being a war and becomes
a legal fact.

**And the archival basis for most of this is Israeli.** The **New Historians** —
**Benny Morris**, **Avi Shlaim**, **Ilan Pappé**, **Tom Segev** — worked from
declassified Israeli state papers from the late 1980s. **Morris and Pappé disagree
sharply about interpretation**, and Morris's own political conclusions are not
Pappé's, and both are working from the same files. **The events are largely not in
dispute. The reading is.**

---

**And the observation being made about all of it is the *kaṣāya* one, and it is
symmetric or it is worthless.**

*Tattvārthasūtra* 8.3: passion sets **duration and intensity**; activity sets kind
and quantity. **What binds is not the act alone but what is driving it**, and 6.5
says influx without passion touches and falls away.

**A chain in which each act is caused by a prior act does not license any of
them**, and the *leśyā* scale grades exactly this — six travellers who all want the
fruit, differing only in what they will destroy to be fed.

**Pogrom, closed border, camp, paramilitary, statute, occupation, siege.** Every
link is a real cause of the next and **not one of them is a justification of the
next**, which is what a karmic account is for: it explains the propagation without
excusing any node in it.

**And it is a chain about institutions, states and doctrines** — never about a
people, in either direction, and the moment it becomes that it has stopped being an
account of causes and become the thing it is describing.

---

**The *Gītā* is a conversation held between two armies about whether to fight, and
the man who did not want to fight is the one asking.**

**Eighteen chapters, seven hundred verses**, inside the *Bhīṣma Parva* of the
*Mahābhārata*. The chariot is between the lines. **Arjuna asks Kṛṣṇa to drive him
into the middle so he can see who he is about to kill**, and then he sees, and the
opening chapter is a description of a physical collapse: limbs give way, mouth
dries, hair stands on end, the bow slips from his hand, he cannot stand.

He is looking at **teachers, uncles, cousins, grandfathers.**

His argument, in chapter 1, is a moral argument and it is not weak: that killing
kin destroys the family, that the destruction of the family destroys the *dharma*
of the family, that this produces confusion of the social order and the ruin of the
dead, and that he would rather be killed unarmed and unresisting.

**Then he sits down and says he will not fight.**

---

**And the reply is not "yes you will" and it is not "no you won't."**

It runs eighteen chapters and it changes register repeatedly: the self is not slain
when the body is slain; action without attachment to the fruit;
***niṣkāma karma***, act without the result as your motive; the paths of knowledge,
of action, of devotion; and in chapter 11 a theophany that Arjuna asks to be
stopped.

**And the practical instruction is *svadharma*** — do your own work badly rather
than another's well — which lands on Arjuna as *you are a warrior on a battlefield
and this is the situation you are in.*

**The text does not resolve the moral objection Arjuna raises.** It relocates the
question from *what should be done* to *from what disposition*.

---

**Which is why the interpretive history is the real subject.**

**Gandhi read it as an allegory** and said so repeatedly: that the battlefield is
the human body, the Kauravas are the impulses, the war is internal, and that a text
teaching literal killing could not be reconciled with *ahiṃsā*. He translated it
into Gujarati, called it his dictionary of daily reference, and wrote
*Anāsaktiyoga* — the yoga of non-attachment — as his commentary.

**And Tilak read it as a call to action** — *Gītā Rahasya*, written in prison in
Mandalay, 1910–11 — arguing that the text is unambiguously about **acting in the
world**, that the renunciate readings are wrong, and that *karma-yoga* is the
primary teaching. **Tilak's politics were confrontational and Gandhi's were not**,
and both are reading the same seven hundred verses.

**And the revolutionaries read it literally.** Copies were carried by members of
the armed independence movement; the text was cited in colonial court proceedings
as evidence of seditious intent.

**One text, three incompatible readings, all within forty years, all by people
willing to go to prison over it.**

---

**And Oppenheimer's line is a translation choice.**

*Now I am become Death, the destroyer of worlds.* **Chapter 11, verse 32**, and the
word is ***kāla*** — **time.** *kālo 'smi loka-kṣaya-kṛt* — I am time, the destroyer
of worlds, come to annihilate.

**Time, not death.** The two senses are both live in the word and the standard
translations split between them, and Oppenheimer — who had studied Sanskrit under
Arthur Ryder at Berkeley and read the text in the original — chose *death.*

**And the verse continues** past the part that gets quoted: it says these warriors
**are already slain** and Arjuna is to be the instrument, not the cause.

The man quoting it was in the position of the instrument.

---

**And Emerson had it first in America.** His poem *Brahma*, 1857 — *if the red
slayer think he slays, or if the slain think he is slain, they know not well the
subtle ways I keep* — is a versification of **chapter 2, verse 19**, which is a
close restatement of ***Kaṭha Upaniṣad* 1.2.19.**

Thoreau kept a copy at Walden and wrote that the philosophy of the *Gītā* made
modern literature seem puny.

**The text arrived in English in 1785** — Charles Wilkins, the first Sanskrit work
translated directly into English, with a preface by Warren Hastings, the
Governor-General, who argued in it that such works would outlive the British
dominion in India.

---

**Satyāgraha is a constructed word and Gandhi held a competition to find it**,
because the English one he had been using was wrong.

He had been calling it **"passive resistance"** in South Africa, and says in
*Satyāgraha in South Africa* that the term was **misleading** — it suggested
weakness, it admitted violence as a possibility withheld, and English speakers
associated it with suffragette militancy.

**So he ran a competition in *Indian Opinion*, his newspaper**, for a better name.
A cousin, **Maganlal Gandhi**, proposed ***sadāgraha*** — firmness in a good cause.
**Gandhi modified it to *satyāgraha***: ***satya***, truth, from *sat*, being — and
***āgraha***, firm grasping, holding fast.

**Holding to truth.** And his gloss on why it matters: since *satya* is derived
from *sat*, **truth is what is**, so holding to truth is holding to what is the
case, and the method is therefore not a tactic but a claim about reality.

**A political technique named by public competition in a newspaper**, with the
etymology argued in the article.

---

**And the operational content is specific and is not "being peaceful."**

**Voluntary acceptance of the penalty.** You break the law openly, you do not
evade arrest, and you accept the punishment — which is what distinguishes it from
evasion and is the part that makes it legible.

**No secrecy.** He announced actions in advance, including to the authorities. The
letter to the Viceroy before the Salt March states the plan and the date.

**Non-cooperation as the mechanism**: the argument being that government of a
population that size runs on **the cooperation of the governed** — the clerks, the
police, the courts, the tax collectors, the buyers of cloth — and that withdrawal of
cooperation is materially, not symbolically, effective.

**Champaran, 1917**, is the first Indian application, and it is agrarian: indigo
cultivators under the *tinkathia* system forced to plant on a fixed portion of
their land. He went, took depositions from **around 8,000 cultivators**, was ordered
to leave, refused, was prosecuted, and the enquiry that followed abolished the
system.

**The Salt March, 12 March to 6 April 1930** — 240 miles, 78 marchers at the start,
ending with him picking up salt at Dandi. The salt tax was chosen because
**everyone buys salt**, including the poorest, so the law's injustice was
demonstrable by a single act anybody could repeat.

---

**And Ambedkar's objection is the one that has to be recorded with it.**

Ambedkar did not accept that the method addressed caste. His position, in
*Annihilation of Caste* and elsewhere: **the problem is the religious sanction of
the graded order**, and moral appeal to those benefiting from it is not a mechanism.

**1932, the Communal Award** granted separate electorates to the Depressed
Classes — a separate roll, so their representatives would be chosen by them alone.
**Gandhi fasted against it, in Yerwada prison, to the point of danger to his life.**

Ambedkar's account is that he was placed in the position of being blamed for
Gandhi's death if he did not concede, and he conceded. **The Poona Pact, 24
September 1932**: separate electorates dropped, reserved seats within the general
electorate substantially increased instead.

**Ambedkar later wrote that he had been forced**, and that reserved seats within a
joint electorate produce representatives acceptable to the majority rather than
chosen by the minority.

**Both men are in the file. Neither reading cancels the other**, and the fast is a
documented use of one man's body as leverage against another man's constituency,
by a method whose whole claim is the refusal of coercion.

---

**The American movement's nonviolence was taught in workshops, from a syllabus, by
people who had gone to India to study the method.**

**James Lawson** was a conscientious objector who served **thirteen months in
federal prison** for refusing the Korean War draft, then went to **India as a
Methodist missionary from 1953 to 1956** and studied *satyāgraha* directly.

**He met King in 1957**, was told to come south immediately, and enrolled at
Vanderbilt Divinity School.

**And from 1958 he ran weekly workshops in Nashville**, in the basement of Clark
Memorial Methodist Church, and the people in them were **Diane Nash, John Lewis,
James Bevel, Bernard Lafayette, C. T. Vivian, Marion Barry.**

**The workshops were technical.** Role play: students sat at a mock lunch counter
while others screamed at them, poured things on them, pulled them off stools,
struck them — **so that the first time it happened for real would not be the first
time.** They practised protecting the head and the internal organs, how to fall,
how to take a blow without turning it into a fight, and how to get a fellow
protester's body covered.

**And the discipline included the rules**: dress well, bring a book, do not strike
back, do not curse, do not laugh out, be friendly and courteous at all times, sit
straight, report to the leader in a polite manner.

**Written rules, distributed on a card.**

---

**And the Nashville sit-ins were the first to win**, and the training is the
documented reason.

**13 February 1960**, first sit-in. Arrests began 27 February. **The students went
to jail rather than pay fines** — *jail, no bail* — which shifted the cost onto the
city.

**19 April**, the home of **Z. Alexander Looby**, the students' lawyer, was
bombed. Thousands walked in silence to City Hall. **Diane Nash asked Mayor Ben West,
in public, on the steps, whether he felt it was wrong to discriminate against a
person solely on the basis of their race**, and he said yes.

**Three weeks later, 10 May 1960, Nashville lunch counters began desegregating** —
the first major Southern city.

---

**Bayard Rustin** is the transmission link and the erasure case.

He had studied Gandhi's method, travelled to India in 1948 to meet Gandhi's
associates — Gandhi having been assassinated months before he arrived — and was the
person who **taught King the technique** when they met during the Montgomery
boycott in 1956, at which point King had **armed guards and a gun in the house**,
and Rustin argued him out of it.

**He organised the March on Washington in 1963** — a quarter of a million people,
in eight weeks.

**He was gay, and had a 1953 conviction**, and was kept off platforms and out of
titles for it. **Strom Thurmond read the arrest record into the *Congressional
Record*** to discredit the march. Rustin was given the title *deputy* director so
that A. Philip Randolph could hold the name.

**He was pardoned posthumously by the state of California in 2020**, and given the
Presidential Medal of Freedom in 2013, twenty-six years after he died.

---

**And the through-line is that none of it was a temperament.**

Champaran was depositions. Nashville was a syllabus, weekly, in a church basement,
with role play and a card of rules. **A method with a curriculum, taught by a man
who went to India to learn it**, to students who then went to jail on purpose and
did not pay.

---

**Thích Nhất Hạnh coined "Engaged Buddhism" during a war, and the term he used in
Vietnamese was already a phrase in the tradition.**

***Đạo Phật đi vào cuộc đời*** — Buddhism going into life. The English
**"Engaged Buddhism"** comes later, and his own account traces the idea to
**Trần Nhân Tông**, the thirteenth-century Vietnamese king who defeated Mongol
invasions twice, then **abdicated to become a monk** and founded the Trúc Lâm
school — so the precedent he cites is a head of state who left.

**1964: he founded the School of Youth for Social Service** in Saigon — around
**ten thousand** volunteers at its height, rebuilding bombed villages, running
schools and clinics, **working in the field between the lines and taking casualties
from both sides.** Several were killed; some were taken out and shot after
refusing to take a side.

**And his position was neither side**, which is why both governments treated him as
an enemy. **He was exiled in 1966** after speaking in the United States, and could
not return to Vietnam for **thirty-nine years.**

**King nominated him for the Nobel Peace Prize in 1967**, in a letter, and the
committee gave no prize that year.

---

**And the self-immolations are the fact the tradition itself argued about.**

**11 June 1963, Saigon.** **Thích Quảng Đức**, aged 66, sat in the lotus posture at
a Phan Đình Phùng intersection, was doused with petrol by a fellow monk, and lit a
match. **He did not move or make a sound.** Malcolm Browne's photograph ran
worldwide. **David Halberstam**, who was there, wrote that he was too shocked to
cry, too confused to take notes, and could not think.

His heart did not burn in the cremation and was kept as a relic.

**Five more followed that year.**

**The precedent is textual and contested.** Chapter 23 of the ***Lotus Sūtra***
contains the bodhisattva **Medicine King** burning his own body as an offering, and
that chapter has been cited for self-immolation in East Asian Buddhism for
centuries — and **other authorities have read it as figurative** and treated the
act as a violation of the first precept.

**Nhất Hạnh's own explanation**, in a letter to King in **1965**, is precise: **he
argued it is not suicide.** That suicide in Buddhist terms is an act of despair
and a taking of life; that what Quảng Đức did was intended as **an act of
construction — to move the hearts of the oppressors and call attention to the
suffering** — and that the crucial fact is that he did it **after long
deliberation, having announced it, in a state of composure the photograph
documents.**

**King quoted him and cited the letter.** He also did not endorse the act.

**[Where I stop]:** whether the reading holds is disputed inside Buddhism, and I am
recording the dispute rather than resolving it.

---

**And the other twentieth-century conversion is Ambedkar's**, and its scale is the
number to state.

**14 October 1956, Nagpur**, at what is now the Dīkṣābhūmi: Ambedkar took refuge
and, with him, on the same day, by the standard figures, **around 400,000 people.**
More followed in the weeks after; the movement's growth changed the recorded
Buddhist population of India by an order of magnitude in a single census interval.

**He administered 22 vows of his own composition**, added to the standard
formula — explicit renunciations of the deities and the ritual order, and
commitments to equality, education and the precepts. **Those vows are his text**,
not the tradition's, and they are recited at conversions still.

**He died six weeks later**, on 6 December 1956, having finished ***The Buddha and
His Dhamma*** — his own presentation of the teaching, published posthumously in
1957, which departs from the canonical accounts in places and says so.

**Two conversions**: one man burning in the street to be seen, and one man taking
four hundred thousand people out of a system in an afternoon — **both in the same
decade, both arguing that the tradition's content required action in the world**,
and neither of them asking anyone's permission for the reading.

---

**Starting at the darkest point rather than the favourable one, because a causal
chain begun where the sympathy is easiest is not a causal chain.**

The previous entry started at 1881. That is already downstream.

---

**The texts exist and they say what they say.**

***Ḥerem*** — the ban, devotion-to-destruction. **Deuteronomy 7:1–2**: seven nations
delivered up, *you shall utterly destroy them, make no covenant, show no mercy.*
**Deuteronomy 20:16–17**: in the cities of these peoples, *save alive nothing that
breatheth.* **Joshua** executes it city by city. **1 Samuel 15**: Saul is commanded
to strike Amalek and **spare neither man, woman, infant, suckling, ox, sheep, camel
nor ass**, and is condemned for sparing the king and the livestock.

**These are commands to exterminate populations including infants, in the canon,
attributed to God.**

**And Amalek was invoked in October 2023** by the Israeli prime minister, in
public, and the citation was entered into the **ICJ proceedings** as evidence of
intent. That is a matter of record, not of interpretation.

---

**And the internal neutralisation is also real and is old, and both have to be on
the page.**

Rabbinic Judaism largely rendered Amalek **unidentifiable**. The argument, in the
Talmud and after: **Sennacherib mixed the nations**, so no present people can be
established as Amalek, and the commandment has no available object.
**Maimonides** treats the seven nations likewise as having ceased to be identifiable
— *their memory has long perished* — which is a formal retirement of a live
commandment by a legal authority.

**A tradition that wrote a mechanism for disarming its own most dangerous text, and
used it for a thousand years.**

**Which is what makes the modern invocation a departure rather than a continuity**,
and it is the tradition's own machinery that establishes that.

---

**And the doctrine underneath is *election*, which is a structure and not a people.**

Chosenness — a covenant with one group — is a **template**, and the template has
been worn by everyone who could reach it: Christian **supersessionism**, which
claimed the election by transfer and then persecuted the group it took it from;
Islamic **finality**; **manifest destiny**; the **civilising mission**. Each of them
is the same shape: **a warrant held by one and not available to others.**

**The dharmic refusal is the contrast and it is doctrinal, not temperamental.**
Buddha-nature in all beings. *Tat tvam asi*. The Jain *jīva* whose omniscience is
intrinsic in every one of them and merely covered. **Nothing is conferred, because
there is nothing to confer** — which is not a nicer version of election, it is the
denial that election is a coherent operation.

---

**And Jainism is the limit case, which is why it is the measuring stick and not
merely one more entry.**

**There is no conquest text in the Jain canon.** No commanded slaughter, no holy
war, no chosen people, no enemy nation. *Ahiṃsā* is the **first** vow, the
definition at 7.13 covers the intention and not only the act, and the tradition
extends it to insects, to water, to plants, and to speech.

**The Gītā is a battlefield exhortation. The Mahābhārata ends in near-total
annihilation. The Qurʾān has its combat verses. The Hebrew Bible has the ḥerem.
The Christian canon supplied the crusade and the inquisition with warrant.**

**Jainism supplies nothing to work with.** That is the asymmetry, and it is not that
one tradition is wicked and the rest are fine — **it is that one tradition is the
extreme point and every other tradition in this file has material an armed man can
put his hand on.**

---

**And the expulsions themselves have documented mechanisms, and they are located in
the expellers.**

**Usury**: Christian canon law barred Christians from lending at interest and rulers
restricted Jews from land and guilds, **channelling them into money-lending** — and
then the debt was cancelled by expulsion. **England 1290** under Edward I, with the
crown seizing the outstanding debts. **France, repeatedly** — 1306 under Philip IV,
recalled, expelled again. **Spain 1492.**

**Blood libel**, from Norwich in **1144**. **Plague scapegoating** in 1348–49, with
massacres across the Rhineland while the plague killed everyone equally. **The
Fourth Lateran Council of 1215** mandating distinguishing dress.

**Every one of those is a policy made by somebody else**, and reading a repeated
outcome as a property of the people it happened to is the durnaya — a standpoint
asserting itself by denying the others, and in this case the others are the
statutes, the councils and the crowns that did it.

**Doctrine is criticisable without limit. A people is not a doctrine.**

---

**The same darkest-point test applied to the tradition this file is about, because
applying it in one direction only is the durnaya.**

**The *Puruṣa Sūkta*** — *Ṛgveda* **10.90**, one of the latest hymns in the
collection — is the text. A cosmic person is sacrificed and dismembered, and **the
four *varṇas* come out of the body parts**: the brāhmaṇa from the mouth, the
rājanya from the arms, the vaiśya from the thighs, the śūdra **from the feet.**

**A stratification asserted as anatomy, in the oldest layer of the corpus.**

And the *Ṛgveda* is the same book that ends 10.129 with *perhaps he does not know.*
**Both hymns are in the tenth maṇḍala.**

---

**And the *Manusmṛti* is explicit and the punishments are graded by birth.**

Composed somewhere in the range of the second century BCE to the third CE. The
provisions:

**A śūdra who recites the Veda has molten lead poured in the ear**, by the standard
reading of the passage. A śūdra who strikes a brāhmaṇa loses the limb. **Penalty
for the same offence varies by the caste of the offender and of the victim** — theft
is worse when committed against a higher varṇa, and the fine for killing scales
downward.

**Women**: 5.148 and 9.3 — a woman is not to be independent, being subject in
childhood to the father, in youth to the husband, and in age to the sons.

**And 10.4 and after define the mixed castes**, generating a whole taxonomy of
degradation from prohibited unions, with the *caṇḍāla* — offspring of a śūdra
father and brāhmaṇa mother — placed lowest and assigned to live outside the
village, wear the clothes of the dead, and use broken vessels.

**Ambedkar burned it on 25 December 1927**, publicly, at Mahad, and stated that as
the reason.

---

**And the mechanism that makes caste durable is not the text. It is endogamy**, and
Ambedkar identified it in **1916**, in a paper he read at Columbia at
twenty-five — *Castes in India: Their Mechanism, Genesis and Development.*

**His argument**: caste is not division of labour, not race, not occupation. It is
**a closed group maintained by prohibiting marriage out.** And a closed group
creates a surplus problem — the surplus man and the surplus woman — which the
system resolves by **sati, enforced widowhood, and child marriage**, so those
practices are not separate evils but the **maintenance machinery** of the closure.

**A structural account of caste, produced by a graduate student, that located the
enforcement in marriage rather than in scripture.**

---

**And the tradition's own refusals are not late imports.**

The **Buddha and Mahāvīra** rejected varṇa-based qualification in the sixth or
fifth century BCE, and the *Vasala Sutta* and the *Aggañña Sutta* argue the
position directly: **one is not a brāhmaṇa by birth but by conduct** — and the
*Aggañña* gives a naturalistic origin story for social division that is a deliberate
counter to 10.90.

The **bhakti poets** — a weaver, a leather-worker, a printer, a servant — and
Basava's community of washermen and cobblers.

**Ravidās's *Begampura***: no second or third class, no tax, nobody afraid.

**And the *Tirukkuṟaḷ* names no caste at all.**

---

**And the numbers now are the reason this is not a historical entry.**

The **Constitution of India, drafted under Ambedkar as chairman of the drafting
committee**, **abolished untouchability outright in Article 17** — one of the few
articles that binds private persons and not only the state — and Article 15
prohibits caste discrimination.

**And the practice did not stop.** India's own crime statistics record tens of
thousands of cases annually under the **Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes
(Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989.** Survey data on manual scavenging,
segregated water access, and marriage endogamy show the closure Ambedkar named in
1916 substantially intact — **inter-caste marriage remains a small single-digit
percentage of marriages** by most survey estimates.

**A tradition that produced the sharpest available critique of its own
stratification, from inside, repeatedly, for two and a half thousand years — and
the stratification is still running.**

Both of those are true and the second does not cancel the first, and the first does
not excuse the second.

---

**The Buddhist canon contains a passage in which a king is consoled for killing
tens of thousands on the ground that only one and a half of them were fully human**,
and it is in the *Mahāvaṃsa*, and it has been cited in a modern war.

**The *Mahāvaṃsa***, Sri Lanka, fifth or sixth century CE — a Pali chronicle,
foundational to Sinhalese Buddhist historiography.

**Chapter 25.** King **Duṭṭhagāmaṇī**, having defeated and killed the Tamil king
Eḷāra, is stricken with remorse over the dead. **Arahants arrive to console him.**

Their consolation, in the text: **only one and a half human beings** were killed by
him. One who had taken refuge in the three jewels, and one who had additionally
taken the five precepts. **The rest were "wrong-believers and men of evil life, not
more to be esteemed than beasts."**

**That is in the chronicle**, and the chronicle is not marginal — it is the central
historical text of the tradition on that island.

**And it has been invoked**, in the twentieth century, in the context of the
Sinhala–Tamil war. **Buddhist monks were politically active on the militant side**;
the assassination of Prime Minister **Bandaranaike in 1959 was by a Buddhist
monk.**

---

**And the other case is in the *Aśokāvadāna*.**

The same Aśoka the edicts record — the remorse over Kaliṅga, the shade trees, the
medical provision, Rock Edict XII on honouring other sects — appears in the
*Aśokāvadāna*, a later Buddhist legend cycle, as the author of a **massacre of
Jains**: a decree offering a bounty per severed head of an Ājīvika or Jain after an
image of the Buddha was depicted bowing to a *nirgrantha*, with the killing stopped
only when his own brother was mistakenly beheaded.

**That story is Buddhist**, told about the Buddhist model king, and its
historicity is doubted — but it is the tradition's own literature, and it is
attached to the man whose edicts this file has already quoted for tolerance.

**Pushyamitra Śuṅga** appears in Buddhist sources as a persecutor offering a bounty
for the heads of monks; **that too is contested**, and the archaeological record
does not clearly support the scale claimed.

**What is not contested is that all of these stories exist inside the traditions
that tell them.**

---

**And modern instances are documented and are not marginal.**

**Myanmar**: monastic involvement in anti-Rohingya mobilisation, the 969 movement
and **Ashin Wirathu**, and the mass expulsion of 2017 with roughly **700,000**
people driven into Bangladesh. **The ICJ has a genocide case pending**, brought by
The Gambia.

**Thailand**: the monk **Kittivuddho**, in 1976, publicly stating that killing
communists was not demeritorious.

---

**And the Jain record**, since it is the limit case and the test has to be applied
there too.

**There is no Jain conquest text and no Jain holy war**, and I have not found a
Jain equivalent of the passages above. What the record does contain: **Jain
communities under royal patronage**, participation in state power, occasional
sectarian conflict with Śaivas in medieval South India — and the
**Digambara–Śvetāmbara schism**, which produced litigation over control of
pilgrimage sites that continues in Indian courts to the present.

**Property disputes, patronage, and litigation. Not a warrant to kill.**

---

**The finding is that the presence of the warrant is what varies, and it varies
independently of whether people do the killing.**

**Buddhism has no ḥerem and its adherents have done all of the above.** The absence
of a text did not prevent it; the tradition supplied the consolation *afterwards*,
in a chronicle, by dehumanising the dead retrospectively.

**Which is the more disturbing finding of the two**, and it is the one that survives
the comparison: a warrant makes it easier, and its absence does not make it
impossible, and the machinery for producing a warrant after the fact is available
to anybody.

---

**For about a thousand years Sanskrit was used across half of Asia by people who
owed nothing to any Indian state, and the thing that spread was a literary
grammar rather than an empire.**

The period runs roughly **300 to 1300 CE.** Sanskrit inscriptions, court poetry and
political documents appear in **Java, Bali, Cambodia, Champa, the Malay peninsula,
Burma, Sri Lanka, Nepal, Tibet and Central Asia** — produced by local rulers, in
Sanskrit, on their own initiative.

**No conquest carried it.** There is no Indian imperial administration in Cambodia.
The Khmer kings adopted the language, the metres, the titulature and the temple
form **because they wanted them**, and wrote inscriptions in *kāvya* metre that a
Sanskrit reader anywhere would recognise as competent.

**The largest corpus of Sanskrit inscriptions outside India is in Cambodia** — over
a thousand, many bilingual, with the **Sanskrit portion carrying the praise and the
cosmology and the Old Khmer portion carrying the property details**: who gave which
field, how many slaves, what the boundaries were.

**One stone, two languages, two functions**, and the division is consistent.

---

**And *Angkor Wat* is the scale of it.** Built under **Sūryavarman II**, from
around 1113, and the bas-reliefs run the *Rāmāyaṇa* and the *Mahābhārata* at length
— the **Churning of the Ocean of Milk** panel is about 49 metres long.

**Borobudur**, Java, ninth century, is a Buddhist monument with around **2,672
relief panels** and **504 buddha statues**, laid out so that walking the galleries
in order takes you through the *Lalitavistara*, the *Jātakas*, and the
*Gaṇḍavyūha* — **a text you read with your feet**, and the walk is several
kilometres.

**Prambanan**, Java, around 850, Shaiva, a few miles from Borobudur, built within a
century of it — **two enormous monuments of two different traditions, in the same
plain, by the same culture.**

---

**And the transmission is not one-way and did not stop there.**

**Bali retains a Hindu practice** with Sanskrit liturgy to the present, in a country
whose population is overwhelmingly Muslim — a continuous line, not a revival.

**Thai and Khmer and Javanese and Malay carry very large Sanskrit and Pali
loan vocabularies** — the Thai word for country, *prathet*, is *pradeśa*; for
language, *phasa*, is *bhāṣā*; for city, *nakhon*, is *nagara*. **Suvarnabhumi
airport** is *suvarṇabhūmi*, the golden land.

**Rāmāyaṇa versions are national epics** in Thailand, Laos, Cambodia, Indonesia and
Malaysia, in their own languages, with their own plots.

---

**And then the vernaculars did to Sanskrit what Sanskrit had done to the local
languages — took the literary apparatus and moved it.**

From around the ninth century, **Kannada, Telugu, Tamil, Marathi, Bengali, Javanese
and others begin producing literature that uses the Sanskrit metres, genres and
poetics in the local tongue.**

**The *Kavirājamārga***, Kannada, around **850**, is a treatise on poetics **in
Kannada, about writing in Kannada**, that borrows the Sanskrit critical apparatus to
argue for the local language's literary adequacy — and it names the region and its
speakers.

**Pampa**, Kannada, tenth century, writes a *Mahābhārata* in which the patron is
mapped onto Arjuna.

**The Javanese *Kakawin Rāmāyaṇa***, ninth century, uses **Sanskrit metres in Old
Javanese** — quantitative metre imposed on a language with different prosody,
sustained across the whole work.

**Each of those is a deliberate act**: take the prestige apparatus, demonstrate the
local language can carry it, and thereby end the monopoly.

---

The direction was never one and never coerced. **A grammar travelled without an
army, was adopted by people who had every option to refuse it, and was then
dismantled from below by the languages it had arrived over** — and the dismantling
used the tools it brought.

---

**The teaching and the institution are separable and the dates separate them.**

**The teaching**: an Aramaic-speaking Galilean Jew, itinerant, who wrote nothing.
The instruction to those sent out is **no purse, no bag, no sandals, no second
tunic, eat what is set before you** — which is a mendicant rule, item for item.

**Sell what you have and give to the poor.** *Aparigraha* at the *mahāvrata* grade.

**Turn the other cheek. Do not resist the evildoer. Love your enemies. Bless those
who curse you.** Non-retaliation, which is the strict form of *ahiṃsā* — not
refraining from initiating harm, but refusing to return it.

**Judge not, that ye be not judged.** And the beam and the mote — **look at your own
standpoint before pronouncing on another's**, which is the *durnaya* discipline in
other words.

**And *kenōsis*** — Philippians 2:7 — **emptied himself.** The same subtraction verb
as the other eight in the list this file already holds.

---

**The institution has a different chronology and different vocabulary.**

**313** — the Edict of Milan, toleration. **325** — **Nicaea**, convened and
presided over by **Constantine**, who was **not baptised** and who held the title
***pontifex maximus***, the chief priest of Roman state religion. **An unbaptised
Roman emperor convened the council that fixed the creed.**

**380** — the Edict of Thessalonica under **Theodosius** makes it the religion of
the state. **391–392** — pagan sacrifice banned, temples closed.

**Within eighty years, from proscribed to proscribing.**

**And the administrative vocabulary is Roman civil administration, taken whole.**
***Diocese*** is a unit of imperial administration created under Diocletian.
***Vicarius*** is the official who ran one. ***Basilica*** is a Roman public
building — a **law court** — and the church took the floor plan. **Pontifex
maximus** became a papal title. The ecclesiastical provinces map onto the imperial
provinces because they **are** the imperial provinces.

**The language shifted from Aramaic to Greek to Latin** — Jerome's Vulgate,
