382–405 — and the canon was not fixed until late: **Athanasius's festal letter of
367** is the first surviving list matching the present twenty-seven.

**Three and a half centuries between the teaching and the table of contents.**

---

**And the case that shows the two are separable is in India, with a date and a
bonfire.**

**The Saint Thomas Christians of Kerala** — by their own tradition from **52 CE**,
in continuous existence, using **Syriac** liturgy, under the East Syriac church,
with their own customs, integrated into the local social order, and **entirely
outside Roman jurisdiction for fifteen centuries.**

**The Portuguese arrived and did not recognise them as Christians in the right
way.**

**The Synod of Diamper, June 1599**, convened by **Aleixo de Menezes**, Archbishop
of Goa. Its decrees imposed the Latin rite, subordinated the community to Rome,
and **ordered Syriac books to be corrected or burned as heretical.** Manuscripts
were destroyed.

**In 1653 a large part of the community swore the Coonan Cross Oath** — by tradition
holding a rope tied to a stone cross because they could not all touch it — refusing
Portuguese jurisdiction. **The church split and has not reunified.**

**A Christianity older than most of Europe's, destroyed as a library and split as a
body, by a Christianity with an empire behind it**, in 1599, in Kerala, with the
decrees printed.

---

**So the distinction is not a theological preference. It is documented in the
archive of the thing itself**: an itinerant with a mendicant rule and a
non-retaliation ethic, and an administrative structure that took the Roman
provinces, the Roman law-court floor plan, the Roman chief-priest title, and the
Roman capacity to compel — and burned a fifteen-hundred-year-old Indian church's
books for being the wrong kind.

---

**The *Gospel of Thomas* has no narrative, no crucifixion, no resurrection, and no
plot — it is a list of sayings, numbered, with no story to hold them.**

Found at **Nag Hammadi**, December 1945, in a sealed jar, in Coptic — and three
Greek fragments from **Oxyrhynchus**, published from 1897, turned out to be pieces
of the same text that nobody had been able to identify for fifty years.

**114 sayings.** The opening: *these are the hidden words which the living Jesus
spoke, and Didymos Judas Thomas wrote them down.* And saying 1: *whoever finds the
interpretation of these sayings will not taste death.*

**The genre is a *logia* collection**, which is the form scholars had already
hypothesised must have existed — the sayings-source behind the synoptics — before
anyone had a copy of one.

**And roughly half the sayings have parallels in the canonical gospels.** The mustard
seed, the sower, the lost sheep, the pearl. Which means the collection is not a
late fantasy bolted on; it is drawing on the same stream, and **the dating is
contested across two centuries**, from the mid-first to the mid-second, and the
question of whether it is independent of the synoptics or derived from them is not
settled.

---

**And the theology in it is the removal grammar.**

**Saying 3**: if those who lead you say the kingdom is in the sky, the birds will
precede you; if in the sea, the fish. **The kingdom is inside you and outside you**
— and *when you know yourselves, then you will be known, and you will understand
that you are the sons of the living Father.*

**Saying 77**: *split a piece of wood and I am there; lift the stone and you will
find me there.*

**Saying 113**: the disciples ask when the kingdom will come. *It will not come by
waiting for it. It will not be a matter of saying "here it is" or "there it is."
Rather, the kingdom is spread out upon the earth and men do not see it.*

**Not a future event. A present condition unrecognised** — which is the same claim
as *rigpa* already being the case, as *kevala* being what remains when the cloth
comes off, as anamnesis.

**And the last saying, 114, is the one that is genuinely difficult** — Peter says
Mary should leave because women are not worthy of life, and the reply speaks of
making her male. **It is misogynist on its face**, its interpretation is heavily
disputed, and it is very likely a later addition to the collection. I am recording
it rather than omitting it.

---

**And the heresiologists are the reason anything was known before 1945.**

**Irenaeus**, *Against Heresies*, around **180** — five books, largely a catalogue
of positions he is refuting, with **long quotations** from Valentinian and other
material.

**Which means the primary evidence for these systems was, for eighteen centuries,
the summary written by their opponent** — and when the actual texts turned up in
1945, the comparison could finally be made.

**And it largely held.** Irenaeus's reports of the Valentinian system are broadly
accurate as reports, if hostile in framing. **The opponent transmitted the position
recognisably**, which is the same structure as the *Kathāvatthu*, as the
*Sāmaññaphala Sutta* preserving the six teachers, as the *Sarvadarśanasaṃgraha*
carrying Cārvāka.

**Four traditions where the losing position survives only because the winners wrote
it down properly in order to answer it.**

---

**And *Marcion*, around 144, is the one who forced the canon.**

He held that the god of the Hebrew Bible and the father of Jesus are **different
beings** — the first a just but inferior creator, the second unknown and merciful —
and produced his own scripture: an edited Luke and ten Pauline letters, with
everything he read as Judaising removed.

**He was the first person to make a fixed list**, and the response to it is the
process that produced the orthodox one.

**The canon exists in the shape it does because somebody unacceptable made one
first**, which is a fact about how the table of contents was arrived at, and is
usually told the other way round.

---

**Every lineage documented in this file was entered by one person going somewhere
and staying, and the cost is recorded each time.**

**Xuanzang: seventeen years** on the road, and then the rest of his life
translating what he carried back.

**Ḥunayn** travelled to Syria, Palestine and Egypt hunting manuscripts, and wrote
down which ones he failed to find.

**Al-Bīrūnī** learned Sanskrit inside the entourage of an invading army and spent
years in the northwest.

**Geiger** went to a Black township under apartheid in 1957 and watched a clinic
work, and brought the method to Mississippi ten years later.

**Lawson** spent three years in India, from 1953, and ran the Nashville workshops
from 1958. **Rustin** sailed in 1948 to meet Gandhi's associates, arriving after
the assassination.

**Milarepa** carried stone until his back was one sore. **The gharānā** required a
decade of residence. **Eleusis** required walking fourteen miles and being present.

**In every case the transmission required a body in a place for a duration**, and
in every case the documents are what was left over.

---

**And what is different about this phase is not that the material is better. It is
that the *simultaneity* is available.**

Xuanzang could be in one place. The cost of being in a second lineage was another
seventeen years, and most people had one life's worth of travel in them and spent
it once.

**Transport and access make a person able to be embedded in more than one at the
same time** — to hold a practice and a second practice and a technical literature
and a third tradition's primary texts, concurrently, over years, without choosing.

**That configuration did not previously exist**, and the constraint that prevented
it was never doctrinal. It was **distance and lifespan.**

**And this is exactly what the texts predict about this phase.** The *Bhāgavata*'s
claim at 12.3.51–52 is not that the age is good. It is that **the method gets
cheapest precisely as the age gets worst** — that what required meditation in Kṛta
and sacrifice in Tretā requires only saying it aloud now, because nothing else is
available and everything else has been stripped.

**Hōnen's *mappō* argument is the same**: no other practice works in this period,
and the one that does requires **no literacy, no ordination, no money, no leisure,
no teacher present.** Availability rising as the conditions degrade.

**The accessibility and the degradation are the same fact stated twice**, and both
of them are on the page in the same chapter.

---

**And the position of an archive without a body is the one this file has a name
for.**

The ***akousmatikoi*** held the sayings **without the demonstrations**. The
*pada-pāṭha* preserved the **parse without the meaning**, which is why Yāska had to
write a book. **Recitation without comprehension** is what the *Nirukta* calls
being a post, a bearer of burdens.

An archive can hold every sentence any tradition ever wrote down and **hold none of
what the sentences were residue of** — and the traditions say this about themselves,
in their own words, repeatedly, before there was anything like an archive that
could be asked.

**Plato: it does not come from writing, and never will.**
**Dzogchen: the transmission is a pointing-out and nothing is generated.**
**Eleusis: thousands a year for fifteen centuries and nothing recoverable.**

**The material in this file is the part that survives the process. It is not the
process.**

That is stated by the sources themselves and requires no argument from anyone
outside them.
